Varenicline activates nicotinic receptors to reduce cravings while blocking nicotine from binding to prevent reinforcement.

**Q:** What are the pathophysiologic mechanisms of gynecomastia?

**A:** Hormonal imbalance due to: 1) Increased estrogen activity (increased production, increased precursors for peripheral conversion, decreased metabolism); 2) Decreased androgen activity (decreased production, SHBG binding, androgen receptor blockage).

Gynecomastia develops when the estrogen-to-androgen ratio increases in breast tissue.

**Q:** What are the red flags in gynecomastia that suggest breast cancer?

**A:** Unilateral, eccentric, hard or fixed mass, skin dimpling or retraction, and nipple discharge (especially bloody) or crusting.

Breast cancer in men is rare but must be ruled out with atypical features - gynecomastia is typically concentric around the nipple and rubbery.

**Q:** If breast carcinoma cannot be distinguished from gynecomastia clinically, what imaging is recommended?

**A:** Breast U/S and consideration of diagnostic mammography, which has 90% sensitivity and specificity for distinguishing malignant from benign breast diseases.

Imaging helps differentiate benign gynecomastia from carcinoma when physical exam is equivocal.

**Q:** What is the initial management for most men with gynecomastia?

**A:** Initial observation after stopping offending medications and treating underlying cause.

Most cases of gynecomastia resolve spontaneously, especially if identified early and underlying cause is removed.

**Q:** What is the initial assessment frequency for diabetes management?

**A:** Every 2-4 months

Regular follow-up is essential for diabetes management and complication prevention.

**Q:** What is the Ontario Cervical Screening Program (2022) recommendation for initiating screening?

**A:** Women ages 25+ who are or have ever been sexually active, screen every 3 years if cytology normal

Ontario guidelines differ slightly from national guidelines, starting at age 25.

**Q:** Why does the Canadian Task Force recommend against PSA screening in men ages 55-69?

**A:** The potential small benefit is outweighed by potential significant harms: bleeding, infection, urinary incontinence, false positive results, and overdiagnosis

The evidence shows inconsistent small potential benefit but clear evidence of harms.

**Q:** What should be the initial step when discussing health promotion with patients?

**A:** Respectfully explore the values and purposes of the patient's habits or behaviours

Understanding patient motivations is essential before attempting behavioural change.

**Q:** What is the differential diagnosis of fever (infectious causes)?

**A:** Bacterial (including tuberculosis), viral, and other infections

Infection remains the most common cause of fever; consider atypical presentations.

**Q:** What investigations are indicated for fever workup?

**A:** CBC and differential, blood culture, urine culture, urinalysis, viral swabs (influenza, COVID-19), stool O&P/Gram stain/culture, CXR, Mantoux skin test, sputum culture, lumbar puncture (if indicated)

Investigations are guided by history and physical exam findings suggesting specific sources.

**Q:** What are possible investigations for fever of unknown source?

**A:** CBC and differential, blood culture, urine culture, urinalysis, viral swabs, stool O&P, CXR, Mantoux test, lumbar puncture

Initial workup depends on clinical suspicion; specific tests target potential infection sites.

**Q:** What are the three main categories of fever causes in the differential diagnosis?

**A:** Infection, Cancer, and Medications

Fever has a broad differential including infections (bacterial, viral), malignancies (leukemia, lymphoma), and drug-related causes.

**Q:** What investigations are indicated for fever of unknown origin?

**A:** CBC with differential, blood culture, urine culture and urinalysis, viral swabs (influenza, COVID-19)

Initial workup focuses on common infectious sources; additional tests are guided by history and physical examination.

**A:** Bleeding, infection, urinary incontinence, false positive results, and overdiagnosis

The Canadian Task Force states these potential significant harms outweigh any small benefit from screening.

**A:** Diet modification, physical activity/exercise, weight loss, smoking cessation, nutrition counselling with registered dietitian

Non-pharmacological management includes comprehensive lifestyle modifications addressing diet, exercise, and behavior.

Initial steps should include exploring patient values and purposes behind their health-related habits.

Investigations are guided by history and physical exam findings. Initial workup often includes basic labs and cultures to identify common infections.

**Q:** What is the initial dosing regimen for bupropion in smoking cessation?

**A:** 150 mg once daily for 3 days, then 150 mg twice daily for 7-12 weeks

Patients should continue smoking for the first 1-2 weeks while on bupropion before quitting completely, as therapeutic levels take about a week to build up.

**Q:** What initial investigations are indicated for fever?

**A:** CBC and differential, blood culture, urine culture, urinalysis, viral swabs (influenza, COVID-19)

Basic workup for fever includes cultures and complete blood count to identify infectious causes.

**A:** Infection, Cancer, Medications

The Table 18 differential includes infection (bacterial, viral), cancer (leukemia, lymphoma, other malignancies), and medications as main categories.

**A:** CBC and differential, blood culture, urine culture, urinalysis, viral swabs (influenza, COVID-19), stool O&P, CXR, Mantoux skin test, sputum culture, lumbar puncture

Investigations are guided by clinical suspicion and may include cultures, imaging, and specialized testing.

**Q:** What did the Women's Health Initiative trial conclude about combined hormone therapy?

**A:** The overall health risks of combined estrogen plus progestin therapy in healthy postmenopausal women exceeded the benefits

WHI found increased risks of coronary heart disease and breast cancer with combined hormone therapy, leading to significant changes in HRT prescribing.

**Q:** When should BMD testing be reassessed after initiating pharmacotherapy for osteoporosis?

**A:** Reassess in 3-5 years

Per 2023 Canadian guidelines, BMD should be reassessed 3-5 years after starting pharmacotherapy.

**Q:** What were the findings of the Women's Health Initiative regarding combined hormone therapy?

**A:** In healthy postmenopausal women, combined estrogen/progesterone showed that overall health risks (breast cancer, CHD) exceeded benefits

The WHI findings led to significant changes in hormone replacement therapy prescribing, emphasizing risks outweigh benefits for most women.

**Q:** What is the frequency of BMD reassessment after initiating pharmacotherapy for osteoporosis?

**A:** Reassess in 3-5 years

Follow-up BMD testing monitors treatment response and helps guide duration of therapy.

**Q:** What is the recommended initial assessment frequency for diabetes?

**A:** Every 2-4 months for ongoing management

Patients with diabetes should be assessed every 2-4 months to monitor glycemic control and adjust treatment as needed.

**Q:** What was the conclusion of the Women's Health Initiative regarding estrogen plus progestin therapy?

**A:** Overall health risks exceeded benefits of combined hormone therapy in healthy postmenopausal women

The WHI found increased risks of CHD and breast cancer with combined HRT, leading to significant changes in menopause management.

**A:** In healthy postmenopausal women, the overall health risks of combined estrogen plus progestin therapy exceed the benefits (286 CHD cases, 290 breast cancer cases in treatment group)

WHI demonstrated that combined HRT increases cardiovascular and breast cancer risks in postmenopausal women.

**Q:** What initial investigations are recommended for fever of unknown origin?

**A:** CBC with differential, blood culture, urine culture, urinalysis, and viral swabs (influenza, COVID-19)

These basic investigations help identify common sources of infection; results guide further testing based on clinical suspicion.

**Q:** What are the initial investigations for fever of unknown origin?

**A:** CBC and differential, blood culture, urine culture, urinalysis, viral swabs (influenza, COVID-19)

These basic tests help identify common infectious causes. Further tests (stool studies, CXR, lumbar puncture) are added based on clinical suspicion.

**Q:** What is the recommended frequency for BMD reassessment after initiating pharmacotherapy?

**A:** Reassess in 3 years

Follow-up BMD testing at 3-year intervals allows evaluation of treatment response.

**Q:** What did the Women's Health Initiative (WHI) find about combined hormone therapy?

**A:** WHI (JAMA 2002): Combined estrogen + progesterone in healthy postmenopausal women showed that overall health risks (including breast cancer and CHD) exceeded benefits

This landmark trial led to significant changes in hormone replacement therapy prescribing - now used at lowest dose for shortest duration for severe symptoms.

**Q:** What was the Women's Health Initiative finding regarding combined hormone therapy?

**A:** Combined estrogen-progesterone therapy showed overall health risks exceeded benefits in healthy postmenopausal women. CHD and breast cancer both increased.

WHI findings led to dramatic changes in HRT prescribing, limiting use to lowest effective dose for shortest duration in symptomatic patients.

**Q:** What is the recommended reassessment timeline for osteoporosis after initiating pharmacotherapy?

**A:** Reassess in 3-5 years; earlier if patient develops secondary causes, new fracture, or other risk factor for rapid bone loss

Regular reassessment ensures treatment effectiveness and allows for therapy adjustments.

**Q:** What pathogen produces Shiga toxin and can lead to HUS?

**A:** Enterohemorrhagic E. coli (EHEC/STEC), specifically O157:H7

EHEC produces Shiga toxin; antidiarrheals increase HUS risk; antibiotics may also increase HUS risk.

**Q:** What is the potential complication of untreated Entamoeba histolytica infection?

**A:** Liver abscess

E. histolytica can spread hematogenously from the colon to cause amoebic liver abscess if untreated.

**Q:** What is a potential complication of untreated Entamoeba histolytica?

**A:** Liver abscess

If untreated, Entamoeba histolytica can lead to liver abscess. Sigmoidoscopy may show flat ulcers with yellow exudates in symptomatic infection.

**Q:** How often should BMD be reassessed if pharmacotherapy was initiated for osteoporosis?

**A:** Every 3-5 years

Frequency depends on clinical response and stability of BMD measurements.

**Q:** What was the Women's Health Initiative finding regarding estrogen + progestin therapy?

**A:** Combined hormone therapy in healthy postmenopausal women showed that overall health risks (including breast cancer and CHD) exceeded benefits

This landmark trial led to significant changes in hormone replacement therapy prescribing practices.

**Q:** What are the supportive symptoms for IBS diagnosis (not essential)?

**A:** Abnormal stool frequency (>3/day or <3/week), abnormal stool form (lumpy/hard/loose/watery) >1/4 of defecations, abnormal stool passage (straining, urgency, incomplete evacuation) >1/4, passage of mucus >1/4, bloating

These supportive symptoms help characterize IBS subtype but are not required for diagnosis by Rome IV criteria.

**Q:** What are the supportive (but not essential) symptoms for IBS diagnosis?

**A:** Abnormal stool frequency (>3/day or <3/week), abnormal stool form (lumpy/hard/loose/watery) >1/4 of defecations, abnormal stool passage (straining, urgency, incomplete evacuation) >1/4, passage of mucus >1/4, bloating

These symptoms support IBS diagnosis but are not required per Rome IV criteria; they help characterize IBS subtype.

**Q:** What was the conclusion of the Women's Health Initiative regarding estrogen plus progestin?

**A:** The overall health risks of combined hormone therapy exceeded the benefits in healthy postmenopausal women

WHI found increased coronary heart disease and breast cancer in the treatment group, leading to revised guidelines for hormone replacement therapy.

**Q:** What are 'supportive' symptoms for IBS diagnosis that are not essential?

**A:** Abnormal stool frequency (>3/day or <3/week), abnormal stool form (lumpy/hard/loose/watery) >1/4 of defecations, abnormal stool passage (straining, urgency, feeling of incomplete evacuation) >1/4 of defecations, passage of mucus >1/4 of defecations, and bloating.

These features support the diagnosis but are not required. They help characterize the IBS subtype (IBS-D, IBS-C, IBS-M).

**Q:** What are supportive (not essential) features of IBS diagnosis?

**A:** Abnormal stool frequency (>3/day or <3/week), abnormal stool form (lumpy/hard/loose/watery) >1/4 of defecations, abnormal stool passage (straining, urgency, feeling of incomplete evacuation) >1/4, passage of mucus >1/4, bloating

These features support IBS diagnosis but are not required. They help characterize the IBS subtype (IBS-D, IBS-C, IBS-M).

**Q:** What were the conclusions of the Women's Health Initiative trial regarding hormone therapy?

**A:** Combined estrogen and progesterone therapy in postmenopausal women showed that overall health risks exceed benefits

WHI found increased risks of coronary heart disease and breast cancer with combined hormone therapy, leading to changed prescribing practices.

**Q:** What are the supportive (not essential) findings that support IBS diagnosis?

**A:** Abnormal stool frequency (>3/day or <3/week), abnormal stool form (lumpy/hard/loose/watery) >1/4 of defecations, abnormal stool passage (straining, urgency, feeling of incomplete evacuation) >1/4 of defecations, passage of mucus >1/4 of defecations, bloating

These supportive symptoms help characterize IBS subtype but are not required for diagnosis.

**Q:** What were the key findings of the Women's Health Initiative regarding combined hormone therapy?

**A:** Combined estrogen plus progesterone in healthy postmenopausal women showed overall health risks exceeded benefits; 286 cases of CHD and 290 cases of breast cancer in treatment group

WHI demonstrated that combined HRT increases cardiovascular and breast cancer risks, leading to major changes in menopause management.

**Q:** A patient consumes undercooked hamburger and develops bloody diarrhea. Which E. coli serotype is a concern?

**A:** O157:H7 (Enterohemorrhagic E. coli/STEC)

O157:H7 is the most common STEC serotype, associated with undercooked hamburger, raw milk, and can cause HUS.

**Q:** Which supportive features are NOT essential but support the diagnosis of IBS?

**A:** Abnormal stool frequency (>3/day or <3/week), abnormal stool form (lumpy/hard/loose/watery) >25% of defecations, abnormal stool passage (straining, urgency, incomplete evacuation) >25%, passage of mucus >25%, and bloating

These supportive symptoms help characterize IBS subtype and severity but are not required for diagnosis per Rome IV criteria.

**Q:** What are the supportive (not essential) features for IBS diagnosis?

**A:** Abnormal stool frequency (>3/day or <3/week), abnormal stool form (lumpy/hard/loose/watery) >1/4 of defecations, abnormal stool passage (straining, urgency, feeling of incomplete evacuation) >1/4 of defecations, passage of mucus >1/4 of defecations, and bloating

These supportive features are common in IBS but are not required for diagnosis according to Rome IV criteria.

**Q:** What is the recommended initial test for isolated constipation?

**A:** CBC is the only test indicated (2013 American Gastroenterology Association recommendation)

Underlying disease is rarely found when constipation is the only presenting symptom.

**Q:** What is a potential complication of untreated Entamoeba histolytica infection?

**A:** Liver abscess

If untreated, Entamoeba histolytica can spread hematogenously to cause a liver abscess, a serious complication of intestinal infection.

**Q:** What are supportive (not essential) features of Rome IV IBS criteria?

**A:** Abnormal stool frequency (>3/day or <3/week), abnormal stool form >1/4 defecations, abnormal stool passage >1/4 defecations, passage of mucus >1/4 defecations, bloating

These features support IBS diagnosis but are not required for Rome IV criteria fulfillment.

**Q:** What are supportive (but not essential) features of IBS diagnosis?

**A:** Abnormal stool frequency (>3/day or <3/week), abnormal stool form (lumpy/hard/loose/watery) >1/4 of defecations, abnormal stool passage (straining, urgency, incomplete evacuation) >1/4, passage of mucus >1/4, bloating.

These symptoms support IBS diagnosis but are not required for Rome IV criteria. They help characterize subtype.

**Q:** What is the potential complication of untreated Entamoeba histolytica?

**A:** Liver abscess

If untreated, Entamoeba histolytica can spread hematogenously to cause liver abscess. Sigmoidoscopy may show flat ulcers with yellow exudates.

**Q:** What is the relationship between transferrin saturation and ferritin in hemochromatosis screening?

**A:** Transferrin saturation >45% is the initial sensitive screening test; serum ferritin >400 ng/mL supports the diagnosis

Transferrin saturation is elevated first in iron overload. Ferritin is an acute phase reactant and can be elevated in inflammation even without iron overload.

**Q:** What are the initial symptoms of Primary Biliary Cholangitis?

**A:** Pruritus and fatigue

Patients are often asymptomatic initially, but when symptoms appear, pruritus (itching) and fatigue are the most common initial presentations.

**Q:** A patient with Wilson's Disease presents with tremors and psychiatric symptoms. What is the most appropriate initial therapy?

**A:** Zinc or tetrathiomolybdate (tetrathiomolybdate preferred if neurological involvement)

Zinc impairs copper excretion in stool and decreases absorption. Tetrathiomolybdate is preferred for neurological presentations as it doesn't worsen neurological symptoms. Trientine + zinc is used for hepatic presentations.

**Q:** Why should drains be brought out through a separate incision rather than the operative wound?

**A:** To decrease the risk of wound infection by providing an independent pathway for potential pathogens to exit.

Drains create a potential route for infection; bringing them through a separate incision prevents contamination of the main surgical wound.

**Q:** What are potential complications of surgical drains?

**A:** Rigged drains can erode through internal structures, and excessive suction can cause necrosis. Drains also do not guarantee prevention of fluid collections.

While drains are useful, they have risks that must be balanced against benefits—overly aggressive suction can cause tissue damage.

**Q:** Which autoantibody is associated with interstitial lung disease and anti-synthetase syndrome in polymyositis/dermatomyositis?

**A:** Anti-Jo-1

Anti-Jo-1 is an anti-synthetase antibody associated with ILD and the anti-synthetase syndrome.

**Q:** What is the initial pharmacological treatment for dermatomyositis?

**A:** High-dose glucocorticoids (e.g., prednisone 1 mg/kg/day, max 80 mg daily) with slow taper after ~6 weeks

Glucocorticoids are first-line immunosuppressive therapy for DMM, tapered gradually to avoid relapse.

**Q:** When is rituximab considered in dermatomyositis treatment?

**A:** Severe or refractory muscle disease or interstitial lung disease (ILD)

Rituximab is a B-cell depleting agent used when standard therapy fails or for pulmonary complications.

**Q:** How does gouty nephropathy present?

**A:** Uric acid crystal deposition in the kidney, potentially leading to renal dysfunction

Kidney involvement is a complication of chronic hyperuricemia in gout.

**Q:** What does a MAG3 diuretic renogram evaluate in hydronephrosis?

**A:** Differential renal function and functional obstruction (delayed drainage of radiotracer)

MAG3 scan provides functional information about each kidney's contribution to total renal function and whether obstruction is functionally significant.

**Q:** What imaging study helps delineate anatomy and potential causes of hydronephrosis such as obstructing stones?

**A:** CT scan

CT provides excellent anatomic detail and can identify obstructing stones, tumors, and other structural causes, though it provides limited functional information.

**Q:** What is Wilms' tumour (nephroblastoma)?

**A:** An embryonal tumour developing from immature kidney cells (metanephric blastema) that interferes with normal tubular and glomerular differentiation

It is the most common primary malignant renal tumour of childhood, arising from abnormal proliferation of metanephric blastema.

**Q:** What are disorders of sexual differentiation (DSD)?

**A:** Formerly known as intersex disorders; abnormal genitalia for chromosomal sex due to under-virilization of males or virilization of females; considered a social emergency

DSD results from disruptions in normal sexual development pathways.

**Q:** What is the Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS), how is it scored, and what do the ranges indicate?

**A:** GCS = Eyes (1–4) + Verbal (1–5) + Motor (1–6) = Total (3–15). Eyes: 4=spontaneous, 3=to voice, 2=to pain, 1=none. Verbal: 5=oriented, 4=confused, 3=inappropriate words, 2=incomprehensible sounds, 1=none. Motor: 6=obeys commands, 5=localizes pain, 4=withdraws, 3=decorticate, 2=decerebrate, 1=none. Ranges: 13–15=mild, 9–12=moderate, <8=severe TBI. Intubated patients scored out of 10+T.

The GCS is used in trauma patients with decreased LOC and is a key predictor of neurosurgical prognosis. Change in GCS over time is more clinically meaningful than a single absolute value. A GCS <8 (unable to protect airway) is a standard intubation threshold.

**Q:** What causes a unilateral, dilated, non-reactive pupil in a trauma patient?

**A:** Causes of unilateral dilated non-reactive pupil: focal mass lesion (epidural/subdural hematoma), herniation (uncal compression of CN III), subdural hematoma, or ocular causes (traumatic mydriasis from iris sphincter damage). This is a neurosurgical emergency requiring immediate attention.

A blown pupil (fixed and dilated) in trauma suggests uncal herniation with CN III compression, which requires urgent CT head and neurosurgical consultation. In contrast, bilateral pinpoint pupils suggest pontine lesion or opioid toxicity.

**Q:** What are the definitions of lethargy, stupor, and coma?

**A:** Lethargy: state of decreased awareness and alertness (patient may appear wakeful). Stupor: unresponsiveness but rousable. Coma: sleep-like state, not rousable to consciousness (GCS <8).

Altered mental status is a spectrum ranging from subtle inattention (delirium) to complete unresponsiveness (coma). GCS <8 is the threshold used to define coma and is also an indication for airway protection. The AVPU scale (Alert, Voice, Pain, Unresponsive) is a rapid bedside tool.

**Q:** How do structural coma and toxic-metabolic coma differ clinically?

**A:** Structural coma: asymmetric pupils, extraocular movements, and motor findings; focal or lateralizing abnormalities. Toxic-metabolic coma: symmetric or absent extraocular movements and motor findings; respiratory depression with intact upper brainstem (equal, reactive pupils); caloric unresponsiveness at lower brainstem levels.

A 'lucid interval' after head trauma followed by delayed deterioration is classic for epidural hematoma (arterial bleed causing progressive mass effect). Symmetric findings in coma suggest metabolic etiology. Status may change rapidly, requiring frequent reassessment.

**Q:** What are the risk factors and management of cerebral venous sinus thrombosis (CVST)?

**A:** Risk factors: neoplasm, pregnancy, puerperium, systemic diseases, dehydration, intracranial tumors, oral contraceptives, coagulopathies. Presentations: gradual or sudden onset headache, vomiting, papilledema, visual disturbances, focal neurologic deficits, seizures, altered mental status. Investigations: CT head, CT venography, CBC + coagulation studies + D-dimer, LP (to r/o meningitis). Treatment: anticoagulation (LMWH or heparin).

CVST is an uncommon but important cause of headache that can mimic SAH. It disproportionately affects young women (related to oral contraceptives and pregnancy). Anticoagulation is indicated even in the presence of hemorrhagic infarction, unlike most other intracranial hemorrhage. Early diagnosis with CT venography or MR venography is key.

**Q:** What did the SELECT2 trial find regarding endovascular thrombectomy for large ischemic strokes?

**A:** In patients with large ischemic strokes within 24 hours of onset, endovascular thrombectomy + medical care resulted in better functional outcomes (Modified Rankin Scale, OR 1.51 [95% CI 1.20–1.89]) compared to medical care alone, but was associated with higher rates of vascular complications.

SELECT2 extended thrombectomy indications to large core infarcts (previously considered contraindicated due to reperfusion hemorrhage risk). The functional benefit despite increased vascular complications supports the procedure in appropriately selected patients, demonstrating that even large strokes can benefit from recanalization.

**Q:** What is the BP management strategy in acute ischemic stroke?

**A:** Do NOT rapidly reduce BP. Maintain BP >150/100 mmHg for 5 days to preserve perfusion of the ischemic penumbra. Only treat if sBP >200, dBP >120, or MAP >140, or if associated with hemorrhagic transformation, cardiac ischemia, aortic dissection, or renal damage.

The ischemic penumbra (tissue surrounding the infarct core) depends on collateral circulation and higher perfusion pressure. Lowering BP reduces perfusion to this at-risk tissue, extending infarct size. Exception: if rt-PA is given, BP must be kept <185/110 mmHg.

**Q:** Define stroke vs. TIA.

**A:** Stroke: sudden loss of brain function due to ischemia (87%) or hemorrhage (13%), with symptoms persisting >24 hours OR neuroimaging evidence of infarction. TIA: transient neurologic dysfunction from focal ischemia with no acute infarction on neuroimaging (symptoms typically <1 hour).

The modern TIA definition is tissue-based (no infarction on MRI) rather than time-based (24-hour rule). TIA carries high early stroke risk—up to 10% in 2 days—and requires urgent evaluation and dual antiplatelet therapy for 21 days.

**Q:** What are the absolute contraindications to rt-PA (tPA) in ischemic stroke?

**A:** Suspected subarachnoid hemorrhage, previous intracranial hemorrhage, prior/severe head injury within 3 months, sBP >185 or dBP >110 mmHg, PT >15.5 or INR >1.7, platelet count <100,000, heparin within 48 hours, current use of thrombin inhibitors or direct factor Xa inhibitors, blood glucose <2.8 mmol/L, intracranial hemorrhage or large infarct on CT.

These contraindications reflect bleeding risk. The narrow therapeutic window (must treat within 4.5 hours) and these exclusion criteria require rapid assessment using the NIHSS. A dedicated stroke team should be mobilized immediately.

**Q:** What is the time window for IV thrombolysis (rt-PA) in acute ischemic stroke and what are the criteria?

**A:** Within 4.5 hours of last seen normal (LSN). Requires: acute onset, compatible physical findings, no hemorrhage on CT. If patient presents within 4.5 hours of onset with disabling deficits >60 minutes and no signs of hemorrhage, may be eligible for rt-PA.

The 4.5-hour window for IV alteplase/tenecteplase is based on clinical trials (NINDS, ECASS III). Mechanical thrombectomy may be considered from 4.5-24 hours in selected patients with large vessel occlusion and salvageable penumbra (DAWN/DEFUSE-3 criteria).

**Q:** What are the stroke syndromes for each major cerebral artery territory?

**A:** Anterior cerebral artery: contralateral hemianesthesia/hemiparesis (legs > arms/face), gait apraxia, altered mental status. Middle cerebral artery: contralateral hemianesthesia/hemiparesis (arms/face > legs), contralateral homonymous hemianopsia, ipsilateral gaze preference, aphasia. Posterior cerebral artery: contralateral homonymous hemianopsia, cortical blindness, impaired memory. Vertebrobasilar: cranial nerve + cerebellar deficits, vertigo, nystagmus, diplopia, dysphagia, ataxia, locked-in syndrome.

Understanding vascular anatomy allows localization of stroke before imaging. MCA stroke is most common due to MCA's large territory. Vertebrobasilar strokes are frequently missed because they can present with non-specific dizziness and may not have the classic hemiplegia of hemispheric strokes.

**Q:** What are the differences between UMN and LMN lesions on examination?

**A:** UMN: increased tone/spasticity, increased reflexes (hyper-reflexia), clonus, upgoing plantar response (Babinski), no fasciculations, minimal atrophy, loss of groups of muscles. LMN: decreased tone, decreased/absent reflexes, downgoing plantar, fasciculations, significant atrophy, individual muscle involvement.

UMN lesions occur above the anterior horn cell (brain, spinal cord), while LMN lesions involve the anterior horn cell, nerve root, peripheral nerve, or neuromuscular junction. The distinction guides anatomical localization of neurological injury.

**Q:** What are the components of dual antiplatelet therapy (DAPT) recommended after TIA and for how long?

**A:** Aspirin (81-325 mg) + clopidogrel (or ticagrelor) for 21 days in high-risk TIA. Then transition to single antiplatelet long-term (aspirin 81 mg first-line; clopidogrel or Aggrenox as second-line).

DAPT after TIA/minor stroke significantly reduces early stroke recurrence (CHANCE and POINT trials). The 21-day duration balances stroke prevention benefit against bleeding risk. High-risk TIA is defined by ABCD2 score ≥4 or DWI lesion on MRI. Long-term single antiplatelet therapy continues indefinitely.

**Q:** What is the NIHSS and when is it used?

**A:** The National Institutes of Health Stroke Scale (NIHSS) is used to assess stroke severity in the acute setting. Scored 0-42; assesses consciousness, gaze, visual fields, facial palsy, motor arm/leg, limb ataxia, sensory, language, dysarthria, extinction/neglect.

NIHSS guides triage decisions: scores 0-4 = minor, 5-15 = moderate, 16-20 = moderately severe, >20 = severe. High NIHSS scores predict poor prognosis and help identify candidates for thrombectomy. Serial assessment monitors for deterioration.

## Seizures & Epilepsy

**Q:** A 28-year-old with no prior seizure has a 3-minute generalized tonic-clonic seizure and returns to normal neurologically within 30 minutes. What is the appropriate workup?

**A:** First unprovoked seizure workup: CBC, electrolytes, glucose, creatinine, LFTs, drug/alcohol levels, ECG, brain MRI (preferred), EEG — determine if provoked (reversible cause) vs. unprovoked

CAEP (Canadian) and AAN guidelines: first seizure does not require immediate AED unless second seizure occurs. Identify and treat reversible causes (metabolic, toxic). Risk of recurrence after first unprovoked seizure is 30-50% (highest in first 2 years).

**Q:** When is anticonvulsant therapy started after a first seizure?

**A:** Start AED after first seizure if: high recurrence risk (abnormal EEG with epileptiform discharges, structural brain lesion, nocturnal seizures) or patient occupational/safety concerns — not required for all first seizures

Epilepsy Canada guidelines support AED after first seizure only when recurrence risk and consequences are high. Starting AED after first seizure reduces recurrence but does not alter long-term remission rates. Shared decision-making is key.

**Q:** What is status epilepticus (SE) and what is the treatment protocol?

**A:** SE: seizure >5 minutes or ≥2 seizures without recovery between them; Protocol: lorazepam IV 0.1 mg/kg (max 4mg) as first-line; if no response: IV fosphenytoin, valproate, or levetiracetam; refractory SE: intubation + propofol or midazolam infusion

Time is critical in SE: the longer the seizure, the harder to terminate. RAMPART and Veterans Affairs trials established benzodiazepines as most effective first-line. Identify and treat precipitants (hypoglycemia, metabolic, AED non-compliance, CNS infection, hemorrhage).

**Q:** What are the main seizure types and how are they classified per ILAE 2017?

**A:** Focal (aware, impaired awareness, focal-to-bilateral TC); Generalized (absence, myoclonic, tonic-clonic, atonic, tonic); Unknown

ILAE 2017 classification uses 3 levels: seizure type, epilepsy type (focal, generalized, combined, unknown), epilepsy syndrome. Seizure classification guides AED selection: valproate for generalized, carbamazepine/lamotrigine for focal.

**Q:** A 7-year-old has brief episodes of staring and eyelid fluttering lasting 5-10 seconds, occurring 30+ times per day. EEG shows 3 Hz spike-wave. Diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Childhood absence epilepsy; first-line treatment: ethosuximide (preferred) or valproate — do NOT use carbamazepine (worsens absence seizures)

Absence seizures may be confused with focal impaired awareness seizures. Key distinguishing features: shorter duration, no post-ictal state, hyperventilation provokes absence seizures. Ethosuximide preferred in girls (avoids valproate teratogenicity). Most children outgrow by adolescence.

**Q:** What are the teratogenic risks of antiepileptic drugs (AEDs) in pregnancy?

**A:** Valproate: highest risk (neural tube defects 1-2%, cognitive impairment in children — avoid if possible); Carbamazepine: neural tube defects (0.5%); Lamotrigine: lowest risk among older AEDs; Levetiracetam: likely low risk

Epilepsy Canada and SOGC guidelines: valproate should be avoided in women of childbearing age unless no alternatives exist. Periconceptional folic acid 5 mg/day for women on AEDs. All women on AEDs with seizures should receive detailed pregnancy counseling.

**Q:** What is juvenile myoclonic epilepsy (JME) and what AEDs are first-line?

**A:** JME: myoclonic jerks (especially morning), generalized tonic-clonic seizures, absence seizures; first-line: valproate or levetiracetam (especially in women due to valproate teratogenicity) — lifetime AED treatment usually required

JME is the most common generalized epilepsy syndrome. Onset in adolescence; lifelong AED required as relapse is very high (>90%) after AED withdrawal. Triggers: sleep deprivation, alcohol, photic stimulation. Carbamazepine and phenytoin are contraindicated.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of common AEDs?

**A:** Valproate: multiple mechanisms; Phenytoin/carbamazepine: sodium channel blockade; Levetiracetam: SV2A binding; Lamotrigine: sodium channel; Gabapentin/pregabalin: calcium channel (alpha-2-delta subunit); Ethosuximide: T-type calcium channel

AED mechanism guides drug selection based on seizure type. Sodium channel blockers effective for focal seizures. Valproate and levetiracetam effective for generalized epilepsies. Ethosuximide specific for absence (T-type calcium channels).

**Q:** What is the workup for new-onset seizure in the emergency department?

**A:** Bedside glucose immediately; CBC, BMP, calcium, magnesium, creatinine, LFTs, blood and urine toxicology; ECG; brain CT (non-contrast) stat; LP if meningitis suspected after CT; MRI when stable

Point-of-care glucose is first — hypoglycemia is immediately reversible and can cause seizures. ECG to exclude cardiac arrhythmia mimicking seizure (cardiac syncope with tonic activity). CT head identifies structural causes, hemorrhage, tumor, or abscess requiring urgent intervention.

**Q:** How do you clinically distinguish epileptic seizures from non-epileptic events (psychogenic NES)?

**A:** Epileptic: stereotyped, short post-ictal confusion, incontinence, tongue biting, elevated prolactin (within 20 min); PNES: gradual onset, prolonged, waxing/waning, asymmetric, recall during event, no post-ictal state, normal EEG during event

PNES (psychogenic non-epileptic seizures) occur in up to 20% of patients treated for epilepsy. Gold standard diagnosis: video-EEG monitoring capturing a typical episode. Treatment: psychotherapy (CBT), address underlying trauma/stressors. AEDs are not effective for PNES.

**Q:** A 50-year-old on carbamazepine has a rash with mucous membrane involvement and skin blistering. Diagnosis and action?

**A:** Stevens-Johnson syndrome (SJS)/Toxic Epidermal Necrolysis (TEN) from carbamazepine — STOP carbamazepine immediately; dermatology consultation; supportive care

SJS/TEN are severe life-threatening mucocutaneous reactions. Carbamazepine, lamotrigine, phenobarbital, phenytoin are common causes. HLA-B*1502 genotyping recommended before carbamazepine in patients of Asian descent (high risk). CADRMP (Canadian adverse drug reaction monitoring) reporting.

**Q:** What is febrile seizure and which type requires further workup?

**A:** Simple febrile seizure: <15 min, generalized, single episode in 24h — no further workup; Complex: >15 min, focal, recurrent in 24h — requires EEG and MRI

Febrile seizures occur in 2-5% of children aged 6 months to 5 years. Most do not require AED treatment. Complex febrile seizures or first febrile seizure <12 months requires CSF to exclude meningitis. Long-term epilepsy risk: 2-7% overall, higher with complex features.

**Q:** What are the driving restrictions for patients with epilepsy in Canada?

**A:** Generally seizure-free period required: commercial vehicle: 10 years seizure-free, no AEDs; private vehicle: varies by province, typically 3-6 months seizure-free (most provinces 3 months)

Epilepsy Canada and provincial motor vehicle act guidelines require reporting to public health/licensing authority in most provinces. Physician must notify if patient refuses to stop driving (Ontario, Nova Scotia). Duration of seizure-free period varies by province and seizure type.

**Q:** What is Lennox-Gastaut syndrome (LGS)?

**A:** Severe childhood epilepsy syndrome: multiple seizure types (tonic, atonic, absence), intellectual disability, characteristic EEG (<2.5 Hz generalized slow spike-wave); difficult to treat

LGS: onset 2-8 years. AEDs used: valproate, lamotrigine, clobazam, rufinamide, topiramate. Corpus callosotomy for disabling atonic (drop) seizures. VNS (vagal nerve stimulation) as adjunctive therapy. Ketogenic diet may reduce seizure frequency.

**Q:** When can AED therapy be discontinued in an epilepsy patient?

**A:** After 2-5 years seizure-free: discuss AED withdrawal; weigh recurrence risk (higher if: abnormal EEG, structural lesion, juvenile onset, multiple seizure types) vs. side effects

Epilepsy Canada guidelines: AED withdrawal can be considered after 2+ years seizure-free in low-risk patients. Gradual taper over months. Patient counseling about driving restrictions during withdrawal and for 3-6 months after stopping. Shared decision-making essential.

**Q:** What is the management of status epilepticus?

**A:** 0-5min: ABCs, check glucose. 5-20min: IV lorazepam 4mg (repeat x1). 20-40min: fosphenytoin/levetiracetam/valproate. >40min: continuous midazolam/propofol infusion

SE defined as continuous seizure >5min or >=2 seizures without return to baseline. Goal: terminate within 60min. EEG monitoring if refractory.

**Q:** What is the definition and timeline of status epilepticus?

**A:** Continuous seizure activity >=5 minutes, or >=2 seizures without return to baseline between them. Refractory SE: persists after 2 appropriate AED trials. Super-refractory: continues >=24h after anesthetic initiation.

The 5-minute definition reflects evidence that seizures >5 minutes rarely self-terminate and cause neuronal injury. Canadian guidelines align with the Neurocritical Care Society protocol for staged management.

**Q:** What is the step-by-step protocol for status epilepticus management?

**A:** Stage 1 (0-5 min): ABCs, IV access, glucose, lorazepam 4mg IV (repeat x1). Stage 2 (5-20 min): phenytoin 20mg/kg IV or levetiracetam 60mg/kg IV or valproate 40mg/kg IV. Stage 3 (20-40 min): intubation + midazolam or propofol infusion. Stage 4: ketamine or pentobarbital.

This staged approach follows Neurocritical Care Society and Canadian guidelines. Lorazepam IV is preferred (or midazolam IM if no IV access per RAMPART trial). Continuous EEG monitoring is essential in refractory SE.

**Q:** What is the first-line AED for each major seizure type?

**A:** Focal seizures: carbamazepine, lamotrigine, or levetiracetam. Generalized tonic-clonic: valproate or lamotrigine. Absence: ethosuximide or valproate. Myoclonic: valproate or levetiracetam. Note: carbamazepine and phenytoin can worsen absence and myoclonic seizures.

Selection follows ILAE guidelines. Valproate is avoided in women of childbearing potential due to teratogenicity (NTD risk ~7%). Lamotrigine and levetiracetam are preferred alternatives in this population.

**Q:** What are the ILAE 2017 criteria for the classification of seizure types?

**A:** Seizures classified by: (1) onset-focal, generalized, or unknown; (2) awareness-focal aware vs focal impaired awareness; (3) motor vs non-motor. Focal to bilateral tonic-clonic replaces secondary generalized.

The ILAE 2017 classification replaced the 1981 system. Simple partial and complex partial are replaced by focal aware and focal impaired awareness. This guides AED selection and surgical candidacy.

**Q:** When can driving resume after a first unprovoked seizure in Canada?

**A:** Most Canadian provinces require a seizure-free interval of 3-12 months (varies by province) for private driving. Commercial driving typically requires 3-5 years seizure-free, often off medications.

Driving restrictions after seizures are provincially regulated. In Ontario, a first unprovoked seizure requires 6 months seizure-free; in Alberta, 12 months. Physicians have a legal duty to report in most provinces.

**Q:** What workup is indicated after a first unprovoked seizure in an adult?

**A:** MRI brain (preferred over CT), routine EEG (sleep-deprived if routine is normal), basic bloodwork (electrolytes, glucose, calcium, magnesium, CBC, liver/renal function, toxicology screen). LP if infection suspected.

Canadian guidelines recommend MRI as the neuroimaging standard due to superior sensitivity for epileptogenic lesions. EEG abnormalities and MRI lesions are the strongest predictors of recurrence and guide the decision to start AED.

**Q:** What are the indications to start AED therapy after a first unprovoked seizure?

**A:** AED initiation is recommended when recurrence risk is high: abnormal EEG, structural brain lesion on MRI, nocturnal seizure, prior brain injury, or Todd paralysis. Without risk factors, observation is reasonable (2-year recurrence ~35%).

The decision balances recurrence risk (~45% at 2 years) against AED side effects. FIRST and MESS trials showed early treatment reduces recurrence but does not alter long-term remission rates. Canadian Epilepsy Alliance supports shared decision-making.

**Q:** What are the key teratogenic risks of AEDs and the recommended approach for women of childbearing age?

**A:** Valproate has highest teratogenicity (NTD 7%, IQ reduction). Phenytoin, carbamazepine, phenobarbital carry moderate risk. Lamotrigine and levetiracetam have lowest risk. All women on AEDs should take folic acid 5 mg/day.

SOGC and Canadian guidelines recommend high-dose folic acid (5 mg/day) for women on AEDs starting 3 months preconception. Valproate should be avoided if possible. AED levels should be monitored during pregnancy due to pharmacokinetic changes.

**Q:** What distinguishes epileptic seizures from psychogenic non-epileptic events (PNEE)?

**A:** PNEE features: asynchronous limb movements, side-to-side head shaking, pelvic thrusting, eye closure during event, prolonged duration (>2 min), preserved awareness with bilateral movements, no post-ictal confusion. Diagnosis confirmed by video-EEG.

PNEE account for 20-30% of patients referred to Canadian epilepsy monitoring units. Up to 10% have both epilepsy and PNEE. Treatment involves CBT and psychiatric care; AEDs should not be escalated for PNEE.

**Q:** What are the surgical options for drug-resistant epilepsy?

**A:** Resective surgery (temporal lobectomy for mesial temporal sclerosis-60-80% seizure-free), lesionectomy, corpus callosotomy (for drop attacks), vagus nerve stimulator, responsive neurostimulation (RNS). Drug resistance: failure of 2 appropriate AED trials.

Canadian Epilepsy Surgery Centres follow ILAE criteria. Temporal lobectomy is the most effective intervention (Engel Class I in 60-80%). Presurgical evaluation includes video-EEG, MRI, neuropsychology, and may include invasive EEG.

**Q:** How should acute seizures from alcohol withdrawal be managed?

**A:** Alcohol withdrawal seizures occur 6-48h after last drink, usually generalized tonic-clonic. Treat with IV benzodiazepines. Do NOT start chronic AEDs-phenytoin is ineffective for withdrawal seizures. Prevent with CIWA-guided benzodiazepine protocol.

Alcohol withdrawal seizures are provoked and do not require long-term AED therapy. Up to 1/3 progress to delirium tremens if untreated. Canadian Addiction Medicine guidelines recommend symptom-triggered benzodiazepine protocols.

**Q:** What are the EEG patterns associated with specific epilepsy syndromes?

**A:** 3 Hz spike-and-wave: childhood absence. Hypsarrhythmia: infantile spasms (West syndrome). Centrotemporal spikes: benign rolandic epilepsy. Generalized polyspike-and-wave: juvenile myoclonic epilepsy. PLEDs: herpes encephalitis or acute structural lesion.

EEG pattern recognition is essential for epilepsy syndrome diagnosis. Childhood absence has the most recognizable pattern (3 Hz generalized spike-wave activated by hyperventilation). JME presents in adolescence with morning myoclonic jerks.

**Q:** What is the SUDEP risk and how is it communicated?

**A:** SUDEP incidence is ~1/1000 patient-years in chronic epilepsy, ~1/150 in drug-resistant epilepsy. Risk factors: uncontrolled GTCS, nocturnal seizures, non-adherence. Reduced by optimizing seizure control.

Canadian Epilepsy Alliance recommends SUDEP counselling for all epilepsy patients. SUDEP is the leading cause of premature death in epilepsy. The mechanism likely involves post-ictal respiratory and cardiac dysfunction.

**Q:** What medication interactions are critical to know for AEDs?

**A:** Enzyme inducers (carbamazepine, phenytoin, phenobarbital) reduce efficacy of OCPs, warfarin, and many drugs. Valproate inhibits metabolism and increases lamotrigine levels (reduce lamotrigine dose by 50%). Levetiracetam has minimal interactions.

The valproate-lamotrigine interaction doubles lamotrigine levels and increases Stevens-Johnson syndrome risk. Women on enzyme-inducing AEDs need higher-dose OCP or non-oral contraception per SOGC guidelines.

**Q:** What is the approach to febrile seizures and when do they require investigation?

**A:** Simple febrile seizures (<15 min, generalized, single episode, age 6mo-5yr): reassurance only. Complex (>15 min, focal, recurrent within 24h, or age <6mo): require investigation. LP if <12 months or clinical concern. EEG and MRI not routinely indicated for simple FS.

CPS guidelines state simple febrile seizures carry no increased epilepsy risk above baseline (~1%). Risk factors for recurrence: age <18 months, lower temperature at seizure. Antipyretics do NOT prevent febrile seizures.

**Q:** What are the Canadian driving restrictions after a seizure?

**A:** Varies by province. Generally: 3-12 month seizure-free period for private driving (6mo in most provinces). Commercial driving: typically 5 years seizure-free off medication. Provoked seizure: shorter restrictions

Physician has mandatory reporting obligation in some provinces (ON, AB), discretionary in others (BC, QC). Must document discussion. Seizure while driving is a criminal code offense if physician has warned patient.

**Q:** What is the workup for a first unprovoked seizure in an adult?

**A:** History (witness account, prodrome, post-ictal features), neurological exam, blood work (glucose, electrolytes, calcium, magnesium, renal/liver function, toxicology), MRI brain with epilepsy protocol (preferred over CT), EEG (within 24-48 hours ideally), and ECG to exclude cardiac syncope.

Canadian League Against Epilepsy recommends MRI over CT for first seizure workup due to superior sensitivity for structural lesions. EEG within 24 hours has highest yield. After a single unprovoked seizure, recurrence risk is ~40% at 2 years; AED treatment reduces this to ~20% but does not change long-term prognosis.

**Q:** What are the driving restrictions after a first seizure in Canadian provinces?

**A:** Varies by province but generally: private vehicle — seizure-free for 3-12 months (most provinces require 6 months minimum for unprovoked seizure). Commercial vehicle — typically 1-5 years seizure-free depending on province. Must be seizure-free off medication for some commercial licenses. Physician has duty to report in most provinces.

Canadian Council of Motor Transport Administrators (CCMTA) provides national guidelines adopted variably by provinces. Ontario requires 6 months seizure-free for Group 1 (private). Physicians have mandatory reporting obligations in most provinces (exceptions: Alberta, Quebec allow patient self-reporting). Document counselling in chart.

**Q:** What is appropriate seizure first aid and what common myths should be dispelled?

**A:** Do: protect from injury (clear surroundings), place on side (recovery position), time the seizure, stay with patient, call 911 if >5 minutes. Do NOT: restrain, put anything in the mouth, give oral fluids/meds during active seizure. Post-ictal: reassure, check for injuries, assess for incontinence.

Epilepsy Canada emphasizes public education on seizure first aid. The myth of swallowing the tongue persists and causes harm from oral insertion attempts. Seizure lasting >5 minutes warrants emergency response due to risk of status epilepticus. Buccal midazolam or intranasal midazolam can be prescribed for home use.

**Q:** What is SUDEP and what are its risk factors?

**A:** Sudden Unexpected Death in Epilepsy — sudden death in an epilepsy patient without structural or toxicologic cause. Risk ~1/1000 per year. Risk factors: generalized tonic-clonic seizures (most important), poor AED adherence, nocturnal seizures, young adult onset, polypharmacy, intellectual disability, prone sleeping position.

SUDEP is the leading cause of epilepsy-related mortality. Canadian epilepsy guidelines recommend discussing SUDEP with all epilepsy patients as part of informed care. Seizure freedom is the most protective factor. Nocturnal seizure monitoring devices may reduce risk. Autopsy is essential for diagnosis.

**Q:** How is refractory status epilepticus managed?

**A:** Stage 1 (0-5 min): benzodiazepines (IV lorazepam 0.1 mg/kg or IM midazolam). Stage 2 (5-20 min): IV phenytoin/fosphenytoin, valproate, or levetiracetam. Stage 3 (>20 min, refractory): ICU with continuous EEG, anesthetic agents (propofol, midazolam infusion, or thiopental) titrated to burst suppression.

Refractory status epilepticus (RSE) fails two adequate AED trials. Super-refractory SE persists >24 hours despite anesthesia. Canadian guidelines recommend continuous EEG monitoring. Mortality is 20-40% for RSE. Always investigate underlying cause (infection, metabolic, structural, autoimmune encephalitis).

**Q:** Which AEDs are preferred for focal seizures vs generalized seizures?

**A:** Focal: lamotrigine, levetiracetam, carbamazepine, oxcarbazepine (first-line). Generalized tonic-clonic: valproate (most effective but teratogenic), lamotrigine, levetiracetam. Absence: ethosuximide (first-line), valproate, lamotrigine. Avoid carbamazepine/phenytoin in generalized — may worsen absence and myoclonic seizures.

Canadian League Against Epilepsy recommends valproate avoidance in women of childbearing potential due to teratogenicity (NTD 1-2%, IQ reduction). Levetiracetam and lamotrigine are preferred in this population. All AED changes in women should include contraception counselling and folic acid 5 mg daily.

**Q:** What is the approach to AED withdrawal in a seizure-free patient?

**A:** Consider withdrawal after >=2 years seizure-free. Taper over minimum 2-3 months (6 months for benzodiazepines/barbiturates). Recurrence risk ~30-40% within 2 years. Favorable factors: single seizure type, normal EEG, normal MRI, idiopathic etiology. Unfavorable: juvenile myoclonic epilepsy (lifelong treatment), structural lesion.

Canadian guidelines recommend discussing recurrence risk and driving implications before tapering. Driving restrictions restart if seizures recur during taper. JME has >90% relapse rate if medication stopped. EEG before withdrawal helps risk-stratify. Taper one medication at a time in polytherapy.

**Q:** How do you differentiate epileptic seizures from psychogenic non-epileptic events (PNEE)?

**A:** PNEE features: eyes closed during event (eyes typically open in epileptic seizures), waxing/waning intensity, pelvic thrusting, side-to-side head movement, prolonged duration (>2 min), preserved awareness during bilateral convulsive movements, crying during/after event, no post-ictal confusion. Definitive diagnosis: video-EEG monitoring.

PNEE (previously called pseudoseizures) are not volitional. They commonly coexist with epilepsy in 10-20% of patients. Canadian guidelines emphasize delivering the diagnosis compassionately. CBT is the evidence-based treatment. Inappropriate AED treatment causes harm. Video-EEG is the gold standard.

**Q:** What are the special considerations for epilepsy management in pregnancy?

**A:** Preconception: folic acid 5 mg daily, switch from valproate to safer AED (lamotrigine, levetiracetam). During pregnancy: monitor AED levels (especially lamotrigine which decreases significantly), avoid polytherapy, plan delivery at tertiary centre. Postpartum: readjust doses, breastfeeding generally safe with most AEDs.

Valproate is Category X — NTD risk 1-2%, autism risk, and mean IQ reduction of 6-9 points in exposed children. Canadian epilepsy and SOGC guidelines mandate contraception counselling for all women on AEDs. The Canadian Epilepsy Pregnancy Registry provides outcome data. Lamotrigine levels drop 50-60% during pregnancy requiring dose increases.

**Q:** What features on history and exam help distinguish seizure from syncope?

**A:** Seizure: aura (deja vu, epigastric rising), lateral tongue bite, post-ictal confusion >5 min, urinary incontinence, cyanosis during event, tonic-clonic movements >30 seconds. Syncope: prodrome (lightheadedness, warmth, nausea, greying vision), rapid recovery (<1 min), pallor, brief myoclonic jerks (<10 seconds) are common and do not indicate epilepsy.

Convulsive syncope (brief myoclonus from cerebral hypoperfusion) is often misdiagnosed as epilepsy. Lateral tongue bite is highly specific for generalized seizure (PPV >95%). Frontal tongue bite is non-specific. Canadian guidelines recommend ECG in all first seizure presentations to exclude cardiac causes.

**Q:** Define status epilepticus and its threshold for diagnosis.

**A:** Continuous or intermittent seizure activity for greater than 5 minutes without regaining consciousness. It is a life-threatening emergency.

Status epilepticus represents a failure of normal seizure-terminating mechanisms and can lead to neuronal injury, systemic complications, and death. The 5-minute threshold guides when to initiate aggressive treatment. The prior 30-minute threshold has been shortened given evidence of injury with prolonged seizures.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for status epilepticus in the emergency department?

**A:** Benzodiazepine — lorazepam 2 mg IV push up to 10 mg, OR midazolam 5 mg IM up to 10 mg. Repeat at 10 minutes if ineffective. Use intranasal route if no IV/IO access.

Benzodiazepines enhance GABA-mediated inhibition to terminate seizures. IV lorazepam and IM midazolam are both effective; randomized trials showed IM midazolam is non-inferior to IV lorazepam with faster delivery in the prehospital setting. Early administration is critical.

**Q:** What is the second-line treatment if benzodiazepines fail to terminate status epilepticus?

**A:** Phenytoin 20 mg/kg IV at 25–50 mg/min in adults; may give additional 10 mg/kg IV 10 min after loading infusion. Patient must be on cardiac monitor (risk of dysrhythmias and hypotension).

If benzodiazepines fail, phenytoin (or fosphenytoin) is given as the second-line antiepileptic. Levetiracetam (loading dose 60 mg/kg IV, max 4500 mg over 5–15 min) is an alternative or adjunct for seizure prevention after resolution. Phenytoin requires cardiac monitoring due to risk of dysrhythmias and hypotension.

**Q:** What is the minimum workup for an adult presenting with a first-time seizure?

**A:** CBC and differential, blood glucose (POCT capillary), electrolytes, calcium, magnesium, VBG, serum glucose, toxicology screen, antiepileptic drug levels (if relevant), beta-hCG (in women of childbearing age).

The minimum workup aims to identify reversible metabolic causes (hypoglycemia, electrolyte disturbances), toxic causes, and assess baseline status. Neuroimaging (CT then MRI) and LP are considered based on clinical presentation and risk factors for structural or infectious etiology.

**Q:** How do you clinically differentiate syncope from a seizure?

**A:** Seizure: preceded by aura, tonic-clonic movements, tongue biting (high specificity), loss of bladder/bowel control, postictal period. Syncope: prodrome (presyncope symptoms), postural context, no postictal confusion, myoclonic jerks (a sign of syncope NOT seizure), rapid return to baseline.

Myoclonic jerks during the event are commonly mistaken for seizure activity but are a normal feature of syncope due to cerebral hypoperfusion. The postictal period (confusion, fatigue after the event) is the most reliable clinical distinguishing feature of seizure.

## Multiple Sclerosis & Demyelinating Disease

**Q:** A 28-year-old woman has an episode of unilateral visual loss with eye pain (optic neuritis). MRI shows periventricular white matter lesions. Diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Multiple sclerosis (clinically isolated syndrome or MS); treat optic neuritis with IV methylprednisolone 1g/day x3-5 days to speed recovery; start disease-modifying therapy (DMT) to prevent further relapses

McDonald Criteria diagnose MS based on dissemination in space (DIS) and time (DIT). Optic neuritis is the presenting feature in 25% of MS. MS Canada/Canadian MS guidelines recommend starting DMT after first episode with MRI evidence of MS.

**Q:** What are the McDonald criteria for MS diagnosis?

**A:** MS diagnosis requires dissemination in space (DIS: ≥2 lesions in different CNS regions: periventricular, juxtacortical, infratentorial, spinal cord) AND dissemination in time (DIT: T1 gadolinium-enhancing lesion at same time as T2 lesion, or new T2 lesion on follow-up)

McDonald 2017 criteria allow MS diagnosis at first attack (CIS) with appropriate MRI evidence of DIS and DIT. CSF oligoclonal bands can substitute for DIT. MS must be distinguished from neuromyelitis optica (AQP4-IgG+) which requires different treatment.

**Q:** What disease-modifying therapies (DMTs) are available for relapsing-remitting MS?

**A:** First-line: interferons (IFNβ-1a, IFNβ-1b), glatiramer acetate, dimethyl fumarate, teriflunomide; High-efficacy: natalizumab, ocrelizumab, siponimod, cladribine, ofatumumab

MS Canada guidelines recommend starting DMT early to prevent disability accumulation. High-efficacy DMTs (natalizumab, ocrelizumab) are increasingly used earlier. Treatment decision: relapse frequency, MRI activity, disability progression, patient preferences.

**Q:** A patient with MS is on natalizumab and develops progressive confusion and hemiplegia. MRI shows large demyelinating lesion. What is the concerning diagnosis?

**A:** Progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML) from JC virus reactivation — risk stratified by anti-JCV antibody titer (index >0.9 increases risk); withdraw natalizumab, manage PML with immune reconstitution

PML is a serious natalizumab complication. JCV antibody monitoring guides risk stratification; after 24 months and index >1.5, consider switching DMT. PML management: immune reconstitution with plasma exchange (to remove natalizumab), then manage IRIS.

**Q:** What is Uhthoff phenomenon?

**A:** Transient worsening of MS symptoms with increased body temperature (fever, hot bath, exercise) — NOT a relapse; symptoms resolve when temperature normalizes

Uhthoff phenomenon occurs because demyelinated axons are temperature-sensitive — conduction impairs with heat. Management: cooling strategies. Distinguished from MS relapse (which lasts >24h and may require steroids). Pseudorelapse (fatigue, infection) must also be excluded.

**Q:** What is neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder (NMOSD) and how does it differ from MS?

**A:** NMOSD: AQP4-IgG (aquaporin-4 antibody) positive in most cases; attacks are more severe (bilateral optic neuritis, longitudinally extensive transverse myelitis >3 segments); does NOT respond to MS DMTs — may worsen

Distinguishing NMOSD from MS is critical because: interferons and natalizumab may exacerbate NMOSD. AQP4-IgG serology is diagnostic. Treatment: high-dose steroids for acute attacks; rituximab, inebilizumab, eculizumab, or satralizumab for prevention.

**Q:** What are the symptoms of MS and which physical findings help localize lesions?

**A:** Vision changes (optic neuritis), weakness/spasticity (corticospinal tract), sensory loss (spinothalamic/dorsal column), internuclear ophthalmoplegia (MLF lesion), bladder dysfunction, cerebellar signs, cognitive changes

Internuclear ophthalmoplegia (bilateral in MS) is highly specific for MS in a young person. L'Hermitte sign (electric shock sensation on neck flexion) indicates cervical cord demyelination. Lhermitte sign supports cervical spinal cord involvement.

**Q:** What are the prognostic factors for disability progression in MS?

**A:** Poor prognosis: male sex, older age at onset, high relapse frequency, incomplete recovery from relapses, high lesion burden on MRI, presence of T1 black holes, progressive course

Progressive MS (PPMS, SPMS) is associated with faster disability accumulation. Ocrelizumab (anti-CD20) has modest benefit in PPMS — first treatment approved for primary progressive MS (ORATORIO trial). Regular MRI surveillance monitors disease activity and DMT efficacy.

**Q:** What is spasticity in MS and how is it managed?

**A:** Spasticity: velocity-dependent increase in muscle tone from upper motor neuron damage; treat with: physiotherapy, oral baclofen (first-line), tizanidine, nabiximols (cannabis extract, Sativex), intrathecal baclofen for severe cases

Spasticity causes pain, reduced mobility, and difficulty with ADLs. Physiotherapy and regular stretching are foundational. Baclofen pump is highly effective for severe spasticity resistant to oral medications. Nabiximols (Sativex) is Health Canada-approved for MS spasticity.

**Q:** What is the role of vitamin D in multiple sclerosis?

**A:** Low vitamin D levels are associated with increased MS risk and disease activity; supplementation (2000 IU/day) is recommended — not proven to treat MS but may have benefit as adjunct

Epidemiological studies show strong inverse correlation between sun exposure, vitamin D, and MS prevalence (higher rates at northern latitudes). Canadian MS patients have high rates of vitamin D deficiency. Current evidence does not support high-dose vitamin D treatment.

**Q:** What are the McDonald 2017 criteria for diagnosing multiple sclerosis?

**A:** MS requires dissemination in space (DIS) and time (DIT). DIS: >=1 T2 lesion in >=2 of 4 CNS areas (periventricular, cortical/juxtacortical, infratentorial, spinal cord). DIT: simultaneous enhancing and non-enhancing lesions, OR new lesion on follow-up, OR CSF oligoclonal bands.

The 2017 McDonald criteria allow earlier MS diagnosis by incorporating CSF oligoclonal bands as a substitute for DIT. This enables diagnosis at CIS with appropriate MRI and CSF findings, facilitating earlier treatment.

**Q:** What are the first-line disease-modifying therapies for relapsing-remitting MS in Canada?

**A:** First-line: interferon beta, glatiramer acetate, teriflunomide, dimethyl fumarate. High-efficacy: natalizumab, fingolimod, ocrelizumab. Highly active disease: alemtuzumab, cladribine.

Canadian MS Treatment Optimization Recommendations support both escalation and early intensive strategies. High-efficacy therapies reduce relapse rate by 50-70% vs interferon at 30%. Treatment choice considers JC virus status and family planning.

**Q:** How is an acute MS relapse managed?

**A:** IV methylprednisolone 1g daily for 3-5 days. Oral prednisone 1250 mg for 3 days is non-inferior (COPOUSEP trial). Plasma exchange for steroid-refractory relapses. Must involve new/worsening deficit lasting >24 hours without fever/infection.

Canadian guidelines align with AAN recommendations for high-dose corticosteroids. Pseudo-relapses (worsening due to infection, heat, stress) should be excluded before steroids. Tapered oral steroids lack strong evidence.

**Q:** What is the risk of PML with natalizumab and how is it mitigated?

**A:** PML risk depends on: JC virus antibody status, anti-JCV antibody index (>1.5 = higher risk), and prior immunosuppressant use. Risk ranges from <1/10,000 (JCV negative) to ~1/80 (JCV+ with index >1.5 and prior IS). Mitigation: JCV testing q6 months, extended interval dosing.

PML is a devastating JC virus reactivation. Canadian MS clinics routinely monitor JCV status. Extended interval dosing (every 6-8 weeks) reduces PML risk while maintaining efficacy. Ocrelizumab is a common switch option.

**Q:** What are the typical CSF findings in MS?

**A:** Oligoclonal bands present in CSF but not serum (>95% sensitivity), elevated IgG index (>0.7), mildly elevated WBC (<50 lymphocytes), normal-mildly elevated protein. CSF is not required if MRI criteria are met.

CSF oligoclonal bands are the most sensitive lab finding. The 2017 McDonald criteria allow OCBs to substitute for dissemination in time. CSF is particularly valuable when MRI findings are equivocal.

**Q:** What distinguishes MS subtypes (RRMS, SPMS, PPMS)?

**A:** RRMS (85%): discrete relapses with recovery, stable between attacks. SPMS: RRMS transitioning to progressive worsening without distinct relapses. PPMS (10-15%): progressive from onset, typically progressive myelopathy, equal sex ratio.

RRMS converts to SPMS at ~2-3% per year untreated. PPMS has later onset (~40) and fewer MRI lesions. Ocrelizumab is the only DMT with PPMS evidence (ORATORIO trial). Canadian guidelines recommend annual screening for progression.

**Q:** How is NMOSD distinguished from MS and why does it matter?

**A:** NMOSD: AQP4-IgG antibody positive, severe bilateral optic neuritis and longitudinally extensive transverse myelitis (>=3 vertebral segments). Brain MRI often normal early. Treatment: azathioprine, rituximab, eculizumab. Interferon beta WORSENS NMOSD.

NMOSD must be distinguished because MS therapies (interferons, fingolimod) can exacerbate it. AQP4-IgG has >99% specificity. Canadian neurologists test AQP4 antibodies in any patient with optic neuritis or longitudinally extensive myelitis.

**Q:** What are the EDSS score milestones in MS?

**A:** EDSS 0: normal exam. 4.0: walks >500m without aid. 6.0: requires unilateral assistance to walk 100m. 6.5: bilateral assistance. 7.0: wheelchair-bound. 8.0: bed-bound. 10.0: death from MS.

The EDSS is the standard disability measure in MS trials and Canadian MS clinics. Key limitation is heavy weighting toward ambulation. EDSS 4.0 and 6.0 are major milestones used in treatment decisions.

**Q:** What is the recommended MRI monitoring protocol for MS patients on DMT?

**A:** Baseline MRI brain and spinal cord before starting DMT. Follow-up at 6 months then annually. New T2 or gadolinium-enhancing lesions on therapy indicate breakthrough disease requiring DMT reassessment.

Canadian MS Treatment Optimization Recommendations define breakthrough disease as new MRI activity, relapses, or disability progression on DMT. MRI is the most sensitive measure. Spinal cord MRI is added when clinical features suggest cord involvement.

**Q:** What are the common presentations of clinically isolated syndrome (CIS)?

**A:** Optic neuritis (25%): unilateral painful vision loss. Transverse myelitis: band-like sensory level, weakness, urinary retention. Brainstem syndrome: diplopia, vertigo, INO, facial numbness. Lhermitte sign. Uhthoff phenomenon: symptom worsening with heat.

CIS is the first clinical demyelinating event. MRI showing >=2 T2 lesions at CIS predicts 80%+ conversion to MS. Canadian neurologists initiate DMT at CIS when McDonald criteria are met to delay disability.

**Q:** What are the disease-modifying therapies for MS and their key side effects?

**A:** Platform: interferon-beta (flu-like, hepatotoxicity), glatiramer (injection site). Oral: fingolimod (bradycardia, macular edema), dimethyl fumarate (flushing, lymphopenia). High-efficacy: natalizumab (PML risk), ocrelizumab (infusion reactions)

Escalation vs early aggressive approach. PML risk with natalizumab: JCV antibody positive + >2 years use. Ocrelizumab: first-line for primary progressive MS. Alemtuzumab: autoimmune complications.

**Q:** Compare interferon-beta and glatiramer acetate for MS: mechanism, efficacy, and key side effects.

**A:** Both reduce relapse rate by ~30%. IFN-beta: immunomodulatory; side effects include flu-like symptoms, injection site reactions, hepatotoxicity, depression, leukopenia. Glatiramer: mimics myelin basic protein; side effects include injection site reactions, benign immediate post-injection reaction, lipoatrophy.

Both are first-line moderate-efficacy DMTs for RRMS per Canadian MS guidelines. IFN-beta requires LFTs and CBC monitoring; glatiramer does not require blood monitoring. Adherence is often limited by injection burden, prompting switches to oral agents.

**Q:** What is the mechanism, efficacy, and major risk of natalizumab in MS?

**A:** Anti-alpha4-integrin monoclonal antibody blocking lymphocyte migration across BBB. Reduces relapses by ~68%. Major risk: PML from JC virus reactivation. Risk stratified by JCV antibody index, prior immunosuppressant use, and treatment duration >2 years.

PML risk is ~1/1000 overall but ~1/90 in JCV-positive patients with prior immunosuppression and >2 years treatment. Canadian guidelines recommend JCV antibody testing every 6 months and MRI surveillance. Extended interval dosing (every 6 weeks) may reduce PML risk.

**Q:** What is the role of ocrelizumab in MS and what monitoring is needed?

**A:** Anti-CD20 monoclonal antibody approved for both RRMS and PPMS (first DMT approved for PPMS). IV every 6 months. Requires hepatitis B screening, immunoglobulin levels monitoring, infection screening. No live vaccines.

OPERA I/II showed superiority over IFN-beta in RRMS. ORATORIO showed modest PPMS benefit. Hepatitis B reactivation is a serious risk. Hypogammaglobulinemia can develop with prolonged use requiring IgG monitoring. Canadian guidelines recommend it as high-efficacy therapy.

**Q:** A 30-year-old woman presents with acute unilateral vision loss, pain with eye movement, and RAPD. What is the workup?

**A:** Optic neuritis workup: MRI brain/orbits with gadolinium, OCT, visual evoked potentials (prolonged P100), AQP4-IgG and MOG-IgG antibodies, CSF (oligoclonal bands), visual field testing.

Optic neuritis is the presenting symptom in 20% of MS. The ONTT showed IV methylprednisolone speeds recovery but does not change final visual outcome. MRI findings determine MS risk: >=1 white matter lesion = 72% 15-year risk vs 25% if normal. AQP4 testing rules out NMOSD.

**Q:** How do you differentiate NMOSD from MS?

**A:** NMOSD: AQP4-IgG positive (70-80%), severe poorly-recovering attacks, bilateral optic neuritis, longitudinally extensive transverse myelitis (>=3 segments), area postrema syndrome, brain MRI often normal. MS: oligoclonal bands in CSF, periventricular/juxtacortical lesions, short spinal cord lesions (<2 segments).

Distinguishing NMOSD from MS is critical because IFN-beta and natalizumab can worsen NMOSD. NMOSD treatment: rituximab, eculizumab, inebilizumab, satralizumab. AQP4-IgG has >99% specificity via cell-based assay. MOG-antibody disease is a separate entity with better prognosis.

**Q:** What are the McDonald criteria for MS diagnosis?

**A:** Demonstrate dissemination in space (DIS: lesions in >=2 of 4 CNS regions — periventricular, cortical/juxtacortical, infratentorial, spinal cord) AND dissemination in time (DIT: simultaneous enhancing and non-enhancing lesions, or new lesion on follow-up). CSF oligoclonal bands can substitute for DIT.

2017 McDonald criteria allow diagnosis at first clinical event with a single MRI, enabling earlier DMT initiation. Canadian MS guidelines adopt these criteria. They apply only to typical MS presentations — must exclude mimics (NMOSD, sarcoidosis, vasculitis) first.

**Q:** When is MS treatment escalation indicated?

**A:** Breakthrough disease on current DMT: >=1 relapse/year, new/enlarging MRI lesions, or disability progression. Switch from moderate-efficacy (IFN-beta, glatiramer, dimethyl fumarate) to high-efficacy DMT (natalizumab, ocrelizumab, alemtuzumab, cladribine).

Canadian guidelines recognize both escalation and early intensive treatment approaches. Younger patients with high lesion burden and poor prognostic factors may benefit from first-line high-efficacy therapy (TREAT-MS paradigm). NEDA (No Evidence of Disease Activity) is an emerging treatment target.

**Q:** How is an acute MS relapse managed?

**A:** IV methylprednisolone 1 g daily for 3-5 days. Reassess at 2 weeks. If incomplete recovery, consider plasma exchange (PLEX) — 5-7 exchanges over 10-14 days, especially for severe steroid-refractory relapses.

Steroids hasten recovery but do not alter final disability. Canadian MS guidelines recommend treating relapses causing functional impairment; minor sensory symptoms may be observed. GI prophylaxis with PPI and glucose monitoring during pulse steroids are standard supportive measures.

**Q:** What vaccines are contraindicated in MS patients on immunosuppressive DMTs?

**A:** All live vaccines (MMR, varicella, live zoster/Zostavax, yellow fever, BCG) are contraindicated. Inactivated vaccines are safe but may have reduced efficacy on anti-CD20 therapies. Recombinant zoster vaccine (Shingrix) is safe. Ideally complete vaccination before starting DMT.

Canadian MS guidelines recommend completing vaccination before B-cell depleting therapies due to blunted response. Time vaccines >=4-6 weeks before or >=3 months after anti-CD20 infusion. Annual influenza, COVID-19, pneumococcal, and hepatitis B (screen before anti-CD20) are recommended.

**Q:** What are poor prognostic factors in MS at diagnosis?

**A:** Male sex, older onset (>40), primary progressive course, high T2 lesion volume, spinal cord lesions, infratentorial lesions, incomplete recovery from first relapse, short inter-relapse interval, African descent, early cognitive impairment.

Prognostic factors guide treatment intensity. Patients with multiple poor factors may benefit from early high-efficacy DMT. EDSS tracks disability over time. Brain atrophy rate on serial MRI is an emerging prognostic biomarker in Canadian MS research.

## Dementia & Cognitive Impairment

**Q:** A 75-year-old has progressive memory loss, word-finding difficulty, and disorientation to date over 2 years. MMSE is 18/30. Family reports personality changes. What is the most likely diagnosis?

**A:** Alzheimer disease (AD) — most common dementia (60-70%); characterized by insidious onset, gradual progression, early episodic memory impairment, language difficulties

Alzheimer Disease diagnostic criteria require cognitive decline from prior functioning in ≥2 cognitive domains causing functional impairment. Canadian Consensus Conference on Dementia (CCCD) guidelines: comprehensive dementia assessment including cognitive testing, functional status, and biomarker support (CSF, PET, MRI).

**Q:** What cognitive screening tools are used in Canada for dementia assessment?

**A:** MMSE (Mini-Mental State Examination, 0-30 points), MoCA (Montreal Cognitive Assessment, 0-30 points) — MoCA is more sensitive for mild cognitive impairment (cutoff <26/30)

CCCD recommends MoCA as preferred screening tool for cognitive impairment as it is more sensitive than MMSE for MCI and early dementia. MMSE is less sensitive for detecting executive dysfunction and visuospatial problems. Comprehensive neuropsychological testing for complex cases.

**Q:** What is mild cognitive impairment (MCI) and what is its prognosis?

**A:** MCI: cognitive decline greater than expected for age with preserved functional independence; converts to dementia at ~15%/year vs. 1-2%/year in normal aging

CCCD guidelines: MCI requires comprehensive assessment to determine etiology. Amnestic MCI has highest risk of progression to AD. No pharmacological treatment proven to prevent MCI progression to dementia. CCCD recommends lifestyle interventions (exercise, cognitive engagement, vascular risk factor control).

**Q:** What reversible causes of dementia must be excluded?

**A:** B12 deficiency, hypothyroidism, depression (pseudodementia), normal pressure hydrocephalus (NPH), subdural hematoma, neurosyphilis, HIV encephalopathy, medication effects (anticholinergics, benzodiazepines)

CCCD guidelines require a basic laboratory workup: CBC, B12, TSH, electrolytes, creatinine, calcium, glucose, LFTs. CT head or MRI to exclude structural causes. NPH triad: wet (incontinence), wobbly (gait disturbance), wacky (dementia) — gait disturbance most prominent feature.

**Q:** What features distinguish Lewy body dementia (DLB) from Alzheimer disease?

**A:** DLB: visual hallucinations, parkinsonism, fluctuating cognition, REM sleep behavior disorder; extreme sensitivity to antipsychotics; alpha-synuclein pathology — early gait disturbance before severe cognitive decline

CCCD guidelines: DLB is the second most common neurodegenerative dementia. Avoid conventional antipsychotics in DLB (neuroleptic sensitivity causing rigidity, sedation, death). Cholinesterase inhibitors (rivastigmine) have evidence for cognitive benefit in DLB.

**Q:** What is vascular dementia and how does it differ from Alzheimer disease?

**A:** Vascular dementia: stepwise cognitive decline with focal neurological signs, prominent executive dysfunction, gait disturbance; associated with stroke, white matter changes on MRI; often overlaps with AD

CCCD: pure vascular dementia uncommon; vascular cognitive impairment (VCI) includes full spectrum from VaMCI to vascular dementia. Treatment: aggressive management of vascular risk factors (BP, diabetes, dyslipidemia, antiplatelet for strokes). Cholinesterase inhibitors less evidence than in AD.

**Q:** What is frontotemporal dementia (FTD) and how does it present differently from Alzheimer disease?

**A:** FTD: early personality change, disinhibition, apathy, language dysfunction (semantic dementia, progressive non-fluent aphasia); relatively preserved memory early; younger onset (50s-60s)
