CCCD guidelines: FTD diagnosis requires neuropsychological testing and neuroimaging (frontotemporal atrophy on MRI). Behavioral variant FTD: social inappropriateness, impulsivity, loss of empathy. TDP-43 or FUS protein pathology. No approved disease-modifying therapy.

**Q:** What pharmacological treatments are available for Alzheimer disease?

**A:** Cholinesterase inhibitors (donepezil, rivastigmine, galantamine) for mild-moderate AD; memantine (NMDA antagonist) for moderate-severe AD or as add-on; lecanemab (anti-amyloid antibody) for early AD — modest benefits

CCCD recommends cholinesterase inhibitors as first-line for mild-moderate AD. They improve cognitive and functional outcomes. Memantine reduces agitation and improves moderate-severe AD. Lecanemab (FDA and Health Canada approved 2024) slows cognitive decline in early AD by 27%.

**Q:** What is the approach to behavioral and psychological symptoms of dementia (BPSD)?

**A:** Non-pharmacological first: structured environment, activities, caregiver education, sleep hygiene; pharmacological only if non-pharm fails: antidepressants (SSRIs for depression/agitation), low-dose atypical antipsychotics (risperidone for severe agitation/psychosis)

CCCD guidelines emphasize non-pharmacological approaches for BPSD as first-line. Conventional antipsychotics and benzodiazepines increase falls, sedation, and mortality in dementia. Atypical antipsychotics (risperidone, quetiapine) carry black box warning for increased mortality in elderly dementia patients.

**Q:** A 70-year-old has dementia with early gait disturbance, urinary incontinence, and enlarged ventricles on CT with no significant cortical atrophy. Diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Normal pressure hydrocephalus (NPH); treat with large-volume LP (remove 30-50 mL CSF) as diagnostic test — if gait improves, proceed to VP shunt

NPH triad: gait disturbance (most prominent and first), urinary incontinence, dementia. Diagnosis: CT/MRI showing hydrocephalus out of proportion to cortical atrophy; CSF tap test with gait assessment before and after 30-50 mL removal. Ventriculoperitoneal (VP) shunt provides durable improvement.

**Q:** What is the diagnostic workup for dementia per CCCD guidelines?

**A:** History (including informant), cognitive testing (MoCA/MMSE), functional assessment, neurological exam; Labs: CBC, B12, TSH, electrolytes, LFTs, creatinine, glucose; Brain MRI (preferred over CT); neuropsychological testing

Structural neuroimaging (MRI preferred) is recommended in all new dementia assessments to exclude reversible causes and support subtype diagnosis. CSF biomarkers (amyloid, tau) and amyloid PET scan increasingly available to confirm AD pathology.

**Q:** What is the role of genetic testing in early-onset dementia?

**A:** Genetic counseling and testing offered for: family history of early-onset AD (<65), APOE ε4 status (increases AD risk 3-8x), APP/PSEN1/PSEN2 mutations (autosomal dominant AD), FTD-related mutations (C9orf72, MAPT, GRN)

CCCD guidelines: genetic testing for familial early-onset AD requires genetic counseling first. APOE ε4 is a risk factor, not diagnostic. PSEN1 mutation carriers have nearly 100% lifetime AD risk. Genetic testing affects insurance and employment — careful pre-test counseling.

**Q:** What is Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (CJD) and what are its diagnostic features?

**A:** CJD: rapidly progressive dementia with myoclonus, cerebellar ataxia, visual disturbances; EEG: periodic sharp wave complexes; MRI: DWI cortical ribboning/basal ganglia signal; CSF 14-3-3 protein; RT-QuIC in CSF

Prion disease: abnormal PrP protein accumulates. Sporadic CJD most common. Variant CJD (vCJD) from BSE exposure — young adults. CJD is rapidly fatal (median 8 months). No effective treatment. Public health reporting required.

**Q:** What ethical and legal considerations are important in dementia care?

**A:** Advance care planning early in disease; capacity assessment for medical decisions; Power of Attorney (POA) for personal care and property; driving assessment and reporting; residential care planning

CCCD guidelines recommend discussing advance care planning and legal documents (POA, advance directive) early in dementia. Capacity is decision-specific and fluctuating. Substitute decision-makers follow patient's prior expressed wishes. Driving assessment: formal road test often required.

**Q:** How should pain be assessed and managed in a non-verbal patient with advanced dementia?

**A:** Use observational pain scales (PAINAD, PACSLAC for non-verbal); treat pain with acetaminophen first-line; opioids for moderate-severe pain; avoid NSAIDs (GI/renal risk in elderly)

CCCD and CCEC (Canadian Coalition for End-of-life Care) guidelines emphasize that pain assessment requires behavioral observation in patients who cannot self-report. Regular scheduled analgesics often preferable to PRN dosing in dementia. Non-pharmacological approaches (positioning, massage) as adjuncts.

**Q:** What is the workup for new-onset dementia?

**A:** CBC, lytes, Ca, TSH, B12, glucose, syphilis serology, CT/MRI brain. Consider: HIV, heavy metals, LP if atypical

Reversible causes: B12 deficiency, hypothyroidism, NPH, subdural hematoma, depression. Alzheimer's: temporal/parietal atrophy. Vascular: stepwise decline, periventricular WM changes.

**Q:** What is the recommended initial workup for suspected dementia?

**A:** Cognitive testing (MoCA preferred), bloodwork (CBC, electrolytes, calcium, glucose, TSH, B12, folate), and neuroimaging (CT or MRI brain). Consider HIV, syphilis serology in appropriate contexts.

CCCD recommends MoCA over MMSE for screening (better sensitivity for MCI). Reversible causes must be excluded. MRI is preferred for vascular and frontotemporal patterns.

**Q:** What distinguishes Alzheimer, frontotemporal, Lewy body, and vascular dementia?

**A:** AD: episodic memory loss, temporal-parietal atrophy. FTD: personality/behavioral change or progressive aphasia, age <65. DLB: fluctuating cognition, visual hallucinations, parkinsonism, REM sleep behavior disorder. VaD: stepwise decline, executive dysfunction, white matter disease.

Distinguishing subtypes is high-yield for MCCQE1. DLB patients have severe neuroleptic sensitivity. FTD is the second most common young-onset dementia. CCCD guidelines emphasize clinical phenotyping.

**Q:** What pharmacological treatments are available for Alzheimer disease?

**A:** Cholinesterase inhibitors (donepezil 5-10mg, rivastigmine, galantamine) for mild-moderate AD. Memantine (NMDA antagonist) for moderate-severe AD. Can combine ChEI + memantine.

CCCD guidelines recommend ChEIs as first-line for mild-moderate AD. Side effects: GI (nausea, diarrhea), bradycardia, syncope. Memantine added at moderate stage (MMSE 10-20). Reassess at 3-6 months.

**Q:** What is the MoCA test and how does it compare to MMSE?

**A:** MoCA: 30-point test assessing visuospatial/executive, naming, memory, attention, language, abstraction, orientation. Score <26 suggests impairment (+1 if <=12 years education). MoCA detects MCI better than MMSE (sensitivity 90% vs 18%).

MoCA was developed in Montreal and is the preferred Canadian screening tool. It assesses executive function more thoroughly than MMSE, making it superior for vascular cognitive impairment and MCI.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for mild cognitive impairment (MCI)?

**A:** Subjective cognitive complaint, objective impairment on testing (1-1.5 SD below norms), preserved functional independence (ADLs intact), does NOT meet dementia criteria. Subtypes: amnestic and non-amnestic.

Amnestic MCI converts to AD at ~10-15% per year. Canadian guidelines recommend annual reassessment. No pharmacological treatment is recommended for MCI. Exercise and vascular risk management are advised.

**Q:** What are the potentially reversible causes of dementia?

**A:** Hypothyroidism, B12 deficiency, neurosyphilis, HIV, normal pressure hydrocephalus (gait apraxia, incontinence, dementia), chronic subdural hematoma, depression (pseudodementia), medication effects (anticholinergics, benzodiazepines).

CCCD guidelines mandate screening for reversible causes. NPH is treatable with VP shunt. Depression-related pseudodementia improves with antidepressants. Anticholinergic burden is a major modifiable risk factor in elderly.

**Q:** What is the management hierarchy for behavioral symptoms of dementia (BPSD)?

**A:** (1) Non-pharmacological first: caregiver education, structured routines, music therapy, redirection. (2) Risperidone (only antipsychotic with Health Canada approval for BPSD) at lowest dose. (3) Avoid benzodiazepines and anticholinergics.

CCCD guidelines emphasize non-pharmacological approaches first. Antipsychotics carry a black box warning for increased mortality in elderly dementia patients (1.6-1.7x). Attempt tapering q3 months.

**Q:** What is the presentation and management of normal pressure hydrocephalus?

**A:** Triad: gait apraxia (magnetic gait, first symptom), urinary incontinence, cognitive decline. Ventriculomegaly on imaging (Evans index >0.3). CSF tap test: removal of 30-50 mL improves gait. Treatment: VP shunt.

NPH is one of few surgically treatable dementias. CSF tap test has ~50-80% sensitivity for predicting shunt response. VP shunt improves gait in ~80%, cognition in ~50% of selected patients.

**Q:** What genetic risk factors and biomarkers are relevant in Alzheimer disease?

**A:** APOE e4: strongest genetic risk (3x heterozygote, 12x homozygote). Early-onset AD genes: APP, PSEN1, PSEN2 (autosomal dominant). Biomarkers: CSF Abeta42 (decreased), CSF tau/p-tau (increased), amyloid PET.

APOE testing is not recommended for routine diagnosis per Canadian guidelines. CSF biomarkers are primarily research tools in Canada. Plasma p-tau217 is an emerging blood-based biomarker.

**Q:** How is Lewy body dementia managed differently from other dementias?

**A:** DLB responds to ChEIs (rivastigmine preferred). AVOID antipsychotics-severe neuroleptic sensitivity. If needed: quetiapine at lowest dose only. Parkinsonism: cautious low-dose levodopa. RBD: melatonin first-line.

Neuroleptic sensitivity in DLB is a critical safety concern. Up to 50% experience severe reactions to antipsychotics. DLB has the best ChEI response among dementias. Visual hallucinations alone may not require treatment if non-distressing.

**Q:** What non-pharmacological interventions have evidence for dementia prevention?

**A:** Physical exercise (strongest evidence), cognitive stimulation, Mediterranean diet, vascular risk factor management, social engagement, hearing aid use for hearing loss. Midlife hearing loss treatment may reduce risk by 8%.

Lancet Commission identified 12 modifiable risk factors for 40% of dementias. Canadian Brain Health Strategy emphasizes midlife hypertension treatment, activity, and social engagement. FINGER trial showed multimodal lifestyle intervention benefit.

**Q:** What is the prognosis of different dementia types?

**A:** AD: median 8-10 years. VaD: stepwise, 5-year survival. DLB: 5-7 years, faster decline. FTD: 6-8 years. CJD: rapid progressive dementia, death in <1 year.

Understanding trajectories aids prognostication and advance care planning. Canadian guidelines recommend early goals-of-care discussions. CJD should be suspected with rapid dementia + myoclonus + periodic sharp waves on EEG.

**Q:** When is genetic testing indicated in dementia?

**A:** Early-onset AD (<65) with autosomal dominant pattern, suspected genetic FTD (C9orf72, GRN, MAPT). FTD has highest heritability (~40% family history). APOE genotyping NOT recommended routinely.

Canadian guidelines are conservative on genetic testing. C9orf72 causes both FTD and ALS. Genetic counseling is mandatory before and after testing. Early-onset AD genes: APP, PSEN1, PSEN2.

**Q:** What tools are used for staging dementia severity?

**A:** GDS (Global Deterioration Scale): 7 stages. CDR (Clinical Dementia Rating): 0-3 scale. FAST: 7 stages aligned with AD. Practical: mild (MMSE 20-26), moderate (10-19), severe (<10).

GDS and CDR are used in Canadian clinical practice. FAST >=7A predicts <6 month survival for palliative care referral. ChEIs continued until moderate-severe stage, then reassessed.

**Q:** What medicolegal issues arise in dementia care in Canada?

**A:** Capacity is decision-specific. Power of attorney should be established early. Mandatory driving reporting in most provinces. Elder abuse screening. Cognitive impairment does not automatically negate capacity.

In Canada, capacity must be assessed for each specific decision per provincial legislation (e.g., Ontario Substitute Decisions Act). Physicians must report unfit drivers to the Ministry of Transportation in most provinces.

**Q:** What distinguishes Lewy body dementia from Alzheimer's and frontotemporal dementia?

**A:** LBD: visual hallucinations, parkinsonism, fluctuating cognition, REM sleep behavior disorder, neuroleptic sensitivity. FTD: personality/behavioral changes, disinhibition, early executive dysfunction, language variants. AD: memory loss, temporal-parietal atrophy

LBD: avoid antipsychotics (severe sensitivity). Treatment: cholinesterase inhibitors effective. FTD: no effective disease-modifying treatment, younger onset (45-65), SSRIs for behavioral symptoms. Overlap with ALS in some cases.

**Q:** What are the distinguishing clinical features of Lewy body dementia vs frontotemporal dementia vs vascular dementia?

**A:** LBD: visual hallucinations, fluctuating cognition, parkinsonism, REM sleep behaviour disorder, severe neuroleptic sensitivity. FTD: early personality/behavioural changes (disinhibition, apathy), language variants (semantic, nonfluent), preserved visuospatial initially. Vascular: stepwise decline, focal neurological signs, executive dysfunction, vascular risk factors, white matter changes on MRI.

Accurate subtype diagnosis guides treatment: cholinesterase inhibitors help LBD and AD but not FTD. Antipsychotics can cause life-threatening reactions in LBD. Canadian Consensus Conference on Dementia provides diagnostic criteria. FTD is the most common dementia in patients <65 years.

**Q:** What are the indications, efficacy, and side effects of cholinesterase inhibitors in dementia?

**A:** Indicated for mild-moderate Alzheimer's and LBD. Agents: donepezil (5-10 mg), rivastigmine (patch or oral), galantamine. Modest benefit (2-4 point ADAS-cog improvement). Side effects: nausea, diarrhea, anorexia, bradycardia, insomnia, muscle cramps. Monitor for falls and syncope.

Canadian Consensus Conference recommends cholinesterase inhibitors for mild-moderate AD. Benefit should be reassessed every 6-12 months. Abrupt discontinuation may cause acute cognitive decline. They are particularly beneficial in LBD for hallucinations and cognition. Not recommended for FTD or MCI.

**Q:** What are evidence-based non-pharmacologic approaches to managing behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia (BPSD)?

**A:** First-line (before medications): identify and treat triggers (pain, infection, constipation, environment), person-centred care, music therapy, structured activities, caregiver education (PIECES/GPA frameworks), physical exercise, aromatherapy, reduce sensory overload, maintain routines.

Canadian guidelines (CCCDTD) strongly recommend non-pharmacologic interventions as first-line for BPSD. The P.I.E.C.E.S. framework (Physical, Intellectual, Emotional, Capabilities, Environment, Social) is a Canadian model for understanding and managing BPSD. Antipsychotics should be last resort due to increased mortality risk.

**Q:** When are antipsychotics indicated for BPSD, and what are the risks?

**A:** Indicated only when: severe aggression/agitation causing danger to self/others, psychosis causing significant distress, AND non-pharmacologic approaches have failed. Use lowest effective dose, reassess in 1-3 months. Risks: 1.5-1.7x increased mortality (black box warning), stroke risk, metabolic effects, falls, parkinsonism.

Health Canada and CCCDTD recommend time-limited use with regular reassessment and deprescribing attempts. Risperidone is the only antipsychotic with Health Canada approval for BPSD. Avoid in LBD (severe sensitivity). The Canadian Deprescribing Network provides an antipsychotic deprescribing algorithm.

**Q:** What tools are used to assess decision-making capacity in patients with dementia?

**A:** Capacity assessment evaluates 4 domains: 1) understanding (comprehend relevant information), 2) appreciation (apply information to own situation), 3) reasoning (weigh risks/benefits), 4) expressing a choice (communicate consistent decision). Tools: Aid to Capacity Evaluation (ACE), MacArthur Competence Assessment Tool (MacCAT).

Capacity is decision-specific, not global — a patient may lack capacity for financial decisions but retain capacity for simple medical decisions. In Canada, capacity is a clinical (not legal) determination. Each province has specific legislation (e.g., Ontario Health Care Consent Act). Document assessment thoroughly.

**Q:** What is the role of memantine in dementia management?

**A:** NMDA receptor antagonist indicated for moderate-severe Alzheimer's disease. Can be combined with cholinesterase inhibitor. Modest benefit in cognition and function. Better tolerated than ChEIs (side effects: dizziness, headache, constipation). Not indicated for mild AD or other dementia types.

Canadian Consensus Conference recommends memantine for moderate-severe AD when ChEIs alone are insufficient. The combination of donepezil + memantine showed benefit in the DOMINO-AD trial. Memantine may help with agitation in some patients. Dose adjustment needed with renal impairment.

**Q:** What cognitive screening tools are recommended for dementia assessment in primary care?

**A:** MoCA (Montreal Cognitive Assessment): preferred, tests executive function, visuospatial, memory, attention, language, orientation. Score <26/30 is abnormal. MMSE: older tool, less sensitive for mild cognitive impairment and executive dysfunction. Clock Drawing Test: quick screen for visuospatial/executive function.

MoCA was developed in Montreal and is the preferred screening tool per Canadian guidelines. It is more sensitive than MMSE for MCI and vascular cognitive impairment. Education-adjusted scoring exists. Neither tool is diagnostic alone — comprehensive neuropsychological testing is needed for atypical presentations.

**Q:** How do you differentiate delirium from dementia, and what is their relationship?

**A:** Delirium: acute onset, fluctuating attention, altered consciousness, often reversible, triggered by medical illness/drugs. Dementia: insidious onset, progressive, clear consciousness (until late), irreversible. Key: delirium is common IN dementia (dementia is the #1 risk factor for delirium) and delirium accelerates dementia progression.

The Confusion Assessment Method (CAM) diagnoses delirium: acute onset + fluctuating course + inattention + EITHER disorganized thinking or altered consciousness. Delirium in hospitalized dementia patients is often missed (hypoactive subtype). Canadian guidelines recommend delirium prevention protocols in all hospitalized elderly.

**Q:** What are the recommended investigations in the workup of new-onset dementia?

**A:** Bloodwork: CBC, electrolytes, calcium, glucose, TSH, B12, creatinine. Consider: syphilis serology (if risk factors), HIV. Neuroimaging: CT or MRI (to exclude structural causes — SDH, NPH, tumor). MRI preferred for subtyping (hippocampal atrophy in AD, focal atrophy in FTD, white matter disease in vascular).

CCCDTD recommends routine bloodwork and neuroimaging for all new dementia diagnoses to exclude reversible causes (5-10%). Reversible causes include hypothyroidism, B12 deficiency, NPH, and depression (pseudodementia). Specialized biomarkers (CSF amyloid/tau, PET) are reserved for atypical presentations.

**Q:** What resources and supports should be discussed with families of newly diagnosed dementia patients?

**A:** Advance care planning and power of attorney (while capacity remains), driving assessment, home safety evaluation, Alzheimer Society of Canada resources, caregiver support groups, day programs, respite care, community services (meals on wheels, personal support workers), medication management (blister packs, supervision).

Canadian guidelines emphasize early advance care planning while the patient retains capacity. The Alzheimer Society of Canada provides First Link programs connecting families to local resources. Caregiver burnout is a major concern — 50% of caregivers develop depression. Provincial home care services vary but should be proactively arranged.

**Q:** How is acetylcholinesterase (ACh) action terminated in the synapse, and which drugs exploit this mechanism therapeutically?

**A:** ACh is rapidly hydrolyzed by acetylcholinesterase in the synaptic cleft and by pseudocholinesterase in plasma. Acetylcholinesterase inhibitors (pyridostigmine, donepezil, galantamine, rivastigmine) increase ACh by blocking its breakdown.

Acetylcholinesterase inhibitors are used in myasthenia gravis (pyridostigmine — peripheral) and Alzheimer's disease (donepezil, galantamine, rivastigmine — central). Organophosphate poisoning also inhibits acetylcholinesterase, causing a cholinergic crisis.

**Q:** How do cholinesterase inhibitors (donepezil, galantamine, rivastigmine) differ in their approved indications for dementia?

**A:** Donepezil: approved for moderate to severe Alzheimer's dementia. Galantamine and rivastigmine: approved for mild to moderate Alzheimer's dementia. All three are reversible acetylcholinesterase inhibitors. Rivastigmine has very slow reversal ('pseudoirreversible'). Common side effects: nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, anorexia.

Donepezil is the only cholinesterase inhibitor approved for moderate-severe Alzheimer's disease. All three share the same general mechanism but differ in pharmacokinetics and indication scope.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action and indication for memantine in dementia?

**A:** Memantine is an NMDA-receptor antagonist. It is indicated for mild to moderate Alzheimer's dementia. Starting dose is 5 mg PO once daily, titrated up to 10 mg PO BID. Side effects include dizziness, headache, hypertension, constipation, confusion, and hallucinations.

Memantine works by blocking pathological glutamate-mediated NMDA receptor activation (excitotoxicity), which differs from the cholinergic mechanism of cholinesterase inhibitors. It can be used in combination with cholinesterase inhibitors.

## Headache Disorders & Migraine

**Q:** A 25-year-old woman has unilateral throbbing headache with nausea, photophobia, and phonophobia, lasting 8-72 hours and occurring 3x/month. Diagnosis and acute treatment?

**A:** Migraine without aura; acute treatment: NSAIDs (naproxen, ibuprofen), triptans (sumatriptan) for moderate-severe, anti-emetics (metoclopramide) — treat early for best effect

ICHD-3 diagnostic criteria for migraine without aura: 5+ attacks lasting 4-72h, 2+ of: unilateral, pulsating, moderate-severe, aggravated by activity; plus nausea/vomiting or photophobia + phonophobia. CTHF guidelines recommend treating early before headache becomes severe.

**Q:** What is the criteria for migraine prophylaxis and what are first-line agents?

**A:** Prophylaxis indicated when: ≥4 headache days/month, significantly disabling attacks, analgesic overuse risk, patient preference; first-line: propranolol, topiramate, amitriptyline, valproate (avoid in women of childbearing age)

Canadian Headache Society guidelines recommend prophylaxis to reduce frequency by ≥50%. CGRP monoclonal antibodies (erenumab, fremanezumab, galcanezumab) are first-line for chronic migraine or episodic migraine refractory to traditional prophylactics.

**Q:** What is medication overuse headache (MOH) and which analgesics cause it?

**A:** MOH: headache on ≥15 days/month from regular use of analgesics ≥10-15 days/month; triptans, opioids, butalbital: overuse ≥10 days/month; NSAIDs, acetaminophen: ≥15 days/month

MOH is the most common cause of chronic daily headache. Canadian Headache Society recommends withdrawal of overused medications — headache often worsens initially (rebound) before improving. Topiramate or amitriptyline can ease withdrawal. Opioid overuse requires medically supervised withdrawal.

**Q:** What are red flag features in headache requiring urgent investigation?

**A:** Thunderclap onset (SAH), neurological deficits, fever + meningism, papilledema, new headache in age >50, immunocompromised, cancer history, postural component, worsening with Valsalva

SNNOOP10 red flag criteria identify potentially dangerous secondary headaches. Thunderclap headache (reaching maximum in <1 min) requires urgent CT head followed by LP to exclude SAH. Headache with fever + stiff neck → meningitis workup.

**Q:** What is cluster headache and how does it differ from migraine?

**A:** Cluster: unilateral periorbital/temporal pain, maximal intensity within minutes, lasting 15-180 min, with ipsilateral autonomic features (lacrimation, rhinorrhea, ptosis, miosis, facial flushing); occurs in clusters (1-3x/day x weeks to months)

Cluster headache is more common in men (3:1). Episodic or chronic form. Acute treatment: 100% O2 (7-12 L/min x15-20 min) and subcutaneous sumatriptan. Prophylaxis: verapamil (first-line), lithium, melatonin. Occipital nerve block for transitional therapy.

**Q:** What is giant cell arteritis (GCA) and why must it be treated immediately?

**A:** GCA: granulomatous large vessel vasculitis in patients >50; temporal headache, scalp tenderness, jaw claudication, elevated ESR and CRP; treat immediately with high-dose prednisone (60-100 mg/day) — risk of irreversible blindness

GCA affects temporal arteries and can involve ophthalmic artery causing ischemic optic neuropathy and permanent vision loss. Temporal artery biopsy confirms diagnosis but treatment must NOT be delayed waiting for biopsy. Biopsy remains positive for 2 weeks after starting steroids.

**Q:** What is idiopathic intracranial hypertension (IIH) and who is at risk?

**A:** IIH: elevated ICP without hydrocephalus or mass lesion; predominantly overweight women of childbearing age; symptoms: headache, visual obscurations, pulsatile tinnitus, papilledema; treat with weight loss and acetazolamide

IIH diagnosis: elevated opening pressure >25 cm H2O on LP, normal CSF composition, normal MRI (may see empty sella, flattening of posterior globe). Acetazolamide reduces CSF production. Bariatric surgery for morbid obesity. Optic nerve sheath fenestration or VP shunt for vision-threatening cases.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of action of triptans and their main side effects?

**A:** Triptans: 5-HT1B/1D agonists — cause cranial vasoconstriction and inhibit CGRP release; side effects: chest tightness, flushing, paresthesias, contraindicated in cardiovascular disease and uncontrolled hypertension

Triptans are contraindicated in: coronary artery disease, MI, uncontrolled hypertension, stroke/TIA, and hemiplegic migraine. Serotonin syndrome risk when combined with MAOIs. Multiple triptans exist with different routes (SC, nasal spray, oral); subcutaneous fastest onset.

**Q:** What is a migraine with aura and what aura types are associated with contraindication to OCPs?

**A:** Migraine with aura: reversible focal neurological symptoms (visual: scintillating scotoma, fortification spectra; sensory; speech/language) before or during headache; aura increases stroke risk — migraine with aura + OCP + smoking = very high stroke risk

Canadian Headache Society and SOGC: combined OCP contraindicated in migraine with aura due to increased ischemic stroke risk. Progestin-only pill or non-hormonal contraception is safer. Migraine with aura is an independent risk factor for ischemic stroke in young women.

**Q:** What is tension-type headache and how is it differentiated from migraine?

**A:** Tension-type headache: bilateral, pressing/tightening (non-pulsating), mild-moderate intensity, not aggravated by activity, no nausea/vomiting (may have photophobia OR phonophobia but not both)

Tension-type headache is the most common headache type. Treatment: NSAIDs or acetaminophen for acute episodes; amitriptyline for prophylaxis. Absence of nausea and aggravation by activity distinguishes from migraine. Combination analgesics (codeine-containing) should be avoided to prevent MOH.

**Q:** What distinguishes migraine from tension headache from cluster?

**A:** Migraine: unilateral pulsating 4-72h, nausea, photo/phonophobia. Tension: bilateral pressing, no nausea. Cluster: unilateral periorbital, autonomic features (lacrimation, rhinorrhea), 15-180min

Migraine with aura: visual scotoma before headache. Cluster: O2 15L/min or sumatriptan SC for acute. Preventive: verapamil.

**Q:** What are the ICHD-3 diagnostic criteria for migraine without aura?

**A:** >=5 attacks lasting 4-72 hours with >=2 of: unilateral, pulsating, moderate-severe, aggravated by activity; AND >=1 of: nausea/vomiting, photophobia and phonophobia.

ICHD-3 criteria are the gold standard. Migraine affects ~12% of Canadians (F:M 3:1). MCCQE1 expects distinguishing migraine from tension-type and cluster headache.

**Q:** What are the first-line and second-line prophylactic medications for migraine?

**A:** First-line: propranolol (40-240mg), amitriptyline (10-75mg), topiramate (50-200mg), candesartan (16mg). Second-line: venlafaxine, valproate. CGRP mAbs (erenumab, fremanezumab) after failing >=2 preventives. Start prophylaxis when >=4 migraine days/month.

Canadian Headache Society recommends >=2-month trial at adequate dose. Topiramate is contraindicated in pregnancy. CGRP antibodies available in Canada with special authorization.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic features and treatment of cluster headache?

**A:** Severe unilateral orbital/temporal pain 15-180 min with ipsilateral autonomic features (lacrimation, conjunctival injection, nasal congestion, ptosis). Acute: sumatriptan SC 6mg or high-flow O2 12-15 L/min. Prophylaxis: verapamil first-line.

Cluster headache has M:F 3:1 (opposite of migraine). High-flow oxygen is uniquely effective with no contraindications. Verapamil requires ECG monitoring for PR prolongation.

**Q:** What red flags (SNOOP) warrant urgent headache investigation?

**A:** S: Systemic symptoms (fever, cancer, HIV). N: Neurological signs. O: Onset sudden (thunderclap). O: Older (>50, new onset). P: Pattern change, positional, Valsalva-precipitated, papilledema, progressive, pregnancy/postpartum.

Thunderclap headache mandates CT + LP to rule out SAH (CT ~95% sensitive within 6 hours). Canadian guidelines support CTA within 6 hours as potentially sufficient without LP.

**Q:** What is medication overuse headache and how is it managed?

**A:** MOH: acute medications >=10-15 days/month for >3 months (triptans/opioids >=10 days; simple analgesics >=15 days). Management: withdraw offending medication, bridge with naproxen or prednisone taper, start preventive.

MOH is the third most common headache disorder and a major cause of chronic daily headache. Withdrawal headache lasts 2-10 days for triptans, longer for opioids. Patient education on limiting acute medication is essential.

**Q:** How is subarachnoid hemorrhage diagnosed?

**A:** CT head (98% sensitive within 6h, drops to 50% by day 7). If CT negative: LP at >=6-12 hours for xanthochromia. CTA to identify aneurysm. Treatment: nimodipine 60mg q4h for 21 days; secure aneurysm within 24 hours.

Ottawa SAH Rule identifies patients needing investigation. Recent Canadian data suggest CTA within 6 hours may suffice without LP. Nimodipine prevents vasospasm. Aneurysm secured by clipping or coiling.

**Q:** What is the acute migraine treatment algorithm in the ED?

**A:** First-line: IV metoclopramide 10-20mg + diphenhydramine 25-50mg. Alternatives: ketorolac 30mg IV, prochlorperazine 10mg IV. Avoid opioids. Dexamethasone 10mg IV reduces 72-hour recurrence (NNT 9).

Canadian Headache Society and CAEP guidelines emphasize dopamine antagonists as ED first-line, not opioids. Dexamethasone single dose prevents recurrence. IV fluids recommended for dehydrated patients.

**Q:** What features distinguish tension-type headache from migraine?

**A:** TTH: bilateral, pressing/tightening, mild-moderate, NOT aggravated by activity, no nausea, may have photophobia OR phonophobia (not both). Duration 30 min to 7 days. Prophylaxis for chronic TTH: amitriptyline.

TTH is the most common primary headache but less disabling. Chronic TTH (>=15 days/month) should be distinguished from MOH and chronic migraine. Canadian guidelines recommend limiting analgesic use.

**Q:** What is the evaluation and treatment of idiopathic intracranial hypertension?

**A:** Overweight woman with daily headache, pulsatile tinnitus, visual obscurations, papilledema. Opening pressure >25 cmH2O, normal CSF, normal MRI. Treatment: weight loss, acetazolamide 500-1000mg BID. Surgery if vision threatened.

IIH requires urgent ophthalmologic assessment. Acetazolamide is primary therapy (IIHTT trial). Canadian guidelines recommend 5-10% weight loss. Topiramate is second-line (promotes weight loss + reduces CSF).

**Q:** What are the CGRP-targeted therapies for migraine?

**A:** CGRP mAbs: erenumab (anti-receptor), fremanezumab, galcanezumab (anti-ligand)-monthly/quarterly SC. Gepants (rimegepant, ubrogepant): acute treatment. Available in Canada after failing 2-3 conventional preventives.

CGRP therapies are migraine-specific with minimal side effects. Unlike triptans, they have no vasoconstrictive effects and are not contraindicated in cardiovascular disease. Canadian Headache Society supports use after conventional failures.

**Q:** When should CT head be ordered before lumbar puncture?

**A:** Focal neurological deficits, papilledema, altered consciousness, immunocompromised, new seizure, age >60

IDSA guidelines. CT before LP to rule out mass lesion/herniation risk. Do NOT delay antibiotics for LP/CT in suspected meningitis.

**Q:** A 35-year-old woman presents with the worst headache of her life, sudden onset reaching maximum intensity within seconds, and neck stiffness. What is the most critical diagnosis to rule out, and what is the diagnostic algorithm?

**A:** Subarachnoid hemorrhage (SAH). Algorithm: 1) Non-contrast CT head (sensitivity ~98% within 6 hours). 2) If CT negative and clinical suspicion remains, perform lumbar puncture looking for xanthochromia and elevated RBCs that do not clear with sequential tubes. 3) If LP positive, CT angiography to identify the aneurysm.

Thunderclap headache (maximal intensity within seconds) is SAH until proven otherwise. The Ottawa SAH Rule can help identify patients who need investigation. CT sensitivity decreases over time (98% at 6h, 93% at 12h, 50% at 1 week), making LP essential when CT is negative but suspicion is high.

**Q:** A 72-year-old woman presents with new-onset unilateral temporal headache, jaw claudication, and visual disturbances. ESR is 85 mm/hr. What is the diagnosis and the most urgent step?

**A:** Giant cell (temporal) arteritis. Start high-dose corticosteroids (prednisone 1 mg/kg/day) IMMEDIATELY — do not wait for temporal artery biopsy. Biopsy should be performed within 2 weeks (remains positive despite steroids). Untreated GCA can cause irreversible bilateral blindness from anterior ischemic optic neuropathy.

GCA should be suspected in any patient >50 with new headache, scalp tenderness, jaw claudication, visual symptoms, or unexplained elevated ESR/CRP. Polymyalgia rheumatica coexists in 40-60% of cases. Temporal artery biopsy is the gold standard but may show skip lesions causing false negatives.

**Q:** What are the red flag features in a headache presentation that suggest a secondary (dangerous) cause? Use the mnemonic SNOOP.

**A:** S — Systemic symptoms/signs (fever, weight loss, HIV, cancer). N — Neurological signs (focal deficits, papilledema, altered consciousness). O — Onset sudden (thunderclap). O — Older age of onset (>50, new headache). P — Pattern change (progressive worsening, positional, Valsalva-triggered, waking from sleep).

SNOOP flags identify headaches requiring urgent neuroimaging and further workup. Headaches worsening with Valsalva or recumbency suggest raised intracranial pressure. Morning headaches with papilledema and progressive neurological deficits raise concern for intracranial mass lesion.

**Q:** A 20-year-old presents with fever, severe headache, photophobia, and neck stiffness. Kernig's and Brudzinski's signs are positive. What is the diagnosis and the immediate management before LP results are available?

**A:** Bacterial meningitis until proven otherwise. Immediate empiric antibiotics (ceftriaxone + vancomycin; add ampicillin if >50 or immunocompromised for Listeria coverage) and dexamethasone BEFORE or WITH the first dose of antibiotics. Do not delay antibiotics for LP or CT.

In suspected bacterial meningitis, every hour of antibiotic delay increases mortality. CT before LP is only needed if there are signs of raised ICP, focal neurological deficits, immunocompromise, or altered consciousness. CSF findings in bacterial meningitis: high WBC (neutrophilic), high protein, low glucose, positive Gram stain/culture.

**Q:** What are the Ottawa SAH Rule criteria for investigating headache?

**A:** Investigate if ≥1 high-risk variable present in neurologically intact adults presenting to ED within 14 days of headache onset: (1) Age ≥40 yr; (2) Neck pain or stiffness; (3) Witnessed loss of consciousness; (4) Onset during exertion; (5) Thunderclap headache (instantly peaking pain); (6) Limited neck flexion on examination. Not for patients with new neurologic deficits, previous SAH, or known brain tumors.

The Ottawa SAH Rule has 100% sensitivity and 13.6% specificity. Non-contrast CT head is 100% sensitive for SAH within 6 hours of onset. If CT is normal and SAH is still suspected after 6 hours, LP is performed to look for xanthochromia (yellow CSF from hemoglobin breakdown).

**Q:** What is the investigation of choice for SAH and what is done if it is negative?

**A:** Non-contrast CT head — 100% sensitive within 6 hours of symptom onset (hyperattenuating signal around Circle of Willis). If CT is negative and SAH is still suspected after 6 hours: lumbar puncture (LP) to look for xanthochromia. Management: urgent neurosurgery consult.

Sensitivity of CT for SAH decreases with time (100% at 6h, ~93% at 24h, less thereafter). Xanthochromia (yellow discoloration of CSF due to breakdown of hemoglobin) develops 2–4 hours after hemorrhage and persists for up to 2 weeks. CT angiogram or MR angiography is used to identify the underlying aneurysm.

**Q:** What are the treatment options for an acute migraine in the ED?

**A:** First-line: NSAIDs, acetaminophen, Aspirin (simple analgesics), antiemetics (e.g., metoclopramide, prochlorperazine), triptans. ED-specific: ketorolac 30 mg IV, ergotamine, sumatriptan, other analgesics. Consider parenteral dexamethasone to prevent headache recurrence within 72 hours.

Antiemetics (especially dopamine antagonists like prochlorperazine) have intrinsic antimigraine properties in addition to treating nausea. Dexamethasone has been shown to reduce migraine recurrence within 72 hours when added to standard abortive therapy (NNT ~9). Opioids are generally avoided due to risk of medication overuse headache and limited efficacy.

## General

**Q:** What are the neurological criteria for death in Canada?

**A:** Brain death criteria must be established before organ donation considerations

Neurological death determination includes absence of brainstem reflexes and apnea testing, with organ donation discussed afterward.

**Q:** What neurologic complications are associated with alcohol use disorder?

**A:** Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome, peripheral neuropathy

These result from thiamine (B1) deficiency commonly seen in chronic alcohol use.

**Q:** What neurological complications are associated with alcohol use disorder?

**A:** Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome and peripheral neuropathy

Thiamine deficiency causes these serious neurological manifestations of chronic alcohol use.

**Q:** When is neuroimaging indicated for headache evaluation?

**A:** When red flags are present to rule out intracranial pathology

CT or MRI is not routinely indicated for primary headaches but should be performed when secondary causes are suspected.

**Q:** What are secondary headaches?

**A:** Headaches caused by underlying organic disease; account for <10% of headaches but may be life-threatening

Secondary headaches require urgent evaluation to identify the underlying cause.

**Q:** What are the 3 main categories of headaches?

**A:** Primary headaches (most common), secondary headaches (caused by underlying organic disease), and other serious causes

Primary headaches include tension headaches, migraines, and cluster headaches. Secondary headaches account for <10% of headaches and may be life-threatening.

**Q:** What percentage of all headaches are secondary headaches?

**A:** Less than 10%

Secondary headaches caused by underlying organic disease account for less than 10% of all headaches but may be life-threatening.

**Q:** What are the neurological causes of constipation?

**A:** Intestinal pseudo-obstruction, Parkinson's disease, and multiple sclerosis.

Neurological diseases can affect the autonomic innervation of the gut, leading to impaired motility and constipation.

**Q:** What is the preferred treatment for Wilson's disease with neurological involvement?

**A:** Tetrathiomolybdate

Tetrathiomolybdate is preferred in neurological Wilson's disease as it preferentially treats neurological symptoms and has better tolerability.

**Q:** Which drug is preferred for Wilson's disease with neurological involvement?

**A:** Tetrathiomolybdate

Tetrathiomolybdate is preferred in neurological presentations as it may prevent worsening of neurological symptoms that can occur with chelators.

## Neuro Sz

**Q:** Define seizure and status epilepticus

**A:** Seizure: paroxysmal alteration of behaviour and/or EEG changes from abnormal, excessive neuronal activity. Status epilepticus: continuous or intermittent seizure activity for >5 min without regaining consciousness

Status epilepticus is life-threatening and requires immediate intervention

**Q:** What is the loading dose of levetiracetam for status epilepticus?

**A:** Loading dose of 60 mg/kg IV in adults, maximum 4500 mg, infused over 5-15 min

Levetiracetam is used after seizure resolves to prevent recurrence

**Q:** What is the definition of status epilepticus?

**A:** Continuous or intermittent seizure activity for greater than 5 minutes without regaining consciousness

Status epilepticus is a life-threatening emergency requiring immediate intervention to prevent permanent neurological damage.

**Q:** What investigations should be ordered urgently in status epilepticus?

**A:** CBC, electrolytes, calcium, magnesium, venous blood gas, serum blood glucose, toxins, antiepileptic drug levels, pregnancy test (β-hCG)

These tests help identify underlying metabolic, toxic, or infectious causes of seizures.

**Q:** After a seizure resolves, what antiepileptic drug is recommended to prevent recurrence?

**A:** Levetiracetam: loading dose of 60mg/kg IV in adults, maximum 4500mg, infused over 5-15 min

Levetiracetam is preferred due to its favorable side effect profile and broad-spectrum efficacy.

**Q:** What is the urgent management for status epilepticus if seizures persist after initial benzodiazepine therapy?

**A:** Establish second IV line. If status persists, give phenytoin 20mg/kg IV at 25-50 mg/min in adults; may give additional 10mg/kg IV 10 min after loading infusion. If seizure resolves, start antiepileptic drug (e.g., levetiracetam loading dose 60mg/kg IV, max 4500mg).

Phenytoin requires cardiac monitoring due to risk of dysrhythmias. Loading doses achieve therapeutic levels rapidly.

**Q:** What are the key differences between DKA and HHS clinical features?

**A:** HHS occurs in older T2DM patients with more comorbid illness, larger fluid losses, symptoms evolving over days to weeks, fewer GI symptoms, and more neurological deficits including coma, delirium, and seizures

Unlike DKA, HHS typically presents with predominant neurological manifestations due to extreme hyperosmolarity, and lacks the GI symptoms and metabolic acidosis seen in DKA.

**Q:** What are the key clinical features distinguishing a seizure from syncope?

**A:** Seizure: rapid onset, brief duration, tongue biting (high specificity), loss of bladder/bowel control, emesis, postictal period; Syncope: slower onset, brief loss of tone, usually no tongue biting or postictal confusion

Differentiation is critical as management differs significantly between these conditions.

**Q:** What is the loading dose of levetiracetam after seizure resolves in status epilepticus?

**A:** 60 mg/kg IV (maximum 4500 mg), infused over 5-15 minutes

Levetiracetam is used to prevent seizure recurrence after acute control is achieved.

**Q:** What neurological deficits are more common in HHS compared to DKA?

**A:** Mental disturbances, coma, delirium, and seizures are more common in HHS.

HHS occurs in older T2DM patients with more severe hyperosmolarity leading to neurological complications.

**Q:** When can a patient with a seizure typically be discharged from the emergency department?

**A:** If the patient had a brief generalized seizure, has returned to baseline function, and is neurologically intact

Most first-time seizures with complete recovery and normal investigation can be managed as outpatient with neurologist follow-up.

**Q:** What investigations should be sent urgently in a patient with suspected status epilepticus?

**A:** CBC, electrolytes, calcium, magnesium, VBG, serum glucose, toxins screen, and antiepileptic drug levels; also send p-hCG in women of childbearing age

These tests help identify reversible causes and guide ongoing management in the emergent setting.

**Q:** What is the loading dose of levetiracetam for seizure termination in status epilepticus?

**A:** Loading dose of 60 mg/kg IV in adults, maximum 4500 mg, infused over 5-15 minutes

Levetiracetam is increasingly used as first-line adjunct therapy after benzodiazepines due to favorable side effect profile.

**Q:** What vasopressor support is needed in status epilepticus if hypotension persists despite fluids?

**A:** Vasopressor support if sBP <90 mmHg or MAP <70 mmHg after aggressive fluid resuscitation

Maintaining cerebral perfusion pressure is crucial in status epilepticus to prevent secondary brain injury.

**Q:** What are the key clinical features of heat exhaustion?

**A:** Non-specific malaise, nausea, headache, fatigue, dehydration (tachycardia, orthostatic hypotension), body temperature <40.5°C, no coma or seizures

Heat exhaustion results from loss of circulating volume due to heat stress and is characterized by volume depletion symptoms without altered mental status.

**Q:** What are the key neurological features of HHS compared to DKA?

**A:** Mental disturbances, coma, delirium, seizures

HHS has more neurological deficits than DKA due to extreme hyperosmolarity, including altered mental status and seizures.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of heat exhaustion?

**A:** Non-specific malaise, nausea, headache, fatigue, body temperature <40.5°C (usually normal), no coma or seizures, dehydration with orthostatic hypotension

Heat exhaustion results from loss of circulating volume due to heat stress. It may progress to heat stroke if untreated.

**Q:** What are the key clinical features of HHS compared to DKA?

**A:** HHS typically occurs in older T2DM patients with more comorbidities. Symptoms evolve over days to weeks. Fewer GI symptoms but MORE neurological deficits including mental disturbances, coma, delirium, and seizures.

The neurological symptoms in HHS are due to extreme hyperosmolarity, distinguishing it from DKA.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to bupropion use?

**A:** Seizure disorder, eating disorder, coma, simultaneous use of MAO inhibitors

Bupropion lowers the seizure threshold and is contraindicated in patients with seizure disorders, eating disorders, and those on MAOIs.

**Q:** What percentage of patients in alcohol withdrawal will have seizures or delirium tremens?

**A:** 10%

Alcohol withdrawal can be life-threatening; approximately 10% experience seizures or delirium tremens.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to bupropion?

**A:** Seizure disorder, coma, eating disorder, MAOI use in past 14 days

Bupropion lowers seizure threshold and is contraindicated in conditions predisposing to seizures.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to bupropion use for smoking cessation?

**A:** Seizure disorder, eating disorder, MAOI use within past 14 days

Bupropion lowers seizure threshold and is contraindicated in conditions predisposing to seizures.

**Q:** What are the side effects of bupropion?

**A:** Headache, insomnia, nightmares, seizures

Bupropion can cause CNS stimulation (insomnia, nightmares), headache, and importantly - seizures.

**Q:** What are the side effects and risks of bupropion?

**A:** Headache, insomnia, nightmares, seizures, less sexual dysfunction

Seizure risk is increased at higher doses; contraindicated in patients with seizure disorders.

**Q:** What is a serious side effect of bupropion that requires caution?

**A:** Seizures

Bupropion can cause seizures, particularly at higher doses or in susceptible individuals.

## Neurology

**Q:** What is the TRAP mnemonic for Parkinson's disease?

**A:** T: Tremor (resting, 4-6 Hz pill-rolling). R: Rigidity (cogwheel). A: Akinesia/bradykinesia. P: Postural instability (late finding).

PD diagnosis requires bradykinesia plus either resting tremor or rigidity, with asymmetric onset and progressive course. Motor symptoms emerge when >70% of dopaminergic neurons in substantia nigra pars compacta are lost. Non-motor features include anosmia, REM sleep behavior disorder, constipation, and depression.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of Parkinson's disease?

**A:** Alpha-synuclein accumulates as Lewy bodies causing neuronal loss, especially in substantia nigra pars compacta → decreased dopamine in striatum → disinhibited indirect pathway and decreased direct pathway activation → increased inhibition of cortical motor areas.

Alpha-synuclein pathology progresses from medulla/olfactory bulbs (early: constipation, anosmia) to pons (REM sleep disorder), midbrain/substantia nigra (motor symptoms), and cortex (cognitive impairment, hallucinations). This explains the sequence of prodromal to advanced features.

**Q:** What are the major adverse effects of levodopa therapy in Parkinson's disease?

**A:** Dyskinesia (most important), wearing-off phenomenon (end-of-dose deterioration), on-off fluctuations, and delayed onset of response affected by meals.

Levodopa is combined with carbidopa or benserazide (peripheral decarboxylase inhibitors) to decrease peripheral metabolism and side effects. Dopamine agonists have fewer motor side effects but worse symptom control and more non-motor side effects (hallucinations, dizziness).

**Q:** What are the clinical features that distinguish progressive supranuclear palsy (PSP) from Parkinson's disease?

**A:** PSP: vertical gaze palsy (especially downgaze), early postural instability with frequent falls, axial rigidity, and little or no response to levodopa.

PSP is a tauopathy (unlike PD which is a synucleinopathy). The vertical gaze limitation can be overcome by the oculocephalic reflex, indicating a supranuclear rather than nuclear cause. Early falls and poor levodopa response are red flags against typical PD.

**Q:** What is Huntington's disease and what are its genetics?

**A:** Autosomal dominant CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion in HTT gene on chromosome 4. ≥40 repeats = full penetrance. Shows genetic anticipation (repeats increase with subsequent generations).

HD presents with insidious chorea, cognitive decline, and psychiatric symptoms (mean onset 35-44 years). MRI shows caudate atrophy and ventricular enlargement. Treatment is symptomatic only: tetrabenazine for chorea, antidepressants and antipsychotics for psychiatric symptoms.

**Q:** How do you differentiate essential tremor from Parkinson's disease tremor?

**A:** Essential tremor: action-postural (6-12 Hz), worse with sustained posture, upper limbs > head, autosomal dominant FHx, improved by alcohol. PD tremor: resting (3-6 Hz), pill-rolling, upper limb > jaw, asymmetric, not improved by alcohol.

Essential tremor is the most common movement disorder and usually does not require treatment. When treatment is needed, propranolol and primidone are first-line. In young patients with tremor, TSH and ceruloplasmin should be checked to rule out thyroid disease and Wilson's disease.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of cerebellar dysfunction?

**A:** Ipsilateral limb ataxia (dysmetria, intention tremor, dysdiadochokinesia), truncal ataxia (wide-based gait), nystagmus, scanning speech, and hypotonia with pendular reflexes.

Lateral cerebellar hemisphere lesions cause limb ataxia, while vermis lesions cause truncal ataxia. Cerebellar signs are always ipsilateral to the lesion. Friedreich's ataxia is the most common hereditary ataxia (autosomal recessive, onset 8-15 years).

**Q:** What is ALS and what are its characteristic upper and lower motor neuron findings?

**A:** Progressive neurodegenerative disease with UMN (spasticity, hyperreflexia, bulbar dysarthria) and LMN (weakness, muscle atrophy, fasciculations, muscle cramps) symptoms simultaneously.

ALS affects anterior horn cells, cranial nerve motor nuclei, and corticospinal tracts. Median survival is 3 years, usually due to respiratory failure. Riluzole prolongs survival; edaravone slows functional decline. BiPAP is the other intervention proven to improve survival.

**Q:** What features on exam help distinguish an ALS diagnosis from mimics?

**A:** ALS: combined UMN and LMN signs, no sensory loss, no bowel/bladder incontinence, no extraocular muscle weakness. These absent features help distinguish ALS from other conditions.

EMG shows active denervation (fibrillations, positive sharp waves) and chronic reinnervation (increased amplitude motor units) in multiple regions. MRI brain and spine are done to exclude alternatives. Sensory symptoms, predominant pain, and bowel/bladder dysfunction are red flags against ALS.

**Q:** What is carpal tunnel syndrome and how is it diagnosed?

**A:** Compression of median nerve at the wrist. Symptoms: paresthesias in thumb, index, middle, and lateral ring finger; decreased grip strength. Signs: positive Tinel's and Phalen's tests, thenar atrophy. Diagnosis: clinical + EMG/NCS.

Risk factors include pregnancy, diabetes, rheumatoid arthritis, acromegaly, and hypothyroidism. Tinel's sign is elicited by tapping over the median nerve at the wrist. Phalen's test involves holding wrists in forced flexion for 30-60 seconds.

**Q:** What are the key features of Guillain-Barré syndrome?

**A:** Acute ascending demyelinating polyradiculoneuropathy with distal-to-proximal weakness, areflexia, and autonomic dysfunction. CSF shows albuminocytologic dissociation (high protein, normal WBC).

GBS is a neurological emergency due to risk of respiratory failure. It usually follows a viral/bacterial infection. EMG/NCS shows conduction block and slowing. Treatment is IVIG or plasmapheresis. Miller-Fischer variant presents with ophthalmoplegia, ataxia, and areflexia.

**Q:** What differentiates myasthenia gravis from Lambert-Eaton syndrome?

**A:** MG: autoantibodies to acetylcholine receptors, decremental response on repetitive stimulation, associated with thymoma, ocular/bulbar predominant. LEMS: antibodies to presynaptic voltage-gated calcium channels, incremental response, associated with small cell lung cancer, proximal limb predominant with autonomic symptoms.

MG shows fatigable weakness that worsens with activity and improves with rest. LEMS shows post-exercise enhancement (reflexes increase after active contraction). Both are autoimmune disorders of the neuromuscular junction but target different components.

**Q:** What is the treatment approach for myasthenia gravis?

**A:** 1st line: acetylcholinesterase inhibitors (pyridostigmine). 2nd: corticosteroids. Crisis: IVIG or plasmapheresis. Long-term: azathioprine, mycophenolate. Thymectomy for non-thymomatous AChR antibody-positive generalized MG.

Newer therapies include eculizumab (complement inhibitor) and efgartigimod (neonatal Fc receptor blocker). Per the thymectomy trial, surgery improved clinical outcomes over 3 years with lower prednisone requirements. 15% of MG patients have thymoma.

**Q:** What is the classic presentation of botulism?

**A:** Descending paralysis starting with cranial neuropathies (ptosis, extraocular weakness, dilated pupils, dysarthria, dysphagia) → symmetric descending weakness → autonomic dysfunction. Onset 6-48 hours after ingestion.

Botulinum toxin blocks presynaptic acetylcholine release. Infantile botulism (most common form) is from ingestion of honey or corn syrup. Treatment is botulinum antitoxin plus supportive care. Unlike GBS which is ascending, botulism descends.

**Q:** What is myotonic dystrophy type 1 and what are its systemic features?

**A:** Autosomal dominant CTG repeat expansion at 19q13.3. Features: distal weakness, myotonia (delayed muscle relaxation), cardiac conduction defects (90%), subcapsular cataracts, frontal baldness, diabetes, testicular atrophy.

This is the most common adult muscular dystrophy. EMG shows electrical myotonia ('dive-bomber' sound). Unlike other myopathies, weakness is distal > proximal. Genetic anticipation occurs. No cure exists; progressive with death usually around age 50.

**Q:** What is Tourette's syndrome and how is it defined?

**A:** Multiple motor and ≥1 vocal tics persisting >1 year, onset <18 years, not due to substance or medical condition. Tics wax and wane, can be suppressed temporarily, and worsen with stress.

Prevalence is 3-8/1000 school-age children (M:F 2-4:1). Peak severity at ages 10-12 with decline in adolescence (50% tic-free by age 18). Commonly associated with OCD and ADHD. Treatment: mild tics with behavioral therapy; severe tics with alpha-2 agonists (guanfacine) or antipsychotics.

**Q:** What does an axonal vs. demyelinating pattern on EMG/NCS indicate?

**A:** Axonal neuropathy: decreased amplitude on NCS (fewer functioning axons). Demyelinating neuropathy: decreased conduction velocity on NCS (slower signal transmission through damaged myelin).

This distinction is crucial for differential diagnosis. Demyelinating neuropathies include GBS, CIDP, and Charcot-Marie-Tooth. Axonal neuropathies include DM, alcohol, renal failure, and toxins. GBS shows conduction block, decreased F-wave, and sural sparing on NCS.

**Q:** What is Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome and what causes it?

**A:** Wernicke's encephalopathy (acute): confusion, ataxia, ophthalmoplegia — from thiamine (B1) deficiency. Korsakoff's psychosis (chronic): anterograde amnesia, confabulation. Most common cause: alcohol use disorder.

Other causes include malabsorption, gastric bypass surgery, malnutrition, and hemodialysis. Thiamine should be given BEFORE glucose in malnourished patients to prevent precipitating Wernicke's. Alcohol can also cause cerebellar ataxia separate from thiamine deficiency.

**Q:** How do you differentiate peripheral from central vertigo based on associated symptoms?

**A:** Peripheral: hearing loss, tinnitus, nausea/vomiting, worsened by head movement; no neurologic deficits. Central: diplopia, dysphagia, dysarthria, ataxia, altered consciousness, motor/sensory deficits.

Peripheral vertigo involves the inner ear or vestibular nerve and often has audiological symptoms. Central vertigo involves brainstem or cerebellar lesions and presents with neurological deficits requiring urgent workup.

**Q:** What are the four types of dizziness and how do you differentiate vestibular from non-vestibular causes?

**A:** Vertigo, pre-syncope, disequilibrium, and lightheadedness. Vestibular causes worsen with head movement or eye closure; non-vestibular causes show no change.

This distinction is critical for guiding workup. Neurologic symptoms (diplopia, dysarthria, ataxia) suggest central causes. Audiologic symptoms (hearing loss, tinnitus) suggest peripheral vestibular disorders.

**Q:** What are the headache red flags that require urgent investigation (mnemonic SNOOP)?

**A:** Systemic symptoms/illness/fever, Neurologic signs (focal deficits, altered mental status), Onset sudden/severe, Older age (new after 50), Prior headache history with different pattern. Also: awakens from sleep, jaw claudication, trauma.

Secondary headaches account for <10% of all headaches but may be life-threatening. Investigations may include CBC, ESR/CRP, neuroimaging (CT/MRI), and CSF analysis depending on clinical suspicion.

**Q:** What are the key features of a CORTICAL lesion?

**A:** Contralateral hemiparesis (differential face/arm vs. leg), contralateral hemisensory loss, cortical sensory loss (graphesthesia, stereognosis), homonymous hemianopsia/quadrantanopsia, gaze deviation (frontal eye field), seizure, agnosia, and dominant hemisphere: aphasia/alexia/agraphia/acalculia/left-right disorientation; non-dominant hemisphere: hemineglect, dysprosody, constructional apraxia, alien hand syndrome.

Cortical lesions produce a rich constellation of signs due to the dense concentration of function. Dominant hemisphere lesions (usually left) impair language-related functions, while non-dominant (usually right) cause spatial and attentional deficits. This localization is essential for MCCQE1 neurological examination questions.

**Q:** How does an INTERNAL CAPSULE lesion differ from a CORTICAL lesion in terms of hemiparesis?

**A:** Internal capsule lesion: contralateral hemiparesis with EQUAL face, arm, and leg involvement WITHOUT cortical/sensory deficits (pure motor). Cortical lesion: hemiparesis with DIFFERENTIAL involvement of face/arm vs. leg, AND may have sensory/cortical signs (aphasia, neglect, etc.).

The internal capsule is a compact white matter tract where corticospinal and corticobulbar fibers run tightly together, causing equal motor loss in all regions. The cortex has a wider somatotopic spread, so lesions produce differential weakness. Distinguishing these is a high-yield MCCQE1 localization concept.

**Q:** What are the four internal capsule stroke syndromes?

**A:** 1. Pure motor hemiparesis (contralateral face, arm, leg equally). 2. Sensorimotor stroke (contralateral hemiparesis + sensory deficit). 3. Ataxic hemiparesis (contralateral dysmetria/clumsiness + paresis). 4. Clumsy hand-dysarthria syndrome (dysarthria + ataxia of hand).

These lacunar syndromes result from small vessel disease affecting the internal capsule or pons. They are classic MCCQE1 vignette presentations for lacunar infarcts in hypertensive patients. Ataxic hemiparesis and clumsy hand-dysarthria are especially high-yield.

**Q:** What is the hallmark pattern of a BRAINSTEM lesion?

**A:** CROSSED findings: ipsilateral cranial nerve deficits (face) + contralateral hemiplegia/hemisensory loss (body). Also: ipsilateral limb ataxia, nystagmus, diplopia, ptosis, pupil abnormalities, dysphagia, dysarthria, hearing loss, vertigo, ipsilateral Horner's syndrome.

Crossed deficits (ipsilateral face, contralateral body) are pathognomonic for brainstem localization. This pattern occurs because cranial nerve nuclei are ipsilateral to their effect, while corticospinal and sensory tracts have already crossed or haven't yet crossed at certain brainstem levels.

**Q:** What are the features of a CEREBELLAR lesion, and on which side do they occur relative to the lesion?

**A:** Symptoms are IPSILATERAL to the side of the lesion. Features: limb ataxia (dysmetria, intention tremor, dysdiadochokinesia, hypotonia with pendular reflexes), truncal ataxia (wide-based gait, inability to tandem walk, truncal titubation), nystagmus, hypermetric/hypometric saccades, impaired smooth pursuit, scanning speech (explosive with pauses).

Cerebellar pathways are double-crossed: they cross to the contralateral cortex and back, so the net effect is ipsilateral. This contrasts with cortical/subcortical lesions causing contralateral deficits. Scanning (cerebellar) speech is a distinct dysarthria pattern tested on MCCQE1.

**Q:** What is the significance of a 'sensory level' and 'saddle anesthesia' in spinal cord localization?

**A:** Sensory level: a dermatomal line below which there is sensory loss, indicating the level of a spinal cord lesion. Suspended 'cape-like' sensory level occurs in central cord lesions. Saddle anesthesia (perineum, inner thighs) with preserved lower limb sensation indicates involvement of the conus medullaris (S3-S5 segments).

A sensory level is a hallmark of spinal cord pathology (e.g., transverse myelitis, cord compression). The suspended cape-like pattern of central cord syndrome spares deep sensation. Saddle anesthesia localizes to the conus, whereas cauda equina lesions also cause saddle anesthesia but with LMN signs.

**Q:** How do ANTERIOR HORN CELL lesions differ from PERIPHERAL NERVE lesions clinically?

**A:** Anterior horn cell: weakness WITHOUT sensory symptoms, plus fasciculations, atrophy, cramps. Peripheral nerve: weakness OR sensory loss respecting the distribution of a SPECIFIC nerve (median, ulnar, radial), or length-dependent stocking-glove pattern for polyneuropathy. Anterior horn cell examples: ALS, spinal muscular atrophy, polio.

Anterior horn cells house lower motor neurons but receive no sensory input, so their lesions are purely motor with LMN features. Peripheral nerve lesions involve mixed fibers, causing both motor AND sensory deficits in a nerve distribution. This distinction is key for EMG/NCS interpretation on MCCQE1.

**Q:** What are the distinguishing features of a NEUROMUSCULAR JUNCTION (NMJ) lesion?

**A:** Fluctuating/FATIGABLE symptoms, facial and limb weakness, bulbar dysfunction (dysarthria, dysphonia, dysphagia), ocular involvement (diplopia, ptosis), respiratory distress. Reflexes usually preserved unless severe or in LEMS. No sensory deficits. Examples: myasthenia gravis, LEMS, botulism.

The key differentiator for NMJ disorders is fatigability — weakness worsens with repetitive activity (MG) or improves with repetition (LEMS). Preserved reflexes (unless LEMS) and absence of sensory symptoms help exclude neuropathy. MCCQE1 frequently tests MG vs. LEMS distinction.

**Q:** How do MYOPATHY lesions present, and what are the exceptions to the usual proximal weakness pattern?

**A:** Usually: symmetric PROXIMAL weakness (difficulty climbing stairs, rising from chair) without sensory deficits. Exceptions with distal/asymmetric weakness: distal myopathies, inclusion body myositis, some glycogen storage diseases, facioscapulohumeral dystrophy. Also: myalgia, muscle atrophy.

Proximal symmetric weakness without sensory loss is the classic myopathy pattern. Inclusion body myositis is notable for asymmetric weakness with both proximal and distal involvement, and facioscapulohumeral dystrophy has a distinctive facial + shoulder-girdle pattern. Creatine kinase is elevated in myopathies (except late stage).

**Q:** Compare LMN, UMN, and extrapyramidal lesions across: tone, atrophy, reflexes, plantar response, and involuntary movements.

**A:** LMN: hypotonia, significant denervation atrophy, hyporeflexia/areflexia, flexor plantar, fasciculations. UMN: spasticity (velocity-dependent hypertonia), disuse atrophy over time, hyperreflexia, extensor plantar (Babinski), no involuntary movements. Extrapyramidal: rigidity (non-velocity-dependent hypertonia), variable atrophy, variable reflexes, flexor plantar, tremor/chorea/hemiballism/myoclonus.

This table is foundational for MCCQE1 neurology. Key distinctions: spasticity is velocity-dependent (catches then releases = clasp-knife), rigidity is not velocity-dependent (lead-pipe or cogwheel). Babinski sign is the most reliable UMN sign. Fasciculations indicate active denervation (LMN).

**Q:** What are the components of the Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS), and what is the maximum/minimum score?

**A:** GCS = Eye opening (E/4) + Verbal response (V/5) + Motor response (M/6). Total: 3-15. Eye: 1=none, 2=to pain, 3=to voice, 4=spontaneous. Verbal: 1=none, 2=incomprehensible sounds, 3=inappropriate words, 4=confused, 5=oriented. Motor: 1=none, 2=extension to pain (decerebrate), 3=flexion to pain (decorticate), 4=withdraws, 5=localizes, 6=obeys commands.

GCS is universally used to assess level of consciousness and severity of brain injury. Score ≤8 generally indicates coma requiring airway protection. Minimum score is 3 (not 0). GCS is a key component of trauma assessment and is tested on the MCCQE1 for both interpretation and clinical decision-making.

**Q:** What are the MMSE and MoCA cutoffs for normal cognitive function, and what are the MMSE ranges for mild, moderate, and severe impairment?

**A:** MMSE: scored /30. Normal >24; mild impairment 19-23; moderate impairment 10-18; severe impairment <10. MoCA: scored /30. Normal ≥26.

The MMSE and MoCA are standard screening tools for cognitive impairment in Canadian clinical practice. MoCA is more sensitive for mild cognitive impairment. Both are tested on MCCQE1 in the context of dementia evaluation, delirium workup, and fitness-to-drive assessments.

**Q:** What is a Relative Afferent Pupillary Defect (RAPD), and how is it detected?

**A:** RAPD (Marcus Gunn pupil) = relative reduction in afferent pupillary light response in one eye due to optic nerve or extensive retinal disease. Detected by SWINGING FLASHLIGHT TEST: alternating light between eyes; the affected eye shows paradoxical dilation (both pupils dilate when light moves to that eye) while the normal eye shows constriction.

RAPD indicates a unilateral defect in the afferent arc of the pupillary light reflex (CN II). Common causes: optic neuritis (MS), optic nerve compression, severe retinal disease. It is absent if the lesion is bilateral or posterior to the optic chiasm. RAPD is a key CN II exam finding on MCCQE1.

**Q:** What does CN III (oculomotor) palsy look like clinically, and what are the key causes?

**A:** Eye position 'DOWN AND OUT,' ptosis, mydriasis (fixed dilated pupil), oblique binocular diplopia. Pupil-involving CN III palsy = posterior communicating artery aneurysm (surgical emergency). Pupil-sparing CN III palsy = microvascular (diabetes, hypertension).

CN III innervates the superior rectus, inferior rectus, medial rectus, inferior oblique (extraocular muscles), levator palpebrae (eyelid), and parasympathetic pupilloconstrictor. External compression (PComm aneurysm) affects the outer parasympathetic fibers first (mydriasis), while ischemic causes spare them. This distinction is critical on MCCQE1.

**Q:** What is the difference between LMN and UMN CN VII (facial nerve) palsies?

**A:** LMN (Bell's palsy): ipsilateral complete facial weakness including FOREHEAD (upper and lower face affected). UMN (e.g., stroke): contralateral lower facial weakness with FOREHEAD SPARING (upper face has bilateral cortical representation). LMN also may cause decreased lacrimation, salivation, hyperacusis, loss of anterior 2/3 tongue taste.

The forehead-sparing pattern in UMN lesions occurs because the upper facial motor neurons receive bilateral cortical input, so a unilateral cortical/subcortical lesion only partially affects them. Complete ipsilateral facial palsy including forehead always indicates LMN pathology. This is one of the most high-yield MCCQE1 facial nerve questions.

**Q:** What does CN IV (trochlear nerve) palsy cause, and what is the compensatory head position?

**A:** CN IV palsy causes vertical binocular diplopia and hypertropia (affected eye sits higher). The superior oblique performs INtorsion, DEpression, and ABduction. Compensatory head TILT to the contralateral side (tilting away from the affected eye reduces diplopia by compensating for the lost intorsion).

CN IV has the longest intracranial course and is the most vulnerable CN to closed head trauma. The 3H test (Hypertropia, worsened with Head tilt to ipsilateral side, worsened on contralateral Horizontal gaze) confirms CN IV palsy. Contralateral head tilt is the classic compensatory posture.

**Q:** What does CN VI (abducens nerve) palsy cause?

**A:** Horizontal binocular diplopia (worse on ipsilateral gaze), abduction deficit of the affected eye, and esotropia (eye deviated medially at rest). CN VI has the longest intracranial course before it exits the brainstem and is therefore vulnerable to raised ICP (false localizing sign) and trauma.

CN VI palsy is the most common isolated ocular motor nerve palsy. It causes a lateral rectus palsy — the affected eye cannot abduct. Bilateral CN VI palsy after LP is a recognized complication of post-LP CSF hypotension. Raised ICP causing bilateral CN VI palsy is a 'false localizing sign' (not indicating a pontomedullary lesion).

**Q:** Describe the extraocular muscles and their cranial nerve innervations.

**A:** CN III (oculomotor): superior rectus (elevation), inferior rectus (depression), medial rectus (adduction), inferior oblique (elevation in adduction, extorsion). CN IV (trochlear): superior oblique (depression in adduction, intorsion). CN VI (abducens): lateral rectus (abduction). Mnemonic: SO4, LR6, all others CN3.

Knowing which muscle each CN innervates allows precise localization of diplopia. The superior oblique (CN IV) is tested with the eye in adduction — asking the patient to look down and in isolates it. The lateral rectus (CN VI) is tested by asking the patient to look directly laterally.

**Q:** What are the caloric test responses and what do they indicate?

**A:** Cold water irrigation → nystagmus AWAY (towards contralateral ear, fast phase away from irrigated ear). Warm water → nystagmus TOWARDS (fast phase towards irrigated ear). Mnemonic: COWS = Cold Opposite, Warm Same. Tests vestibular (CN VIII) function and brainstem integrity.

The caloric test evaluates the vestibuloocular reflex. Absent responses indicate ipsilateral brainstem or CN VIII pathology. In comatose patients, absent response to cold caloric suggests severe brainstem dysfunction. The fast phase of nystagmus is the direction conventionally used to describe nystagmus direction.

**Q:** What is the CN XI (accessory nerve) function, and what are the clinical deficits when it is damaged?

**A:** CN XI innervates the sternocleidomastoid (SCM) and trapezius. Deficits: ipsilateral shoulder shrug weakness (trapezius) and contralateral head turn weakness. Note: the left SCM turns the head RIGHT (contracts to rotate head contralaterally). Damage causes inability to turn head away from the side of the lesion.

CN XI is commonly injured by neck dissection surgery, radiation, or trauma. The SCM contraction rotates the head to the opposite side — so a right SCM lesion causes weakness turning the head LEFT. Trapezius weakness leads to shoulder drooping and difficulty elevating the arm above 90 degrees.

**Q:** What does CN XII (hypoglossal nerve) palsy cause, and which way does the tongue deviate?

**A:** Tongue DEVIATES toward the WEAK side on protrusion. Also: atrophy and fasciculations of the tongue on the affected side (LMN lesion). UMN lesion: contralateral deviation (tongue deviates away from the lesion side). LMN lesion: ipsilateral deviation with atrophy/fasciculations.

The tongue deviates toward the weak side because the normal side pushes harder. LMN CN XII palsy (e.g., skull base tumour, neck mass) produces ipsilateral atrophy + deviation. UMN CN XII palsy (e.g., stroke) causes contralateral deviation without atrophy. Testing through the cheek assesses tongue strength.

**Q:** What does CN X (vagus nerve) damage cause?

**A:** Oropharyngeal dysphagia, hoarseness/dysarthria (vocal cord paralysis), soft palate drooping on the affected side, uvula deviates to the CONTRALATERAL (normal) side, absent gag reflex (efferent limb). Unilateral vagal palsy: uvula pulled toward intact side.

CN X damage can result from brainstem lesions (Wallenberg syndrome), skull base pathology, or neck tumors. The uvula deviates away from the weak side — toward the functioning muscles. The vagus also provides parasympathetic innervation to thoracic and abdominal viscera. CN IX provides the afferent limb of the gag reflex.

**Q:** What are the indications and key contraindications for lumbar puncture (LP)?

**A:** Indications — Diagnostic: CNS infection (meningitis, encephalitis), SAH (if CT head negative), idiopathic intracranial hypertension (IIH), NPH, MS, GBS, CNS vasculitis. Therapeutic: decrease ICP, intrathecal drugs (anesthesia, chemotherapy, antibiotics). Imaging: intrathecal contrast for myelography. Contraindications: increased ICP with mass/edema/obstructive hydrocephalus (risk herniation — CT head first), skin infection/epidural abscess at insertion site, severe thrombocytopenia (<50×10⁹/L), high INR (>1.4), coagulopathy.

LP is performed at L3-L4 or L4-L5, below the conus medullaris (L1-L2). In suspected bacterial meningitis, do NOT delay antibiotics to wait for CT or LP — give antibiotics immediately if LP is delayed. A normal CT does not completely exclude elevated ICP, but it is required to rule out obstructive causes before LP.

**Q:** What are the features and management of post-LP headache?

**A:** Onset within 24-48 hours of LP, worse when upright (orthostatic), better when supine (positional). Prevention: smaller gauge needle (22G), reinserting stylet before removal, blunt-ended (atraumatic) needle. Treatment — symptomatic: oral analgesics, antiemetics, IV caffeine/sodium benzoate. Definitive: epidural blood patch.

Post-LP headache (5-40% incidence) results from CSF leakage through the dural puncture site, causing intracranial hypotension. The hallmark orthostatic pattern distinguishes it from other headaches. Epidural blood patch (autologous blood injected into epidural space to seal the leak) is the definitive treatment when conservative measures fail.

**Q:** What is xanthochromia, and what is its diagnostic significance?

**A:** Xanthochromia is yellow discolouration of the CSF caused by bilirubin from the breakdown of RBCs. It is diagnostic of SUBARACHNOID HEMORRHAGE (SAH). Also, in SAH, RBC count is equal in tube #1 and tube #4 (vs. traumatic tap where tube #1 >> tube #4).

Xanthochromia typically appears 2-4 hours after SAH and can persist for up to 2-3 weeks. A CT head negative for SAH should be followed by LP looking for xanthochromia or elevated RBCs. The RBC count comparison between first and last tubes differentiates SAH from traumatic tap — a critical MCCQE1 concept.

**Q:** Compare the CSF findings in bacterial meningitis vs. viral meningitis vs. SAH.

**A:** Bacterial meningitis: cloudy, high protein (~1.0 g/L), low/normal glucose, high WBC (>1000×10⁶/L, neutrophil-predominant), high opening pressure. Viral meningitis: clear, normal or high protein (0.50-3.0 g/L), normal glucose, high WBC (<1000×10⁶/L, lymphocyte-predominant), usually normal opening pressure. SAH: xanthochromic or grossly bloody, high protein, normal glucose, high RBC (equal in tubes 1 and 4), normal opening pressure.
