CSF interpretation is high-yield on MCCQE1. The key differentiators: glucose (low in bacterial, normal in viral), cell type (neutrophils in bacterial, lymphocytes in viral), and colour (xanthochromia in SAH). Low glucose in meningitis results from both bacterial consumption and impaired transport.

**Q:** What are the CSF findings in Guillain-Barré Syndrome (GBS), and what is the classic pattern called?

**A:** GBS CSF: clear fluid, HIGH protein (often markedly elevated), NORMAL WBC count (<5×10⁶/L), normal glucose, normal opening pressure. This pattern is called ALBUMINOCYTOLOGIC DISSOCIATION (elevated protein with normal cell count).

Albuminocytologic dissociation is the CSF hallmark of GBS. It reflects inflammatory damage to nerve roots causing protein leakage without pleocytosis. The degree of protein elevation generally correlates with disease severity. This is one of the most frequently tested CSF patterns on MCCQE1.

**Q:** What are the CSF findings in Multiple Sclerosis (MS)?

**A:** MS CSF: clear, normal or slightly elevated protein, normal glucose, normal or slightly elevated WBC (lymphocyte-predominant), normal opening pressure. Key finding: OLIGOCLONAL BANDS on CSF protein electrophoresis (IgG bands present in CSF but not in serum, indicating intrathecal IgG synthesis).

Oligoclonal bands are found in >90% of MS patients but are not specific (also seen in CNS Lyme, neurosyphilis, viral encephalitis). The finding of oligoclonal bands, combined with clinical presentation and MRI, supports the diagnosis of MS. Elevated IgG index is another supportive finding.

**Q:** What is the normal CSF opening pressure range in adults, and how is it measured?

**A:** Normal CSF opening pressure in adults: 6-25 cmH₂O. In children: 12-28 cmH₂O. Measured using a manometer BEFORE collection of CSF, with the patient in LATERAL DECUBITUS position, head neutral, and legs extended.

Accurate opening pressure measurement requires proper patient positioning — legs extended (not fetal position) to avoid falsely elevated readings. Elevated opening pressure (>25 cmH₂O in adults) suggests idiopathic intracranial hypertension, CNS infection, or venous sinus thrombosis. IIH (pseudotumor cerebri) has elevated pressure with otherwise normal CSF.

**Q:** In radiculopathy vs. peripheral neuropathy affecting C6, which muscles would be weak in each condition?

**A:** C6 radiculopathy: ALL muscles supplied by C6 are weak — both biceps brachii (musculocutaneous nerve, elbow flexion) AND extensor carpi radialis longus (radial nerve, wrist extension). C6 peripheral neuropathy (e.g., musculocutaneous nerve palsy): ONLY biceps brachii (elbow flexion) weak, wrist extension spared. C6 radial nerve palsy: ONLY wrist extension weak, elbow flexion spared.

The key principle: radiculopathy affects ALL muscles sharing a nerve root regardless of which peripheral nerve delivers them. Peripheral neuropathy affects only muscles served by that specific nerve. This table-based approach (Table 5 in Toronto Notes) is high-yield for MCCQE1 EMG and clinical localization questions.

**Q:** Which spinal nerve roots are responsible for key myotomal functions (C5-S2)?

**A:** C5: shoulder abduction (deltoid). C6: elbow flexion, wrist extension. C7: elbow extension, finger extension. C8/T1: finger abduction, thumb movements. L2: hip flexion. L3: hip adduction. L4: knee extension, ankle dorsiflexion. L5: ankle dorsiflexion, big toe extension. S1: ankle plantarflexion. S1-S2: ankle plantarflexion (gastrocnemius/soleus).

Myotomes are essential for localizing radiculopathy. Quick mnemonics: C5=shake hands/raise elbow; C6=bend elbow; C7=push down; C8/T1=fingers; L4=kick forward (knee ext); L5=walk on heels; S1=walk on toes. These are also the reflexes: C5-6=biceps, C7=triceps, L4=patellar, S1=ankle.

**Q:** What does the Romberg test assess, and what does a positive result indicate?

**A:** Romberg test: patient stands with feet together and eyes CLOSED. POSITIVE if patient becomes unstable when eyes are closed (but was stable with eyes open). Indicates loss of proprioception causing SENSORY ATAXIA (dorsal column dysfunction). Eyes-open instability suggests cerebellar ataxia (Romberg negative).

The Romberg test isolates proprioception by removing visual compensation. With eyes open, vision compensates for proprioceptive loss. When eyes close, the patient with dorsal column disease (e.g., B12 deficiency, tabes dorsalis, MS) becomes unsteady. Cerebellar ataxia is present even with eyes open, making Romberg negative.

**Q:** Compare the four pathways: dorsal column-medial lemniscus vs. spinothalamic tract — what do they carry and where do they decussate?

**A:** Dorsal column-medial lemniscus (DCML): fine touch, vibration, proprioception from body. Decussates at the MEDULLA (internal arcuate fibers → medial lemniscus). Spinothalamic tract: pain and temperature from body. Decussates within 1-2 SPINAL LEVELS of entry (crosses at spinal cord level), then ascends as spinothalamic tract.

The different decussation levels explain the pattern in spinal cord syndromes: Brown-Séquard (hemisection) causes ipsilateral loss of fine touch/vibration/proprioception (DCML) and CONTRALATERAL loss of pain/temperature (spinothalamic) below the lesion. Central cord lesions disrupt crossing spinothalamic fibers, causing bilateral pain/temperature loss in a cape-like distribution.

**Q:** What are the key features of neuromuscular disease on EMG/NCS for each category: motor neuron disease, peripheral neuropathy, NMJ disorder, and myopathy?

**A:** Motor neuron disease (e.g. ALS): EMG shows denervation/reinnervation (large motor unit potentials); NCS normal or abnormal; CK normal/mildly increased. Peripheral neuropathy: EMG denervation/reinnervation; NCS shows demyelination and/or axonal loss; CK normal. NMJ disorder: EMG shows jitter on single-fibre EMG; NCS shows decremental response on repetitive nerve stimulation (MG) or incremental response (LEMS); CK normal. Myopathy: EMG shows small, short motor unit potentials; NCS normal; CK increased (early/mid-stage).

EMG/NCS interpretation is high-yield for MCCQE1. Repetitive nerve stimulation: MG shows decremental response (>10% decrement at 3Hz); LEMS shows incremental response (>100% facilitation at high-frequency stimulation). Single-fibre EMG is most sensitive for NMJ disorders. CK elevation points to myopathy or motor neuron disease.

**Q:** What are the gait patterns associated with common neurological conditions?

**A:** Antalgic: limping due to pain. Hemiplegic: circumduction of a spastic leg (UMN). Ataxic: wide-based, staggering (cerebellar or sensory). Parkinsonian: shuffling, festinating, stooped posture, reduced arm swing, difficulty initiating. Steppage (foot drop): high steps to clear a dropped foot (peroneal nerve/L4-L5 lesion). Broad-based: cerebellar or normal pressure hydrocephalus. Apraxic: 'magnetic gait' (feet stick to floor, NPH).

Gait assessment is a key component of the neurological exam. The pull test (stand behind patient, pull shoulders back) assesses postural instability in Parkinson's disease (positive = retropulsion >2 steps). NPH presents with the triad: magnetic gait (apraxic), urinary incontinence, and cognitive decline.

**Q:** What is the approach to a patient presenting with WEAKNESS, including key differentiating features and investigations?

**A:** Mode of onset: abrupt (vascular/toxic/metabolic), subacute (neoplastic/inflammatory), insidious (genetic/degenerative). Course: worse at onset (vascular), progressive (neoplastic/degenerative), episodic (vascular/inflammatory), activity-dependent (NMJ/muscle). Pattern: generalized vs. localized; proximal vs. distal; UMN vs. LMN; symmetric vs. asymmetric. Investigations: LMN → NCS/EMG; UMN → brain/spinal cord MRI; myopathy → CK, muscle biopsy, EMG; NMJ → NCS/EMG with repetitive stimulation, AChR antibodies (MG), VGCC antibodies (LEMS).

A structured approach to weakness localizes the lesion: proximal symmetric → myopathy; distal symmetric → polyneuropathy; fatigable → NMJ; fasciculations + atrophy → anterior horn cell; UMN signs + sensory level → spinal cord; hemiplegia + cortical signs → cortex. This framework guides efficient MCCQE1 clinical reasoning.

**Q:** What is the significance of 'dissociated sensory loss,' and what conditions cause it?

**A:** Dissociated sensory loss: loss of one sensory modality while the other is preserved. Spinothalamic loss (pain/temperature) with preserved dorsal column (fine touch/proprioception): central cord lesion (syringomyelia), Brown-Séquard syndrome. Dorsal column loss with preserved spinothalamic: posterior cord infarction, Tabes dorsalis, B12 deficiency, Friedrich's ataxia.

The anatomical separation of the spinothalamic tract (anterior/lateral) and dorsal columns (posterior) allows selective loss. Syringomyelia causes a cape-like bilateral pain/temperature loss from central canal expansion disrupting crossing spinothalamic fibers. Brown-Séquard (cord hemisection) causes ipsilateral DCML loss and contralateral spinothalamic loss below the lesion.

**Q:** What is the Circle of Willis, and which arteries compose it?

**A:** The Circle of Willis is an anastomotic ring at the base of the brain providing collateral flow. Components: bilateral anterior cerebral arteries (ACA) connected by the anterior communicating artery (AComm); bilateral internal carotid arteries (ICA); bilateral posterior cerebral arteries (PCA) connected to the ICA by the posterior communicating arteries (PComm); basilar artery (formed by vertebral arteries) → PCAs.

The Circle of Willis allows collateral circulation if one vessel is occluded. The most common aneurysm location is the AComm (35%), followed by PComm and middle cerebral artery bifurcation. A PComm aneurysm classically causes CN III palsy. Understanding the circle is essential for stroke localization and aneurysm management on MCCQE1.

**Q:** What are the key arteries supplying the brainstem and cerebellum, and what syndromes result from their occlusion?

**A:** Basilar artery: pons (occlusion → locked-in syndrome or death). PICA (posterior inferior cerebellar artery, from vertebral artery): lateral medulla and inferior cerebellum → Wallenberg syndrome (lateral medullary syndrome). AICA (anterior inferior cerebellar artery, from basilar): lateral pons and anterior-inferior cerebellum → lateral pontine syndrome. SCA (superior cerebellar artery, from basilar): superior cerebellum.

Wallenberg (PICA) syndrome is high-yield: ipsilateral face loss of pain/temperature (spinal trigeminal), contralateral body loss of pain/temperature (spinothalamic), ipsilateral Horner's, ipsilateral dysmetria, dysphagia, vertigo, nystagmus. AICA occlusion classically causes ipsilateral CN VII and VIII deficits. The '5 Ds' of posterior circulation stroke: Diplopia, Dysarthria, Dysphagia, Dizziness, Drop attacks.

**Q:** What is the corticospinal tract pathway, and where does it decussate?

**A:** Upper motor neuron originates in primary motor cortex → descends through internal capsule → cerebral peduncle → pons (pyramidal tracts) → MEDULLA (pyramidal decussation, ~85% of fibers cross) → contralateral lateral corticospinal tract (limb muscles). Remaining ~15% descend ipsilaterally as the anterior corticospinal tract (axial muscles, cross at each spinal level).

Pyramidal decussation at the cervicomedullary junction explains why cortical/subcortical strokes cause contralateral weakness. The anterior corticospinal tract controls axial/trunk muscles bilaterally, explaining why trunk control is often preserved in hemiplegia. Lesions above the decussation cause contralateral weakness; lesions below cause ipsilateral weakness.

**Q:** What are the autonomic functions of the sympathetic vs. parasympathetic nervous systems relevant to the pupil?

**A:** Sympathetic: dilates the pupil (mydriasis) via the dilator pupillae. 3-neuron arc: hypothalamus → ciliospinal center (C8-T2) → superior cervical ganglion → pupil. Disruption → Horner's syndrome (miosis, ptosis, anhidrosis). Parasympathetic: constricts the pupil (miosis) via sphincter pupillae, carried by CN III (Edinger-Westphal nucleus → ciliary ganglion → pupil).

Horner's syndrome (sympathetic disruption): miosis + partial ptosis (loss of Müller's muscle) + anhidrosis of the face. Central Horner's (brainstem/cord): no anhidrosis above T1. Pre-ganglionic (between T1 and superior cervical ganglion): anhidrosis of entire face. Post-ganglionic (above superior cervical ganglion): anhidrosis of medial face only. Localization guides the workup.

**Q:** What are the key components assessed when characterizing an abnormal gait?

**A:** Posture, stride length, width between feet, height of step, stability of pelvis, symmetry, arm swing, difficulty turning, tremor, elaborate/inconsistent movements, standing from sitting, and antalgia.

A systematic gait assessment is required before ordering investigations. Red flags that prompt urgent workup include sudden onset, cerebellar ataxia, paresis (hemi-, para-, quadri-), and bowel/bladder incontinence.

**Q:** What type of gait is associated with proprioceptive loss, and what is the classic finding on exam?

**A:** Sensory ataxia: wide-based gait with high stepping posture and a positive Romberg sign.

Proprioceptive loss causes a sensory ataxia due to impaired joint position sense. Causes include demyelinating neuropathies, paraneoplastic syndrome, tabes dorsalis, MS, compressive myelopathy, and vitamin B12 deficiency. The positive Romberg (worsening with eyes closed) distinguishes it from cerebellar ataxia.

**Q:** What is a steppage gait and what causes it?

**A:** Steppage gait results from foot drop (inability to dorsiflex the foot), causing the patient to lift the leg high to clear the foot. Causes include acquired/hereditary peripheral neuropathy, compressive peroneal neuropathy, and L4-L5 radiculopathy.

Foot drop is caused by lower limb nerve disorders affecting the peroneal nerve or L4/L5 nerve roots. The patient compensates by exaggerated hip and knee flexion to clear the foot during the swing phase.

**Q:** Describe the Parkinsonian gait and list four causes of Basal Ganglia gait disturbance.

**A:** Parkinsonian gait: short stride length, stooped posture, reduced arm swing. Causes include: Parkinson's disease (PD), progressive supranuclear palsy, multiple system atrophy (MSA), Huntington's disease, infarct, neuroleptic medications, Wilson's disease.

Basal ganglia dysfunction leads to impaired initiation and modulation of movement. Other features of Parkinsonism include festination (progressive quickening of pace), freezing, and difficulty turning. Choreic, hemiballistic, and dystonic gaits are also basal ganglia manifestations.

**Q:** How does cerebellar ataxic gait differ from sensory ataxic gait?

**A:** Cerebellar ataxia: wide-based gait WITHOUT high stepping, veers to the side of the lesion. Sensory ataxia: wide-based WITH high stepping and a positive Romberg sign.

In cerebellar ataxia the Romberg test is negative (patient is unsteady with eyes both open and closed). In sensory ataxia, closing the eyes dramatically worsens balance (positive Romberg). Cerebellar causes include neoplasm, toxins (EtOH), vitamin E deficiency, hypothyroidism, hypoxia, and paraneoplastic syndrome.

**Q:** What is a waddling gait and what underlying conditions cause it?

**A:** A broad-based, short-stepped gait with pronounced lumbar lordosis and rotation of the pelvis due to proximal muscle weakness. Caused by myopathies: muscular dystrophy and inflammatory myopathies.

Proximal hip girdle weakness prevents normal stabilization of the pelvis during walking, causing the characteristic waddling movement. Patients also typically have difficulty rising from a chair and climbing stairs.

**Q:** What are the features of a spastic gait and what conditions cause bilateral spastic gait?

**A:** Spastic gait features: spastic foot drop, circumduction, scissoring of legs or toe walking with bilateral circumduction. Bilateral causes include cervical spondylosis, cerebral palsy, spinal cord tumour, combined spinal cord degeneration, MS, and motor neuron disease.

Spastic gait results from corticospinal tract lesions. Unilateral spastic gait is typically caused by stroke (ischemic or hemorrhagic). Bilateral involvement suggests spinal cord pathology or bilateral upper motor neuron disease.

**Q:** What is Kallmann syndrome and which cranial nerve deficit is associated with it?

**A:** Kallmann syndrome is a congenital disorder characterized by anosmia (CN I deficit) and hypogonadotropic hypogonadism.

Kallmann syndrome involves failure of GnRH neurons and olfactory neurons to migrate properly during development. It is an important cause of congenital anosmia and must be distinguished from acquired causes when evaluating CN I deficits.

**Q:** What are the main categories in the differential diagnosis of anosmia (CN I deficit)?

**A:** 1. Nasal obstruction (rhinitis, nasal mass, septal deformity, choanal atresia). 2. Olfactory receptor neuron injury (head trauma, smoking, COVID-19). 3. Central olfactory structure involvement: neurological disease (AD, PD, MS), anterior cranial fossa mass (meningioma, aneurysm), intracranial surgery, Kallmann syndrome.

COVID-19 causing anosmia via damage to receptor neurons is an important recent addition to the differential. Loss of smell is often perceived as altered taste since olfaction contributes to flavour perception.

**Q:** What is the classic eye position in a complete CN III palsy and what are the associated findings?

**A:** Eye is 'down and out' (unopposed lateral rectus and superior oblique). Associated features: mydriasis (pupillary constrictor paralysis) and ptosis (levator palpebrae superioris paralysis). Vertical and horizontal (oblique) binocular diplopia.

CN III innervates the superior rectus, inferior rectus, medial rectus, inferior oblique, levator palpebrae, and pupillary constrictor. Loss of these functions with preserved lateral rectus (CN VI) and superior oblique (CN IV) results in the characteristic down-and-out position.

**Q:** In CN III palsy, how does the pupillary response distinguish ischemic from compressive causes?

**A:** Pupil-sparing CN III palsy suggests ischemic cause (microvascular). Mydriasis (dilated, non-reactive pupil) suggests compressive cause (e.g., posterior communicating artery aneurysm, uncal herniation).

Parasympathetic pupillary constrictor fibers travel in the periphery of CN III and are selectively compressed by external mass lesions. Smaller central blood vessels are more susceptible to ischemia (microvascular), which spares the peripheral parasympathetic fibers. This distinction is clinically critical — compressive causes require urgent neuroimaging.

**Q:** What are the three main categories in the DDx of CN III palsy?

**A:** 1. Ischemic (microvascular — pupil sparing). 2. Compressive (posterior communicating artery aneurysm, cavernous sinus lesion — mydriasis). 3. Midbrain lesion (mass, infarction, demyelination).

A posterior communicating artery aneurysm is an emergency and must be ruled out promptly when CN III palsy presents with mydriasis. Additional compressive causes include uncal herniation, trauma, and orbital or cavernous sinus lesions.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of a CN IV (trochlear nerve) palsy?

**A:** Vertical and torsional binocular diplopia, hypertropia (paralysis of superior oblique with unopposed elevation), contralateral head tilt, difficulty going down stairs or reading.

CN IV is the only cranial nerve that exits the brainstem posteriorly. Its axons decussate in the superior medullary velum. Therefore, lesions of the trochlear nucleus cause contralateral superior oblique paralysis. Common causes include microvascular ischemia, trauma (TBI or surgical), and cavernous sinus lesions.

**Q:** How do CN III, IV, and VI palsies differ clinically in their eye position and diplopia?

**A:** CN III: eye 'down and out', ptosis, mydriasis, vertical/horizontal diplopia. CN IV: hypertropia, contralateral head tilt, diplopia worse going downstairs, torsional diplopia. CN VI: esotropia, diplopia worse on lateral gaze toward affected side (horizontal diplopia only).

Differentiating the cranial nerve responsible requires careful analysis of eye position at rest, direction of diplopia, associated features (ptosis, pupil changes, head tilt), and direction that worsens diplopia. CN VI palsy presents with horizontal diplopia due to failure of abduction.

**Q:** What is the clinical significance of CN VI being a false localizing sign?

**A:** CN VI is vulnerable to increased or decreased ICP because of its long intracranial course, resulting in abducens palsy that does not localize the lesion to the pons — it is a 'false localizing sign'.

The long intracranial path of the abducens nerve makes it susceptible to stretching with elevated or decreased ICP. Therefore, an isolated CN VI palsy does not necessarily indicate a pontine lesion and should prompt investigation for raised ICP (e.g., LP after ruling out mass lesion).

**Q:** What is the difference between LMN and UMN facial nerve (CN VII) palsy in terms of clinical presentation?

**A:** LMN: complete ipsilateral facial paralysis (forehead AND lower face). UMN: contralateral paralysis WITH forehead sparing (bilateral UMN input to frontalis via facial motor nucleus).

The forehead (frontalis) receives bilateral UMN input, so a unilateral upper motor neuron lesion (e.g., stroke in internal capsule) spares forehead movement. A LMN lesion (e.g., Bell's palsy) involves all ipsilateral facial muscles including the forehead. Additional LMN features include decreased lacrimation, hyperacusis, and loss of taste in anterior 2/3 of tongue.

**Q:** What are the main causes of CN VII palsy?

**A:** Bell's palsy (idiopathic, 80-90% of cases), herpes zoster oticus (Ramsay Hunt syndrome), otitis media/mastoiditis, sarcoidosis, Lyme meningitis, HIV, parotid gland disorder, temporal bone fracture.

Bell's palsy is the most common cause and is a diagnosis of exclusion. Ramsay Hunt syndrome presents with painful herpetic vesicles in the ear canal and is associated with worse prognosis. Bilateral facial palsy should prompt investigation for Lyme disease or sarcoidosis.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of CN IX (glossopharyngeal nerve) palsy?

**A:** Oropharyngeal dysphagia (paralysis of stylopharyngeus — less severe than CN X), absent gag reflex (CN IX = afferent limb), loss of taste and sensation from posterior 1/3 of tongue.

CN IX provides the afferent limb of the gag reflex while CN X provides the efferent limb. Isolated CN IX palsy is rare; it often occurs with CN X palsy (jugular foramen syndrome). The dysphagia from CN IX palsy is less severe than CN X because CN X supplies most pharyngeal muscles.

**Q:** What is glossopharyngeal neuralgia and how is it treated?

**A:** Paroxysmal, shock-like pain from the posterior tongue, oropharynx, and ear, triggered by swallowing, chewing, coughing, or talking. Causes: vascular compression (usually PICA), neck mass, Eagle syndrome, demyelination, idiopathic. Treatment: antiepileptic drugs (carbamazepine), lidocaine, or surgery (microvascular decompression or surgical ablation of CN IX).

Glossopharyngeal neuralgia parallels trigeminal neuralgia in its paroxysmal character and response to carbamazepine. The distinction from trigeminal neuralgia is based on trigger zones (swallowing vs. touch) and location (oropharynx vs. face).

**Q:** What direction does the uvula deviate in a CN X LMN lesion and why?

**A:** The uvula deviates AWAY from (contralateral to) the lesion, because paralysis of ipsilateral soft palate muscles prevents elevation on that side, causing the uvula to be pulled to the intact side.

CN X innervates most muscles of the soft palate. When the ipsilateral palatal muscles are weak, the contralateral muscles pull the uvula toward the healthy side. This is opposite to tongue deviation in CN XII, where the tongue deviates toward the weak side.

**Q:** What is the proper clinical screening test for dysphagia and aspiration risk?

**A:** Observe the patient drinking water from a cup while watching for coughing, choking, or 'wetness' of voice. The presence of a gag reflex alone is insufficient as a screening test.

The gag reflex tests CN IX/X but does not reliably predict aspiration risk or safe swallowing. The water swallow test is a validated bedside screening tool. Patients with suspected aspiration should be referred for formal swallowing assessment by speech-language pathology.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of CN XI (accessory nerve) palsy?

**A:** Contralateral head turn weakness (paralysis of sternocleidomastoid) and ipsilateral shoulder shrug weakness (paralysis of trapezius). LMN lesion: ipsilateral paralysis; UMN lesion: contralateral paralysis. CN XI is vulnerable to injury during neck surgery.

The sternocleidomastoid turns the head to the contralateral side, so its ipsilateral weakness (LMN) presents as weakness turning the head toward the contralateral side. The trapezius elevates the ipsilateral shoulder. CN XI can be damaged during cervical lymph node dissection or other neck surgery.

**Q:** In what direction does the tongue deviate in CN XII (hypoglossal nerve) palsy, and how does LMN vs UMN differ?

**A:** LMN lesion: tongue deviates TOWARD the weak (ipsilateral) side with atrophy and fasciculations. UMN lesion: tongue deviates AWAY from (contralateral to) the lesion, without wasting.

The genioglossus muscle protrudes the tongue. In LMN hypoglossal palsy, the ipsilateral genioglossus is weak, so the stronger contralateral side pushes the tongue toward the weak side. LMN signs (atrophy, fasciculations) help distinguish from UMN palsy.

**Q:** What clinical triad characterizes optic neuropathy?

**A:** 1. Vision loss (decreased visual acuity, decreased colour vision, visual field defect). 2. Relative afferent pupillary defect (RAPD) — only if unilateral or asymmetric. 3. Change in appearance of optic disc (disc edema or pallor).

RAPD (Marcus Gunn pupil) is tested with the swinging flashlight test and indicates optic nerve asymmetry. It is absent in symmetric bilateral optic neuropathy. Optic disc pallor indicates chronic irreversible optic atrophy from ganglion cell loss.

**Q:** Compare arteritic vs non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (AION).

**A:** Arteritic AION: usually caused by GCA; associated with headache, scalp tenderness, jaw claudication, systemic symptoms (weight loss, fatigue, fever); RAPD present; treated with corticosteroids urgently. Non-arteritic AION: associated with vascular risk factors (DM, hyperlipidemia, HTN, atherosclerosis); no proven treatment; optimize risk factors.

If GCA is suspected, corticosteroids must be started IMMEDIATELY without waiting for temporal artery biopsy, as delay risks bilateral blindness. Investigation includes CBC, ESR, CRP, and temporal artery biopsy. Non-arteritic AION typically affects patients >50 yr and is the more common form.

**Q:** What distinguishes optic neuritis from papilledema in terms of clinical features?

**A:** Optic neuritis: unilateral/binocular central vision loss with pain on eye movement, RAPD present, age <50, associated with MS. Papilledema: bilateral disc edema from raised ICP, late vision loss, no RAPD, associated with headache/nausea/vomiting and neurological deficits.

Papilledema is bilateral optic disc edema specifically due to increased intracranial pressure and does not initially cause significant vision loss. Optic neuritis causes acute vision loss with pain on eye movement and is a classic MS presentation. Treatment of optic neuritis: high-dose IV or PO corticosteroids (accelerates recovery but does not improve long-term outcome).

**Q:** What visual field defect is associated with a lesion at the optic chiasm, and what causes it by age group?

**A:** Bitemporal hemianopsia (loss of both temporal fields). Causes by age: children — craniopharyngioma; 20-50 yr — pituitary adenoma; >60 yr — meningioma.

The optic chiasm contains decussating nasal fibers from both eyes. A compressive lesion at the chiasm (usually from below, e.g., pituitary adenoma) preferentially damages these crossing fibers, causing loss of both temporal visual fields — bitemporal hemianopsia.

**Q:** Where is the lesion in left homonymous hemianopsia with macular sparing, and what is the significance of congruence?

**A:** Left homonymous hemianopsia = right-sided lesion (optic tract, temporal/parietal radiation, or occipital cortex). Macular sparing suggests occipital cortex lesion. More posterior lesions produce more congruent (symmetric in both eyes) deficits.

Homonymous hemianopsia affects the same side of the visual field in both eyes and indicates a post-chiasmal lesion. The more posterior the lesion, the more congruent and complete the defect. Macular sparing with occipital lesions may be due to dual blood supply of the macula or the large cortical representation of the macula.

**Q:** What visual field defect results from a lesion of Meyer's loop (temporal radiation)?

**A:** Left upper quadrantanopsia (pie in the sky defect) from a right temporal lesion affecting Meyer's loop.

Meyer's loop carries inferior retinal fibers (representing the superior visual field) through the temporal lobe. A temporal lobe lesion (e.g., from temporal lobe surgery or tumour) causes a contralateral superior quadrantanopsia ('pie in the sky'). Parietal lobe lesions cause inferior quadrantanopsia.

**Q:** How does a frontal eye field (FEF) lesion vs. FEF seizure affect gaze deviation?

**A:** FEF lesion (stroke/tumour): eye deviation TOWARD the side of lesion. FEF seizure (overactivation): eye deviation AWAY from the side of overactivation.

The frontal eye field normally drives gaze contralaterally. A destructive lesion removes this drive, so the contralateral FEF drives gaze toward the side of the lesion. During a seizure, the FEF is overactivated and drives gaze to the contralateral side (away from the seizure focus). Similarly, PPRF lesions cause eye deviation away from the side of the lesion.

**Q:** What is internuclear ophthalmoplegia (INO) and what causes it?

**A:** INO is caused by a lesion of the medial longitudinal fasciculus (MLF). It presents with: adduction deficit in the eye ipsilateral to the lesion, and abducting nystagmus in the contralateral eye, with preserved convergence. Causes: demyelination (MS, most common), brainstem infarction/mass.

The MLF connects the abducens nucleus in the pons to the contralateral medial rectus subnucleus of CN III. A MLF lesion impairs the horizontal gaze pathway. Since convergence is independent of the horizontal gaze pathway, preserved convergence helps distinguish INO from CN III palsy. INO can be bilateral, especially in MS.

**Q:** How do you differentiate monocular from binocular diplopia and what does each imply?

**A:** Monocular: diplopia remains when the unaffected eye is closed — implies optical problem (refractive error, cataract, corneal abnormality, dry eye) or functional. Binocular: diplopia disappears when either eye is closed — implies ocular misalignment from muscle, neuromuscular junction (MG, LEMS), or nerve/brain pathology.

This distinction is the first step in evaluating diplopia. Binocular diplopia is the neurologically significant form and requires systematic assessment of which CN or muscle is involved, based on the direction of diplopia (vertical, horizontal, oblique) and the direction of gaze that worsens it.

**Q:** What are the distinguishing features of peripheral vs. central nystagmus?

**A:** Peripheral (vestibular): horizontal + torsional, unidirectional, fast phase away from lesion, gaze fixation DECREASES nystagmus, severe vertigo, auditory symptoms common, adapts within days. Central (brainstem/cerebellum): may be purely vertical/horizontal/torsional, bidirectional/multidirectional, gaze fixation has NO effect, mild vertigo, other neurological signs common, persists.

The most clinically useful differentiating feature is that gaze fixation suppresses peripheral nystagmus (tested by removing visual fixation with Frenzel goggles). Pure vertical nystagmus is always central. Central nystagmus requires urgent neuroimaging. Causes of peripheral: BPPV, vestibular neuritis, Ménière's disease.

**Q:** What neurological condition is nitrous oxide misuse associated with, and by what mechanism?

**A:** Nitrous oxide misuse causes functional vitamin B12 deficiency (inactivates cobalamin), leading to subacute combined degeneration — myeloneuropathy with dorsal and lateral column involvement.

Nitrous oxide irreversibly oxidizes vitamin B12 (cobalamin), rendering it unable to act as a cofactor for methionine synthase. This is clinically important in recreational nitrous oxide users. Serum B12 may appear normal despite functional deficiency; methylmalonic acid and homocysteine levels are more reliable.

**Q:** What are the classic manifestations of Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome (thiamine/B1 deficiency)?

**A:** Wernicke's encephalopathy triad: ophthalmoplegia (eye movement abnormalities), ataxia, confusion. Korsakoff syndrome: anterograde > retrograde amnesia with confabulation. Caused by thiamine (vitamin B1) deficiency, classically in chronic alcohol use.

Wernicke's encephalopathy is a medical emergency. If suspected: thiamine 500 mg IV TID for 2 days, then 250 mg IV/IM for 2 days before any glucose administration. Standard treatment for thiamine deficiency: 200 mg IV TID until nutritional status is stable. Korsakoff syndrome represents irreversible neuronal damage from chronic thiamine deficiency.

**Q:** What are the neurological effects of copper deficiency and what is a common cause?

**A:** Copper deficiency causes myelopathy, myeloneuropathy, sensory ataxia, and spastic gait (similar to B12 deficiency). A common cause is excessive zinc supplementation (zinc competitively inhibits copper absorption). Treatment: discontinue zinc; supplement copper.

Copper deficiency myelopathy mimics subacute combined degeneration of B12 deficiency. Investigation includes serum copper, ceruloplasmin, urinary copper, MRI spine, and EMG/NCS. Bariatric surgery (malabsorptive) is another important cause of copper (and B12) deficiency.

**Q:** What are the neurological features of pellagra (niacin/B3 deficiency)?

**A:** Pellagra: encephalopathy, dementia, and peripheral neuropathy. The classic mnemonic: 3 Ds — Dermatitis, Diarrhea, Dementia.

Pellagra is niacin (vitamin B3) deficiency. Neurological manifestations include cognitive impairment (encephalopathy, dementia) and peripheral neuropathy. Investigation: urinary nicotinamide mononucleotide (NMN) and nuclear matrix protein (NMP) tests, niacin index. Treatment: nicotinic acid 25-50 mg PO/IM daily (watch for niacin flush).

**Q:** What occupational neurotoxin is associated with parkinsonism (manganism), and what psychiatric features precede it?

**A:** Manganese. Psychiatric symptoms and hallucinations ('manganese madness') precede extrapyramidal features, headache, dystonia, and parkinsonism (manganism). Associated with battery/metal production, welding, alloy work.

Manganese toxicity primarily affects the basal ganglia, particularly the globus pallidus, causing a parkinsonism that may be clinically indistinguishable from PD but does not respond well to levodopa. Pesticide exposure is also associated with increased PD risk.

**Q:** What neurological findings are caused by lead toxicity?

**A:** Headache, difficulty concentrating, short-term memory deficits, delayed development and permanent learning disabilities (children), peripheral neuropathy (commonly radial neuropathy causing wrist drop), seizures, coma, delirium, death from encephalopathy (rare).

Lead causes a predominantly motor peripheral neuropathy preferentially affecting the radial nerve (wrist drop). Children are more vulnerable to neurodevelopmental toxicity. Sources include battery/metal production, solder, pipes, and paint.

**Q:** Define seizure and epilepsy, and what are the diagnostic criteria for epilepsy?

**A:** Seizure: transient signs/symptoms due to abnormal hyper-synchronization of neurons. Epilepsy: enduring predisposition to generate epileptic seizures. Diagnosis requires: 1) ≥2 unprovoked seizures >24h apart, OR 2) one unprovoked seizure with ≥60% recurrence risk (based on EEG/imaging), OR 3) diagnosis of an epilepsy syndrome.

Provoked seizures (due to acute brain insults: metabolic, toxic, febrile) do not constitute epilepsy. Etiologies of epilepsy include genetic, structural, autoimmune, metabolic, infectious, and unknown. The most common cause of late-onset (>50 yr) seizures is stroke, accounting for 50-80% of cases.

**Q:** What are the features of absence seizures (petit mal) and what is the classic EEG finding?

**A:** Absence seizures: brief unresponsiveness for 5-10 seconds with arrest of activity, staring, blinking or eye-rolling; NO post-ictal confusion; usually in children. EEG: 3 Hz spike and slow wave activity. Drug of choice: ethosuximide.

Absence seizures can be mistaken for daydreaming. The lack of post-ictal confusion distinguishes them from complex partial seizures. Generalized absence seizures can occur dozens of times per day. Ethosuximide is first-line, with valproate as an alternative (noting safety profile concerns in women of childbearing age).

**Q:** What are the phases of a tonic-clonic (grand mal) seizure?

**A:** 1. Prodrome: unease/irritability hours to days before. 2. Tonic phase: muscle rigidity. 3. Clonic phase: repetitive violent jerking of face/limbs, tongue biting, cyanosis, frothing, incontinence. 4. Post-ictal phase: flaccid limbs, extensor plantar reflexes, headache, confusion, aching muscles, amnesia, elevated CK — may have focal paralysis (Todd's paralysis), lasting hours.

Todd's paralysis is a transient post-ictal focal neurological deficit (typically hemiparesis) lasting minutes to hours, reflecting post-ictal cortical depression. It is important to recognize as it mimics stroke — a careful history of the preceding seizure is essential.

**Q:** What are the features of a temporal lobe (complex partial) seizure?

**A:** Focal impaired awareness with automatisms (chewing, swallowing, lip-smacking, scratching, fumbling, running, disrobing). Aura may include fear, olfactory or gustatory hallucinations, visceral sensations, or déjà vu. May secondarily generalize.

Temporal lobe seizures are the most common focal seizures in adults. Automatisms are stereotyped, repetitive, purposeless movements. The aura (if present) represents simple partial seizure activity before impaired awareness begins. Temporal lobe epilepsy is often amenable to surgical resection if refractory.

**Q:** How do you differentiate seizure, syncope, and functional (pseudoseizure) on clinical grounds?

**A:** Seizure: sudden onset in any position (including sleep), aura possible, brief or prolonged, lateral tongue biting, post-ictal confusion/Todd's paresis/aphasia, incontinence common, synchronous stereotypic movements. Syncope: gradual onset, upright position, lightheadedness/pallor/diaphoresis, brief, no post-ictal, brief tonic stiffening possible. Pseudoseizure: provoked by emotional disturbance/suggestion, prolonged, variable, tip tongue biting, opisthotonos, pelvic thrust, crying.

EEG is usually abnormal in seizures (interictal or ictal discharges) and normal in syncope and pseudoseizures. Lateral tongue biting is highly specific for seizure, while tip tongue biting may occur in pseudoseizures. Pseudoseizures do not rule out coexisting epilepsy.

**Q:** What are EEG findings suggestive of predisposition to epilepsy?

**A:** Spike and wave discharges, polyspike and wave discharges, and spike wave complex discharges.

EEG has a sensitivity of 17% after a first seizure, increasing to 51% with a sleep-deprived EEG. EEG is specific (95%) but not sensitive; a normal EEG does not rule out epilepsy. If the first routine EEG is normal, a sleep-deprived EEG should be considered. EEG guides treatment decisions (indications for anti-seizure medication).

**Q:** What are the indications for initiating anti-seizure medication (ASM) after a first seizure?

**A:** EEG with epileptiform activity, prior brain injury (organic brain disease, prior head injury, CNS infection), abnormal neurological exam or neuroimaging, nocturnal seizure, and recurrent unprovoked seizure.

Not every first seizure requires ASM. The decision is based on risk of recurrence. If recurrence risk is ≥60%, ASM is indicated. The presence of structural brain pathology or epileptiform EEG significantly increases recurrence risk and generally warrants treatment.

**Q:** Which anti-seizure medications are used for primarily focal seizures (with or without secondary generalization) but should NOT be used for generalized seizures?

**A:** Carbamazepine (Tegretol), phenytoin (Dilantin), gabapentin, lacosamide, oxcarbazepine, eslicarbazepine, pregabalin, tiagabine, vigabatrin, cenobamate.

These sodium channel blockers and GABA modulators can worsen certain generalized epilepsies (especially absence and myoclonic seizures). Valproate and lamotrigine are broad-spectrum and effective for both focal and generalized seizures. Ethosuximide is specific to absence seizures.

**Q:** What is the first-line drug for generalized tonic-clonic seizures in Canada, and what alternatives exist?

**A:** Valproate (sodium valproate/Depakene) remains the preferred first-line treatment for generalized tonic-clonic seizures. Lamotrigine and levetiracetam are suitable alternatives, especially considering safety profiles for those of childbearing potential.

Valproate is highly effective for generalized seizures but has significant teratogenicity (neural tube defects, fetal valproate syndrome). In women of childbearing age, lamotrigine or levetiracetam are preferred alternatives. This reflects Canadian and ILAE guidelines balancing efficacy and tolerability.

**Q:** What are the driving regulations for patients with seizures in Canada?

**A:** By law, the Ministry of Transportation in most provinces must be contacted for all patients who have had a seizure. Patients will have their license suspended until seizure-free for 6 months. Commercial drivers face a longer waiting period.

Physicians have a duty to report seizures to the Ministry of Transportation in most Canadian provinces. This is a mandatory safety regulation regardless of patient consent. Counseling patients about driving restrictions, operating heavy machinery, bathing alone, and swimming is an essential part of seizure management.

**Q:** Define status epilepticus. What are its major complications?

**A:** Status epilepticus: seizure lasting ≥5 minutes OR two seizures without return to neurological baseline between them. Complications: hypoxia/anoxia, cerebral ischemia, MI, cerebral edema, arrhythmia/cardiac arrest, rhabdomyolysis (→acute kidney injury), aspiration pneumonitis/pneumonia.

Status epilepticus is a medical emergency that can cause irreversible brain damage without prompt treatment. The most common causes include anti-seizure medication non-compliance, remote symptomatic causes (prior brain injury), and stroke. Despite being a common seizure trigger, EtOH withdrawal is a rare cause of status epilepticus.

**Q:** What is the initial management of status epilepticus?

**A:** 1. ABCs, vitals, monitors, lateral decubitus position, 100% O2 via non-rebreather, 2 peripheral IVs, point-of-care glucose, IV fluids, ECG. 2. Blood work: CBC, electrolytes, Ca, Mg, PO4, glucose, ABG/VBG, toxicology, ASM levels. 3. Treat specific causes: D50 for hypoglycemia, IV thiamine for alcohol withdrawal, magnesium sulfate for eclampsia. 4. Anti-seizure medications (benzodiazepines first-line).

Immediate glucose correction and thiamine administration (before glucose in suspected alcoholics to prevent precipitating Wernicke's) are critical. Consider non-convulsive status epilepticus in any patient with persistent decreased level of consciousness >20 minutes after a generalized seizure — requires EEG to diagnose.

**Q:** What aura features are suggestive of a frontoparietal cortex seizure origin?

**A:** Contralateral focal sensory or motor phenomena (e.g., focal paresthesias, jerking of a limb).

The primary sensory cortex (parietal) and motor cortex (frontal) are somatotopically organized. Focal seizures arising here produce contralateral sensory or motor symptoms that may spread along the body's map in a 'Jacksonian march,' spreading from one body part to adjacent ones as the seizure propagates.

**Q:** What is a Jacksonian march and what does it indicate?

**A:** Jacksonian march is the sequential spreading of clonic or sensory seizure activity to adjacent muscle groups (e.g., thumb → hand → arm → face), indicating a focal motor/sensory cortex seizure spreading across the motor homunculus.

The Jacksonian march reflects the organized somatotopic representation of the motor cortex. As epileptic activity spreads, successive body parts become involved in a predictable pattern. This is pathognomonic of a focal cortical seizure originating in the primary motor or sensory cortex.

**Q:** What are the features of cavernous sinus syndrome affecting cranial nerves?

**A:** Cavernous sinus lesions can cause palsies of CN III, IV, VI, V1, and V2, as well as orbital pain and proptosis. Causes include internal carotid artery aneurysm, meningioma, and sinus thrombosis.

The cavernous sinus contains CN III, IV, VI, V1, V2 and the internal carotid artery (with its sympathetic plexus). Horner syndrome (ptosis, miosis, anhidrosis) may also occur from sympathetic involvement. Multiple cranial nerve palsies should always raise suspicion for cavernous sinus pathology.

**Q:** What is the ILAE 2017 seizure classification system's primary division?

**A:** Seizures are classified by onset as: 1) Focal onset (with or without impaired awareness), 2) Generalized onset (motor or nonmotor), 3) Unknown onset. Focal onset seizures can secondarily spread to become focal to bilateral tonic-clonic (previously called secondary generalization).

The ILAE 2017 classification replaced older terms: 'simple partial' → focal aware; 'complex partial' → focal impaired awareness; 'secondary generalization' → focal to bilateral tonic-clonic. This standardized terminology improves communication and guides treatment selection.

**Q:** What is the neurological significance of pyridoxine (vitamin B6) deficiency in infants?

**A:** Pyridoxine deficiency in infants causes intractable epilepsy (pyridoxine-dependent epilepsy), which responds specifically to pyridoxine supplementation. In adults: painful sensorimotor peripheral neuropathy and confusion.

Pyridoxine is a cofactor for glutamic acid decarboxylase (GAD), which synthesizes GABA. Deficiency reduces GABA levels, lowering the seizure threshold. Pyridoxine-dependent epilepsy is a rare but treatable cause of refractory neonatal/infantile seizures. Investigation: serum pyridoxal phosphate, EMG/NCS, EEG in infants.

**Q:** What is the neurological screening approach for patients with suspected aspiration from CN X dysfunction?

**A:** Observe the patient drinking water from a cup, watching for coughing, choking, or 'wetness' of voice (wet dysphonia). The gag reflex alone is insufficient to screen for aspiration risk.

A preserved gag reflex does not guarantee safe swallowing. The water swallow test is a validated, simple bedside tool to identify patients at risk for aspiration. Patients with positive screen should be referred for formal videofluoroscopic swallowing study (modified barium swallow) by speech-language pathology.

**Q:** What is the first-line abortive treatment for status epilepticus and what is the dose?

**A:** Benzodiazepines are first-line: lorazepam 4 mg IV (0.1 mg/kg) at 2 mg/min, repeat every 3 minutes; OR midazolam 10 mg IM/IN.

Early benzodiazepine administration is critical to terminate seizure activity. Lorazepam is preferred IV; midazolam IM/IN is used when IV access is unavailable. Both are GABA-A receptor agonists that rapidly suppress seizure activity.

**Q:** What maintenance antiseizure medications are used in status epilepticus after benzodiazepines fail to terminate seizures?

**A:** Phenytoin 20 mg/kg IV (or fosphenytoin 1000–1500 mg IV at 150 mg/min), valproic acid 40 mg/kg IV, or levetiracetam 60 mg/kg IV.

After initial benzodiazepines, a second-line agent is needed to prevent seizure recurrence. Fosphenytoin is preferred over phenytoin for faster and safer IV administration. Valproic acid and levetiracetam are alternatives, especially when phenytoin is contraindicated.

**Q:** What is the third-line treatment for refractory status epilepticus?

**A:** Continuous IV infusion of midazolam, propofol, or pentobarbital with EEG monitoring targeting burst suppression pattern in an ICU setting.

Refractory status epilepticus requires aggressive ICU management. Continuous infusions of anesthetic agents are titrated to burst suppression on EEG to completely suppress electrical seizure activity. Ketamine is an additional option.

**Q:** What folic acid dose is recommended for women with epilepsy planning pregnancy who are on antiseizure medications (ASMs)?

**A:** 4 mg/day of folic acid (higher than the standard 0.4 mg/day recommended for the general population).

ASMs, especially valproic acid and multi-drug regimens, are associated with a 2x increased risk of fetal malformations including neural tube defects. High-dose folic acid (4 mg/day) supplementation is recommended preconceptionally to reduce this risk.

**Q:** Which antiseizure medication carries the highest teratogenic risk during pregnancy?

**A:** Valproic acid has the highest teratogenic risk, especially when combined with two or more concurrent ASMs.

Valproic acid is associated with the highest rate of major congenital malformations among ASMs, including neural tube defects, cardiac defects, cleft palate, and urogenital abnormalities. The risk increases with polytherapy. Monotherapy at the lowest effective dose is preferred.

**Q:** What are the four features of the Confusion Assessment Method (CAM) used to diagnose delirium?

**A:** Feature 1: Acute onset and fluctuating course. Feature 2: Inattention. Feature 3: Disorganized thinking. Feature 4: Altered level of consciousness. Diagnosis requires features 1 AND 2, plus either 3 OR 4.

The CAM is the standard validated tool for diagnosing delirium. Its key distinguishing features from dementia are the acute onset, fluctuating course, and inattention. Delirium carries significant morbidity and mortality and should be recognized promptly.

**Q:** What clinical triad is associated with Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome (NMS)?

**A:** Muscle rigidity, hyperthermia, and autonomic instability in the context of antipsychotic medication use.

NMS is a life-threatening reaction to antipsychotics (dopamine antagonists). The classic triad is: (1) severe muscle rigidity, (2) hyperthermia, and (3) autonomic instability. Lab findings include elevated CK, leukocytosis, and elevated LFTs. Treatment includes stopping the offending agent, supportive care, dantrolene, and bromocriptine.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for Mild Neurocognitive Disorder (Mild Cognitive Impairment)?

**A:** 1) Change in cognition; 2) Impairment in one or more cognitive domains (amnestic or non-amnestic); 3) Preservation of independence in ADLs/IADLs (minimal impairment); 4) Not meeting criteria for major NCD; 5) No other reversible/identifiable cause.

Mild NCD represents a transitional state between normal aging and major dementia. Amnestic subtype is the most common and most strongly associated with Alzheimer's pathology. Annual follow-up is recommended; those with mild NCD have a relative risk 3.3x higher for developing major NCD over 2-5 years.

**Q:** What is the prevalence of depression in patients with Mild Cognitive Impairment (MCI)?

**A:** Pooled prevalence is approximately 32% (95% CI 27–37%); higher in clinic-based populations (40%) than community-based populations (25%).

Depression is the most common neuropsychiatric symptom in mild NCD, affecting up to 50% in some series. Recognizing and treating depression is important as it may be a modifiable risk factor for progression to major dementia and significantly affects quality of life.

**Q:** What cognitive screening tools are used for neurocognitive disorder, and which is more sensitive for early changes?

**A:** MMSE (sensitivity 87%, specificity 82%) and MoCA (sensitivity 85%, specificity 82%). MoCA is more sensitive for early cognitive change and mild NCD.

Both the MMSE and MoCA are validated screening tools, but MoCA detects earlier and more subtle cognitive changes. Neither should be used in isolation; they must be combined with history, neurological exam, and neuropsychological testing. Clock drawing and frontal lobe testing (go/no-go, word lists) add additional information.

**Q:** What are the reversible causes of major neurocognitive disorder (dementia) that must be ruled out?

**A:** EtOH (intoxication/withdrawal, Wernicke's), medications (benzodiazepines, anticholinergics), heavy metal toxicity, hepatic/renal failure, vitamin B12 deficiency, hypo/hyperglycemia, cortisol abnormality, thyroid dysfunction, NPH, depression (pseudodementia), intracranial tumour, subdural hematoma, and hypercalcemia.

Up to 10-15% of dementias have potentially reversible causes. Initial workup includes CBC, TSH, vitamin B12, glucose, electrolytes, LFTs, renal function, lipids, and calcium. CT head rules out structural lesions. Treating reversible causes can prevent or partially reverse cognitive decline.

**Q:** What are the 'geriatric giants' that should be assessed in the history of a patient with suspected major NCD?

**A:** Confusion, incontinence, and falls (the 3 classic geriatric giants associated with cognitive decline).

The geriatric giants represent common presentations of underlying disease in older adults. In the context of NCD, all three may reflect functional decline. Safety assessment (wandering, driving, stove use) and evaluation of ADLs/IADLs are essential components of the clinical encounter.

**Q:** What are the key clinical features distinguishing Alzheimer's disease (AD) from normal aging?

**A:** AD: Forgetting names of close relations, repeating phrases/stories in the same conversation, increased frequency of forgetting, unpredictable mood changes, decreased interest in activities. Normal aging: Forgetting acquaintances' names, briefly forgetting part of an experience, not putting things away properly, mood changes in response to appropriate causes, changes in usual interests.

Distinguishing pathological cognitive decline from normal aging is clinically important. The key features in AD are the severity of memory impairment, its impact on daily functioning, and the presence of behavioral/psychiatric changes. Importantly, social cognition is relatively preserved until late in the course of AD.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of dementia and what percentage of cases does it account for?

**A:** Alzheimer's disease (AD), accounting for 60–90% of all dementias depending on setting and diagnostic criteria.

AD is by far the most prevalent dementia. Its prevalence increases dramatically with age: 1/12 of the population aged 65–75 and up to 1/3 of those over 85 are affected. The second most common cause is vascular NCD. FTD is the 4th most common and accounts for 5% of cases.

**Q:** What are the three genes associated with autosomal dominant early-onset Alzheimer's disease?

**A:** 1) Amyloid precursor protein (APP) gene on chromosome 21; 2) Presenilin 1 (PSEN1) on chromosome 14; 3) Presenilin 2 (PSEN2) on chromosome 1. Together they account for 5–10% of early-onset AD cases.

Familial AD is rare (<1% of all AD) but important to identify for genetic counseling. Autosomal dominant mutations in these three genes cause early-onset AD (before age 65). Note that Down syndrome (trisomy 21) also predisposes to early AD due to three copies of the APP gene.

**Q:** What is the role of APOE ε4 in Alzheimer's disease?

**A:** APOE ε4 is a susceptibility genotype that increases risk and decreases age of onset of AD. It is neither necessary nor sufficient for disease occurrence and cannot serve as a diagnostic marker. APOE ε2 is protective.

APOE ε4 is the most common genetic risk factor for sporadic late-onset AD. However, many people with ε4 do not develop AD, and many AD patients do not carry ε4. Therefore, APOE genotyping is not recommended as a routine diagnostic test but is used in research settings.

**Q:** What are the four classic microscopic pathological findings in Alzheimer's disease?

**A:** 1) Senile β-amyloid plaques (extracellular deposits in grey matter); 2) Neurofibrillary tangles (intracytoplasmic hyperphosphorylated tau protein); 3) Loss of synapses; 4) Loss of cholinergic neurons in the nucleus basalis of Meynert.

The amyloid cascade hypothesis proposes that abnormal aggregation of β-amyloid triggers downstream tau pathology and neuronal death. Loss of cholinergic neurons from the nucleus basalis of Meynert accounts for the 50–90% reduction in choline acetyltransferase activity and forms the basis for cholinesterase inhibitor therapy.

**Q:** What imaging findings are characteristic of Alzheimer's disease on MRI and SPECT?

**A:** MRI: preferential atrophy of hippocampi and precuneus of the parietal lobe, dilated lateral ventricles, widened cortical sulci. SPECT: hypoperfusion in temporal and parietal lobes.

Neuroimaging in AD cannot confirm the diagnosis but supports it by demonstrating characteristic atrophy patterns. PET with Pittsburgh compound B (PIB) can image β-amyloid plaques, and FDG-PET shows regional cortical hypometabolism, particularly helpful to distinguish AD from FTD and DLB.

**Q:** What pharmacological treatments are available for Alzheimer's disease and what are their indications?

**A:** 1) Acetylcholinesterase inhibitors (donepezil, rivastigmine, galantamine) — for mild to severe AD; 2) Memantine (NMDA antagonist) — moderate-to-severe AD (MMSE <17); 3) Combination therapy (AChEI + memantine) — most effective for reducing morbidity; 4) Lecanemab (anti-β-amyloid monoclonal antibody) — for early AD.

AChEIs do not prolong life but reduce morbidity. Key contraindications include bradycardia, heart block, CHF, asthma/COPD, and peptic ulcer disease. Galantamine is contraindicated in hepatic/renal impairment. Lecanemab represents a newer disease-modifying approach targeting aggregated β-amyloid.

**Q:** What are the four core diagnostic features of Dementia with Lewy Bodies (DLB)?

**A:** 1) Fluctuating cognition with pronounced variations in attention and alertness; 2) Recurrent well-formed, detailed visual hallucinations; 3) ≥1 feature of parkinsonism (bradykinesia, rest tremor, or rigidity) with onset AFTER cognitive decline; 4) REM sleep behaviour disorder.

Probable DLB requires ≥2 core features; possible DLB requires ≥1 core feature. The sequence of symptoms is important: in DLB, parkinsonism occurs AFTER cognitive decline (opposite of Parkinson's disease dementia, where cognitive decline occurs ≥1 year after motor symptoms). Severe neuroleptic sensitivity is a critical distinguishing feature and a safety concern.

**Q:** Why are neuroleptics dangerous in Dementia with Lewy Bodies (DLB)?

**A:** Patients with DLB have severe sensitivity to neuroleptic medications, which can cause severe rigidity, neuroleptic malignant syndrome, and worsened extrapyramidal symptoms, leading to significant morbidity and mortality.

Neuroleptic sensitivity in DLB is thought to be due to profound dopaminergic depletion. Even low doses of typical or atypical antipsychotics can precipitate life-threatening reactions. This is a critical prescribing consideration. If behavioral symptoms require treatment, acetylcholinesterase inhibitors or very cautious use of quetiapine may be considered.

**Q:** What SPECT/PET finding is indicative (most diagnostic) of Dementia with Lewy Bodies?

**A:** Low striatal dopamine transporter (DAT) uptake on SPECT/PET imaging.

DAT imaging (DaTSCAN) demonstrates reduced dopaminergic activity in the striatum, reflecting degeneration of the nigrostriatal pathway. This finding can help distinguish DLB from AD and other dementias. Supportive SPECT/PET findings include generalized low uptake with occipital predominance.

**Q:** What is the treatment for REM sleep behaviour disorder in Dementia with Lewy Bodies?

**A:** Melatonin (first-line) or clonazepam (use with caution in patients with cognitive impairment and gait disorders).

REM sleep behaviour disorder (RBD) is a core feature of DLB where patients act out dreams due to loss of normal REM atonia. Melatonin is preferred due to its favorable safety profile. Clonazepam is effective but carries risks of falls, sedation, and cognitive worsening in this population.

**Q:** What are the five core symptoms required for behavioural variant Frontotemporal Dementia (bvFTD) diagnosis (at least 3 of 5 must be present)?

**A:** 1) Behavioural disinhibition (socially inappropriate/impulsive); 2) Apathy or inertia; 3) Loss of sympathy or empathy; 4) Perseverative, stereotyped, or compulsive/ritualistic behaviour; 5) Hyperorality and dietary changes (binge eating, increased EtOH/cigarettes, or eating inedible objects).

bvFTD is the most common variant of FTD and typically presents early (within the first 3 years). Memory is relatively spared early, distinguishing it from AD. It reflects progressive frontal lobe degeneration. Genetic variants include MAPT (tau), PGRN, and C9ORF72 genes. Median survival is 6–11 years from symptom onset.

**Q:** What are the three subtypes of Primary Progressive Aphasia (PPA) in frontotemporal dementia?

**A:** 1) Nonfluent/agrammatic PPA (NFAV-PPA): non-fluent, laboured speech, anomia, preserved single-word comprehension, impaired repetition. 2) Semantic variant PPA (SV-PPA): fluent, anomia, impaired single-word comprehension, intact repetition, uses generalizations. 3) Logopenic progressive aphasia (LPA): naming difficulty and impaired repetition.

PPA is defined by prominent language decline as the initial presenting feature. Distinguishing the three subtypes requires detailed language assessment. SV-PPA is particularly recognized by the inability to understand word meaning (e.g., using 'animal' for 'dog'). LPA is most often associated with underlying AD pathology.

**Q:** What is vascular neurocognitive disorder (vascular NCD) and what is its neuroimaging definition?

**A:** Vascular NCD is cognitive impairment due to cerebrovascular disease (CVD) as the dominant pathology. Neuroimaging criteria include: ≥1 large vessel infarct or hemorrhage, a strategically placed single infarct (angular gyrus, thalamus, basal forebrain), ≥2 lacunar infarcts outside brainstem, or extensive confluent white matter lesions.

Vascular NCD is the second most common cause of dementia. Unlike AD, it characteristically affects complex attention and frontal-executive function. Classic teaching describes stepwise deterioration, but progressive decline is actually most common. Risk factor optimization (HTN, DM, dyslipidemia, smoking) is the cornerstone of management.

**Q:** What are the cognitive domains primarily affected in vascular neurocognitive disorder, and how does this differ from Alzheimer's disease?

**A:** Vascular NCD predominantly affects complex attention (processing speed) and frontal-executive function. In contrast, AD typically affects episodic memory first, with later involvement of language and visuospatial function.

The predominant frontal-executive and attention deficits in vascular NCD reflect disruption of cortical-subcortical circuits by white matter lesions and lacunar infarcts. This pattern is sometimes called 'bradyphrenia' or subcortical dementia. Associated features include personality/mood changes, abulia, emotional lability, and psychomotor slowing.

**Q:** What are the key clinical features, investigations, and prognosis of Creutzfeldt-Jakob Disease (CJD)?

**A:** Features: rapidly progressive cognitive impairment, myoclonus, ataxia, akinetic mutism, parkinsonism. Investigations: MRI (cortical ribbon sign, hockey stick sign on FLAIR), EEG (periodic complexes), CSF analysis, real-time quaking-induced conversion test; definitive diagnosis by brain biopsy. Most cases are sporadic (85%). No cure exists — only symptomatic management.

CJD is a fatal prion disease caused by misfolded prion proteins converting from α-helix (normal) to β-pleated sheet (abnormal), leading to aggregation and neuronal loss with spongiform changes. It is the most common cause of rapidly progressive dementia alongside FTD, diffuse Lewy body disease, and tauopathies.

**Q:** What are the language areas of the brain and their functions?

**A:** Broca's area (posterior inferior frontal lobe): language production (expressive). Wernicke's area (posterior superior temporal lobe): language comprehension (receptive). Angular gyrus: relays written visual stimuli to Wernicke's for reading. Arcuate fasciculus: connects Wernicke's and Broca's areas.

Understanding the neural architecture of language is essential for localizing aphasia lesions. >99% of right-handed people have left hemisphere language dominance; 70% of left-handed people also have left hemisphere dominance. Aphasia localizes the lesion to the dominant cerebral hemisphere.

**Q:** A patient has non-fluent speech, poor comprehension, and poor repetition. What type of aphasia is this and where is the lesion?

**A:** Global aphasia. Lesion involves both the posterior inferior frontal lobe and posterior superior temporal lobe (combined Broca's and Wernicke's area), typically from a large MCA territory infarct.

Global aphasia is the most severe form, with impairment in all language domains: fluency, comprehension, repetition, and naming. It results from extensive dominant hemisphere damage. In contrast, mixed transcortical aphasia (poor fluency, poor comprehension, but intact repetition) spares the perisylvian language core.

**Q:** A patient has fluent speech with poor comprehension but intact repetition. What type of aphasia is this?

**A:** Sensory transcortical aphasia (STCA). Lesion is in the posterior superior temporal watershed region between MCA and PCA territories.

Transcortical aphasias are typically associated with cerebral anoxia (e.g., post-MI, CO poisoning, hypotension). The hallmark of all transcortical aphasias is preserved repetition because the perisylvian language core (Broca's, Wernicke's, arcuate fasciculus) is intact, but the surrounding association cortex is damaged.

**Q:** What is Conduction aphasia and what is the key distinguishing feature?

**A:** Conduction aphasia: fluent speech, intact comprehension, but poor repetition. Lesion is in the arcuate fasciculus (the white matter tract connecting Wernicke's and Broca's areas).

Conduction aphasia results from disconnection between the comprehension and production areas while both remain intact. Patients can understand speech and produce fluent speech but cannot accurately repeat what is said to them. Phonemic paraphasias (substituting similar-sounding words) are characteristic.

**Q:** What is ideomotor apraxia, and what is the hemispheric localization?

**A:** Ideomotor apraxia is the inability to perform skilled learned motor sequences (e.g., blowing out a match, combing one's hair) despite intact strength, sensation, and comprehension. It localizes to the left (dominant) hemisphere.

Apraxia is a higher-order motor disorder that cannot be explained by primary weakness, ataxia, sensory loss, or impaired comprehension. It reflects damage to praxis networks in the dominant hemisphere. Ideational apraxia (inability to sequence actions, e.g., mailing an envelope) involves both hemispheres.

**Q:** What are the features of Gerstmann's syndrome and what lesion causes it?

**A:** Gerstmann's syndrome: acalculia (inability to calculate), agraphia (inability to write), finger agnosia (inability to identify fingers), and left-right disorientation. Caused by lesions of the dominant (usually left) parietal lobe.

Gerstmann's syndrome is a classic localization finding. The four components may not all be present simultaneously. It is commonly tested because it localizes precisely to the dominant parietal lobe (specifically the angular gyrus region). In contrast, non-dominant parietal lobe lesions cause neglect, anosognosia, and asomatognosia.

**Q:** What are the findings associated with non-dominant parietal lobe lesions?

**A:** Neglect (hemispatial neglect), anosognosia (unawareness of deficits), and asomatognosia (unawareness of one's own body parts). Also cortical sensory loss: impaired graphesthesia, astereognosis, and impaired 2-point discrimination.

The non-dominant (usually right) parietal lobe is critical for spatial awareness and body schema. Neglect is the failure to attend to stimuli on the contralateral side despite intact primary sensation. Anosognosia is particularly dangerous as patients may deny their hemiplegia, affecting rehabilitation compliance.

**Q:** What is prosopagnosia and what is the localization of the lesion?

**A:** Prosopagnosia is the inability to recognize familiar faces despite intact visual perception and intact auditory recognition. Lesion is in bilateral temporo-occipital areas or the right inferior temporo-occipital region.

Prosopagnosia is a specific form of visual associative agnosia affecting face recognition. Despite knowing a face is a face, patients cannot identify whose face it is. This condition is distinct from visual field defects or general agnosia. It most commonly results from bilateral or right fusiform gyrus lesions.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria and clinical features of mild Traumatic Brain Injury (concussion)?

**A:** Mild TBI = concussion. Trauma-induced transient alteration in mental status: loss of consciousness <30 min, initial GCS 13–15, and post-traumatic amnesia <24 hours. Hallmark: confusion and amnesia. Physical symptoms include headache, sleep disturbance, nausea, blurred vision, photophobia, phonophobia, dizziness.

Concussion is the most common form of TBI (75% of all TBIs). Highest-risk groups are children 0–4 yr, adolescents 15–19 yr, and elderly >65 yr. Cognitive symptoms include attentional impairment, slowed reaction time, and amnesia. The extent of retrograde amnesia correlates with injury severity (more retrograde amnesia = more recent memories affected).

**Q:** What are the Canadian CT Head Rules indications for CT in mild TBI?

**A:** CT head is indicated by the Canadian CT Head Rules (specific criteria include: GCS score <15 at 2h after injury, suspected open/depressed skull fracture, any sign of basal skull fracture, vomiting ≥2 episodes, age ≥65, amnesia before impact ≥30 min, dangerous mechanism). X-ray skull is NOT indicated.

The Canadian CT Head Rules are a validated, widely used clinical decision tool developed in Canada to reduce unnecessary CT imaging after minor head injury while ensuring high sensitivity for clinically important brain injury. MRI is not indicated in initial evaluation but may be considered for persistent or worsening symptoms with a normal CT.

**Q:** What are the sequelae of mild Traumatic Brain Injury (concussion)?

**A:** 1) Post-concussion syndrome: dizziness, headache, neuropsychiatric symptoms, cognitive impairment (usually resolves within weeks to months). 2) Post-traumatic headaches (begin within 7 days of injury). 3) Post-traumatic epilepsy (~2% risk; prophylactic anticonvulsants NOT effective). 4) Post-traumatic vertigo.

While most patients recover with minimal treatment, some experience prolonged or permanent effects. Previous concussion increases risk of subsequent concussions. Repeat TBI can cause second impact syndrome (life-threatening cerebral edema) or cumulative brain injury including chronic traumatic encephalopathy (CTE).

**Q:** What are the components of the basal ganglia and their functional pathways?

**A:** Components: caudate nucleus, putamen, globus pallidus, subthalamic nucleus, and substantia nigra. Input from cerebral cortex; output to thalamus → back to cerebral cortex. Direct pathway: promotes movement. Indirect pathway: inhibits movement.

Understanding basal ganglia circuitry is essential for movement disorder pharmacology. In Parkinson's disease, dopamine deficiency from substantia nigra pars compacta reduces direct pathway activity and increases indirect pathway activity, resulting in reduced movement. Dopaminergic therapy restores this balance.

**Q:** What are the key considerations in managing a patient with major NCD (summarized by the ABCDs mnemonic)?

**A:** A — Affective disorders, ADLs. B — Behaviour management. C — Caretaker support, Cognitive medications and stimulation. D — Directives (advanced), Driving (fitness to drive), glasses/hearing aids (sensory aids).

Comprehensive dementia management extends beyond pharmacotherapy to include legal, safety, and psychosocial domains. Fitness to drive is a mandatory reporting requirement for physicians in Canada when cognitive impairment poses a safety risk. Advanced directives (power of attorney, living will) should be discussed early while the patient still has capacity.

**Q:** What cognitive and neuropsychiatric symptoms characterize the mild versus major phases of Alzheimer's disease?

**A:** Mild NCD (mild phase): memory and learning impairment, sometimes executive function deficits, major depressive disorder and/or apathy. Major NCD: visuospatial/perceptual-motor deficits, language impairment, and behavioural/psychiatric manifestations (psychosis, irritability, agitation, combativeness, wandering) in 80% of patients. Motor symptoms (gait disturbance, dysphagia, incontinence, myoclonus, seizures) occur late.

The progression of AD follows a predictable trajectory. Social cognition is preserved until late in the disease course. Behavioral and psychiatric manifestations are a major source of caregiver burden and often the primary reason for institutionalization. Acetylcholinesterase inhibitors and appropriate psychosocial interventions can reduce this burden.

**Q:** What genetic mutations are associated with Frontotemporal Dementia (FTD)?

**A:** MAPT gene (tau protein), PGRN gene (progranulin), C9ORF72 gene (associated with FTD-ALS), TARDBP gene (TDP-43), VCP gene, and CHMP2B gene. 40% of FTD patients have a family history of early-onset NCD.

FTD has a strong genetic component compared to other dementias. The C9ORF72 hexanucleotide repeat expansion is the most common genetic cause of both FTD and ALS, explaining the FTD-ALS overlap syndrome. Histologically, FTD shows inclusions of either tau or TDP-43 protein.

**Q:** How does Down syndrome predispose to Alzheimer's disease?

**A:** Down syndrome (trisomy 21) involves three copies of chromosome 21, which carries the amyloid precursor protein (APP) gene. This leads to overproduction of APP and consequently increased β-amyloid, predisposing to early-onset AD (typically by age 40).

The genetic basis for early AD in Down syndrome validates the amyloid cascade hypothesis. People with Down syndrome develop AD pathology (amyloid plaques and neurofibrillary tangles) decades earlier than the general population. This also explains why familial AD is associated with APP mutations on chromosome 21.

**Q:** What is the prognosis of major NCD due to Alzheimer's disease, and what is the most common cause of death?

**A:** Mean survival after diagnosis is approximately 10 years, reflecting the advanced age of most patients rather than the disease course itself. Most common cause of death is aspiration (aspiration pneumonia).

As AD progresses, patients develop dysphagia in the late stage, leading to aspiration of food and secretions and ultimately aspiration pneumonia. Other late-stage complications include urinary/fecal incontinence, immobility, pressure ulcers, and infections. Goals of care discussions and advance directives should be initiated early.

**Q:** What is the average survival for Dementia with Lewy Bodies (DLB) compared to Alzheimer's disease and Frontotemporal Dementia?

**A:** DLB: average survival 5–7 years. AD: mean survival ~10 years. FTD: median survival 6–11 years from symptom onset, 3–4 years from diagnosis.

DLB and FTD have shorter survival than AD. In FTD, survival is shorter and cognitive decline is faster than in typical AD. Vascular NCD prognosis depends heavily on vascular risk factor control. These prognostic differences inform discussions with patients and families about disease trajectory and planning.

**Q:** What is the minimum neurological observation period after mild TBI, and when is hospitalization indicated?

**A:** Minimum: hourly clinical observations for at least 4 hours post-injury. Hospitalization is indicated if: CT is abnormal, OR CT is normal but symptoms are not improving at 4–6 hours post-injury.

Early observation allows detection of deterioration from intracranial hemorrhage expansion or cerebral edema. Early rehabilitation (OT, PT, SLP, vestibular therapy, sleep hygiene) maximizes outcomes. Return to Play guidelines (www.thinkfirst.ca) must be followed for sport-related concussions in Canada.

**Q:** What is the risk of post-traumatic epilepsy after mild TBI and is prophylactic anticonvulsant therapy effective?

**A:** Approximately 2% risk of post-traumatic epilepsy after mild TBI. Prophylactic anticonvulsants are NOT effective in preventing post-traumatic epilepsy.

Post-traumatic epilepsy is a recognized complication of TBI. While phenytoin may reduce early post-traumatic seizures (within the first week), it does not prevent the development of late epilepsy. Levetiracetam is often used in moderate-severe TBI for early seizure prophylaxis in clinical practice.

**Q:** What investigations are recommended to exclude treatable causes of cognitive impairment in a patient with suspected NCD?

**A:** CBC (with MCV), glucose, TSH, vitamin B12, RBC folate, electrolytes, LFTs, renal function, lipids, serum calcium, CT head. As clinically indicated: VDRL, HIV, ANA, anti-dsDNA, ANCA, ceruloplasmin, copper, cortisol, toxicology, heavy metals. Consider SPECT or MRI.

A systematic approach ensures reversible causes are not missed. Vitamin B12 deficiency (causing subacute combined degeneration) and hypothyroidism are particularly important to exclude, as both are treatable. NPH (normal pressure hydrocephalus) presents with the classic triad of gait disturbance, incontinence, and dementia and is identified on CT/MRI.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of Vitamin B12 deficiency relevant to neurology?

**A:** Neurological: confusion/change in mental status (if advanced), decreased vibration sense, distal numbness and paresthesia, weakness with upper motor neuron findings (subacute combined degeneration). Hematological: macrocytic anemia, pallor, shortness of breath, fatigue, chest pain, palpitations. GI: diarrhea, anorexia.

Vitamin B12 deficiency causes subacute combined degeneration of the spinal cord, affecting the posterior columns (loss of vibration/proprioception) and corticospinal tracts (UMN signs). It is a reversible cause of cognitive impairment. Mean corpuscular volume (MCV) is elevated on CBC, providing a clue to the diagnosis.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for Myasthenia Gravis (MG)?
