**A:** Acetylcholinesterase inhibitors (e.g. pyridostigmine) are first-line. If ineffective, corticosteroids (e.g. prednisone) are the mainstay. Short-term immunomodulation (IVIG, plasmapheresis) is used for myasthenic crisis.

Pyridostigmine increases acetylcholine availability at the neuromuscular junction by inhibiting its degradation. Corticosteroids modulate the autoimmune response. IVIG and plasmapheresis provide rapid but short-lived benefit during crisis.

**Q:** What are the long-term immunosuppressive options used as steroid-sparing agents in Myasthenia Gravis?

**A:** Azathioprine, cyclophosphamide, and mycophenolate are used as steroid-sparing agents in long-term MG immunosuppression.

These agents reduce the immune-mediated destruction of acetylcholine receptors, allowing corticosteroid doses to be reduced and minimizing steroid side effects over the long term.

**Q:** What newer disease-specific agents are available for Myasthenia Gravis?

**A:** Eculizumab (complement inhibitor) and efgartigimod (neonatal Fc receptor blocker) are newer, more disease-specific therapies for MG.

Eculizumab inhibits complement-mediated destruction at the neuromuscular junction. Efgartigimod reduces circulating IgG antibody levels (including anti-AChR antibodies) by blocking the neonatal Fc receptor recycling pathway.

**Q:** When is thymectomy indicated in Myasthenia Gravis, and what is its success rate?

**A:** Thymectomy is an option in non-thymomatous, acetylcholine receptor antibody-positive generalized MG, with an 85% remission rate. A randomized trial showed thymectomy plus alternate-day prednisone reduced MG manifestations and prednisone requirements compared to prednisone alone.

The thymus is thought to harbour autoreactive T cells in MG. Thymectomy reduces the antigenic stimulus. The MGTX trial demonstrated clinical benefit over 3 years in AChR-antibody-positive, non-thymomatous generalized MG patients.

**Q:** What is the underlying pathophysiology of Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome (LEMS)?

**A:** LEMS is caused by autoimmune antibodies against presynaptic voltage-gated calcium channels (P/Q-type), leading to decreased acetylcholine release at the neuromuscular junction.

Unlike MG where antibodies target postsynaptic AChRs, LEMS antibodies impair presynaptic calcium influx needed to trigger vesicle fusion and ACh release. This results in weakness that paradoxically improves transiently with repetitive activity.

**Q:** What malignancy is most commonly associated with Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome?

**A:** Small cell carcinoma of the lung is associated with LEMS in 50-66% of cases.

LEMS is a paraneoplastic syndrome in the majority of cases. Small cell lung cancer cells express voltage-gated calcium channels, triggering an immune response that cross-reacts with presynaptic NMJ channels. All patients with LEMS should be screened for malignancy.

**Q:** How does Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome differ from Myasthenia Gravis clinically?

**A:** LEMS: proximal and lower limb muscles predominantly affected, reflexes diminished but increase after exercise, bulbar/ocular involvement in only 25%, prominent autonomic symptoms (dry mouth > impotence > constipation > blurred vision). MG: bulbar and ocular involvement in 90%, reflexes usually preserved, no autonomic symptoms.

The key distinguishing features are the pattern of weakness (LEMS predominantly proximal/lower limb), the incremental reflex response after exercise in LEMS (vs. fatigable weakness in MG), and the prominent autonomic involvement in LEMS. EMG shows incremental response on rapid repetitive stimulation in LEMS vs. decremental in MG.

**Q:** What is the electrodiagnostic finding that differentiates LEMS from MG on repetitive nerve stimulation?

**A:** LEMS shows an incremental (facilitating) response on rapid (>10 Hz) repetitive nerve stimulation and with exercise. MG shows a decremental response on slow repetitive stimulation.

In LEMS, repeated stimulation allows calcium to accumulate presynaptically, overcoming the channel deficiency and releasing more ACh (facilitation). In MG, the postsynaptic AChRs are depleted/blocked, leading to progressive decremental response.

**Q:** What antibody is used to confirm Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome?

**A:** Anti-P/Q-type voltage-gated calcium channel (VGCC) antibodies are present in 85-95% of patients with LEMS.

These antibodies are highly specific for LEMS and can be detected in both paraneoplastic and autoimmune (non-paraneoplastic) forms. Their presence supports the diagnosis and mandates workup for underlying malignancy, especially small cell lung cancer.

**Q:** How is botulism caused and what is the most common form?

**A:** Botulism is caused by Clostridium botulinum toxin, which can enter through wounds or by ingestion. Infantile botulism is the most common form, usually from ingestion of honey or corn syrup.

Botulinum toxin irreversibly cleaves SNARE proteins required for presynaptic ACh vesicle fusion. Infants lack the gut flora to prevent C. botulinum spore germination; hence honey and corn syrup are contraindicated in children under 1 year.

**Q:** Describe the typical clinical progression of botulism.

**A:** Symptoms begin 6-48 h after ingestion. Pattern: GI symptoms → bilateral cranial neuropathies (ptosis, extraocular weakness, dilated poorly reactive pupils, dysarthria, dysphagia) → symmetric descending weakness with absent/decreased reflexes → autonomic dysfunction → respiratory failure.

The descending pattern of paralysis and prominent cranial nerve involvement distinguish botulism from Guillain-Barré syndrome (ascending) and MG. Autonomic findings (dry mouth, constipation, urinary retention, orthostatic hypotension) reflect cholinergic blockade.

**Q:** What is the treatment for botulism?

**A:** Botulinum anti-toxin (good prognosis with prompt treatment) plus supportive therapy including monitoring of respiratory status and assessment for intubation.

Anti-toxin neutralizes circulating toxin but cannot reverse already-bound toxin. Supportive care is critical as respiratory failure is the main cause of death. CT/MRI is normal in botulism and is used to rule out intracranial lesions.

**Q:** What are the key clinical, biopsy, and laboratory features of polymyositis vs. dermatomyositis?

**A:** Polymyositis: proximal muscle weakness, myalgias, nasopharyngeal involvement; biopsy shows endomysial infiltrates and necrosis; elevated CK. Dermatomyositis: proximal muscle weakness, myalgias, characteristic rashes (heliotrope, Gottron's papules), can be paraneoplastic; biopsy shows perifascicular atrophy; elevated CK.

The distinction is important because dermatomyositis has characteristic skin findings and higher malignancy association. Biopsy pattern differs: endomysial in polymyositis vs. perifascicular in dermatomyositis, reflecting different immune effectors (cytotoxic T cells vs. complement-mediated).

**Q:** What is inclusion body myositis and how does it differ from polymyositis?

**A:** Inclusion body myositis features weakness of quadriceps AND deep finger flexors (distal involvement), mildly elevated CK, and biopsy showing inclusion bodies. It differs from polymyositis in its distal involvement pattern, older age of onset, and poor response to immunosuppression.

The asymmetric distal involvement (especially deep finger flexors and quadriceps) is the hallmark. Inclusion bodies on biopsy are pathognomonic. Unlike polymyositis, inclusion body myositis does not respond well to steroids or immunosuppression.

**Q:** What is the genetic basis and inheritance of Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1?

**A:** Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 is caused by an unstable CTG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the myotonic dystrophy protein kinase gene (DMPK) at chromosome 19q13.3. It is autosomal dominant with genetic anticipation (increasing severity with successive generations).

The number of CTG repeats correlates with disease severity. Genetic anticipation occurs because the repeat tends to expand further with each transmission, especially maternally. This is the most common adult muscular dystrophy (prevalence 3-5/100,000).

**Q:** What are the multisystem features of Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1?

**A:** Distal > proximal weakness (steppage gait), myotonia (percussion myotonia of thenar muscles), cardiac (90% have conduction defects: 1° heart block, atrial arrhythmias), respiratory (hypoventilation), ocular (subcapsular cataracts, retinal degeneration), DM, infertility, testicular atrophy, frontal baldness, facial/levator/masticatory muscle atrophy.

The multisystem involvement distinguishes myotonic dystrophy from other muscular dystrophies. Cardiac disease and respiratory failure are leading causes of death. EMG shows electrical myotonia with waxing/waning discharges ('dive-bomber' sound).

**Q:** How is myotonia elicited on physical examination in myotonic dystrophy?

**A:** Percussion myotonia is elicited by tapping on the thenar muscles with a reflex hammer; delayed muscle relaxation (myotonia) is observed. EMG shows electrical myotonia with waxing and waning discharges that sound like a 'dive-bomber'.

Myotonia refers to delayed muscle relaxation after contraction. Patients may also report difficulty releasing a handshake. The distribution of weakness in myotonic dystrophy is distal > proximal, the reverse of most other myopathies.

**Q:** What is the treatment for myotonia in Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1?

**A:** Myotonia is treated with mexiletine (first choice) or phenytoin. There is no cure for the underlying disease; management is supportive.

Mexiletine is a sodium channel blocker that reduces the repetitive muscle membrane depolarizations causing myotonia. Phenytoin can be used as an alternative. Overall prognosis depends on age of onset and number of CTG repeats; death typically occurs around age 50.

**Q:** What are the red flag features in headache evaluation ('SNOOP4') that indicate the need for neuroimaging?

**A:** SNOOP4: Systemic symptoms/secondary risk factors (fever, weight loss, immunocompromised); Neurological symptoms; Onset sudden (thunderclap); Older age (new headache >50 yr); Pattern change; Positional variation; Precipitated by Valsalva/cough; Pregnancy (venous sinus thrombosis, IIH, preeclampsia).

These red flags identify headaches that may represent serious secondary causes requiring urgent investigation (CT/MRI and possibly LP). If CT is negative but SAH or meningitis is clinically suspected, lumbar puncture must be performed.

**Q:** Compare tension-type, migraine, and cluster headaches in terms of location, quality, duration, sex bias, and associated features.

**A:** Tension: bilateral frontal/nuchal-occipital, band-like constant, minutes-days, F>M, no nausea/photophobia. Migraine: unilateral > bilateral fronto-temporal, pulsating/throbbing, hours-days, F>M (post-pubertal), N/V + photo/phonophobia + aura. Cluster: retro-orbital/supra-orbital, constant/stabbing, 10 min-2h, M>F, red/watery eye, eyelid/forehead swelling, nasal congestion, Horner's, wakes from sleep.

Cluster headache is the most severe (wakes from sleep) and has distinctive autonomic features (ipsilateral lacrimation, conjunctival injection, nasal congestion, Horner's). Migraine's pulsating quality and photo/phonophobia are diagnostic. Tension is mildest and most common (30-40% prevalence).

**Q:** What are the acute (abortive) and prophylactic treatments for migraine headache?

**A:** Acute/Abortive: NSAIDs, triptans, ergotamine, anti-emetics, CGRP antagonists. Prophylactic: beta-blockers (propranolol, timolol - level A), amitriptyline (TCA - level A), flunarizine (CCB - level A), valproate (level A), topiramate (level A), CGRP antibodies (e.g. galcanezumab), botulinum toxin.

Triptans are 5-HT1B/1D agonists that cause vasoconstriction and reduce CGRP release. Prophylaxis is indicated if attacks are frequent or disabling. Valproate and topiramate each reduce headache days by ~4 and ~1 day/month respectively and double the likelihood of >50% frequency reduction.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for migraine headache?

**A:** ≥5 attacks, each lasting >4 hours, with at least 2 of: unilateral, pulsating, moderate-severe intensity, aggravated by routine activity; AND at least 1 of: nausea/vomiting, photophobia/phonophobia/osmophobia; not better explained by another diagnosis.

These are the International Headache Society (IHS) criteria. Migraine occurs in 18% of females and 6% of males; frequency decreases with age and at menopause. About 20% of migraines are associated with aura caused by cortical spreading depression.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of migraine aura?

**A:** Migraine aura is caused by a depolarizing wave of 'cortical spreading depression' across the cerebral cortex (e.g., visual symptoms result from wave through occipital cortex), associated with possible vasoconstriction/dilation. Auras are positive or negative phenomena lasting 5-60 minutes.

About 20% of migraineurs experience aura. Cortical spreading depression is a slowly propagating wave of neuronal and glial depolarization followed by suppression. This is distinct from TIA/stroke (negative symptoms, no spreading quality) and seizures (shorter duration, typically without spreading march).

**Q:** What are the contraindications and key side effects of propranolol and valproate in migraine prophylaxis?

**A:** Propranolol (level A): contraindicated in asthma, DM (masks hypoglycemia), CHF; side effects: fatigue, depression, light-headedness. Valproate (level A): contraindicated in liver/pancreatic disease; side effects: weight gain, elevated liver enzymes, hyperammonemia, tremor, alopecia; teratogenic (neural tube defect).

Both have strong evidence (level A) for migraine prevention. Valproate's teratogenicity makes it problematic in women of childbearing age. Topiramate (level A) causes weight loss (vs. weight gain with valproate), paresthesia, cognitive effects, and rare acute angle-closure glaucoma.

**Q:** What are the acute and prophylactic treatments for cluster headache?

**A:** Acute: sumatriptan (nasal or SC injection). Prophylaxis: verapamil (first-line), lithium, methysergide, prednisolone, CGRP antibody (galcanezumab). Patients walk around during attacks (vs. lying still in migraine).

Cluster headache affects 1% of the population with M>F predominance, occurring in daily attacks for weeks to months (often nocturnal/early AM). A randomized trial showed galcanezumab reduced weekly cluster attack frequency by 8.7 vs. 5.2 in placebo.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of trigeminal neuralgia and its first-line treatment?

**A:** Recurrent sudden, excruciating, unilateral, paroxysmal, electric-shock pain in trigeminal territory (V3>V2>>V1); triggered by touching face, eating, talking, cold wind, shaving. First-line treatment: carbamazepine or oxcarbazepine. Second-line: baclofen or lamotrigine. Refractory: microvascular decompression.

Classical trigeminal neuralgia is caused by compression of the trigeminal nerve by a tortuous vessel (usually superior cerebellar artery). Sensory exam is usually normal; if abnormal, suspect secondary cause. MRI is required to rule out structural causes (mass, MS).

**Q:** What is postherpetic neuralgia and how is it prevented and treated?

**A:** Pain persisting in the region of a herpes zoster cutaneous outbreak; constant deep ache, burning, lancinating pain, and allodynia. Prevention: varicella vaccine (childhood); Shingrix® (>50 yr, more efficacious) or Zostavax® (>60 yr, live). Treatment: amitriptyline (TCA), pregabalin/gabapentin, opiates, lidocaine patch, topical capsaicin; early antivirals (famciclovir/valacyclovir) during acute zoster. Corticosteroids during acute zoster do NOT prevent postherpetic neuralgia.

Postherpetic neuralgia results from destruction of sensory ganglion neurons during herpes zoster reactivation. Incidence increases with age (2% <60 yr, 19% >70 yr). Hutchinson's sign (tip of nose involvement) predicts corneal involvement when V1 is affected.

**Q:** What are the Canadian Pain Society first-line treatments for neuropathic pain?

**A:** First-line (Canadian Pain Society, 2014): gabapentinoids (pregabalin, gabapentin), TCAs, and SNRIs. Second-line: tramadol, opioid analgesics. Third-line: cannabinoids. Fourth-line: methadone, anticonvulsants (lamotrigine, lacosamide), tapentadol, botulinum toxin.

This stepwise approach is used regardless of neuropathic pain etiology. The goal is to identify and treat the underlying cause first. Non-pharmacologic therapies (CBT, physiotherapy) and surgical options (dorsal column neurostimulator, DBS) complement pharmacotherapy.

**Q:** Define allodynia and hyperalgesia, and in what condition are they characteristically found?

**A:** Allodynia: pain response to a non-noxious stimulus. Hyperalgesia: exaggerated pain response to a noxious stimulus. Both are characteristic features of neuropathic pain and complex regional pain syndrome (CRPS).

These phenomena reflect sensitization of the peripheral and central somatosensory system. They are key diagnostic features of neuropathic pain and CRPS, distinguishing them from nociceptive pain where the pain response is proportional to stimulus intensity.

**Q:** What are the Budapest Criteria for diagnosing Complex Regional Pain Syndrome (CRPS)?

**A:** 1. Continuing regional pain disproportionate to inciting event. 2. Symptoms in ≥3 of 4 categories AND signs in ≥2 of 4 categories: sensory (hyperesthesia/allodynia), vasomotor (temperature/colour asymmetry), sudomotor/edema (edema, sweating changes), motor/trophic (decreased ROM, motor dysfunction, trophic changes). 3. No other diagnosis better explains the findings.

CRPS is a clinical diagnosis. Bone scintigraphy within 5 months of onset may support the diagnosis but a negative test does not exclude it. MRI can rule out other causes. Early mobilization after injury/stroke prevents CRPS.

**Q:** How are CRPS Type I and Type II differentiated?

**A:** CRPS Type I (reflex sympathetic dystrophy): preceded by minor injuries or remote body area lesions, WITHOUT identifiable nerve injury. CRPS Type II (causalgia): preceded by identifiable peripheral nerve injury.

Both types present with regional pain, autonomic changes, and trophic features. The key distinction is the presence of a defined nerve injury in Type II. Treatment principles are similar: early mobilization, PT/OT, topical agents, gabapentinoids, and sympathetic blockade for refractory cases.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for painful diabetic neuropathy?

**A:** Pregabalin is first-line. Second-line options include venlafaxine, duloxetine, amitriptyline, gabapentin, valproate, and rarely opioids or capsaicin.

Pain is neuropathic if it is present at rest, improves with walking, is sharp/tingling, and more prominent in feet than calves. This distinguishes it from vascular (claudication) pain, which worsens with walking. Tight glycemic control addresses the underlying cause.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria and epidemiology of insomnia?

**A:** Insomnia: difficulty initiating/maintaining sleep or waking earlier than desired (non-restorative sleep) despite adequate opportunity. Common secondary causes include psychiatric disorders (80% of psychiatric patients), neurologic disorders, sleep disorders (RLS, OSA), medical conditions, drugs/toxins. Treatment: CBT (first-line), sleep hygiene, stimulus control, sleep restriction, melatonin.

Cognitive behavioural therapy for insomnia (CBT-I) is the most evidence-based long-term treatment. Medications (e.g., benzodiazepines, Z-drugs) are second-line and used short-term. Fatal familial insomnia is a rare prion protein mutation causing autonomic dysfunction and intractable insomnia.

**Q:** What are the types, diagnosis, and treatment of sleep apnea?

**A:** Types: Obstructive (airway collapse in deep/REM sleep), Central (no respiratory effort >10s; etiology: heart failure, opiates, brainstem pathology, myotonic dystrophy), Mixed. Diagnosis: polysomnography (home or in-hospital); AHI ≥5. Treatment: CPAP (mainstay), weight loss, positional therapy, dental devices, surgery; ensure driving safety.

Sleep apnea affects >6% of Canadians. Risk factors: older age, obesity, male sex, craniofacial/upper airway abnormalities. Significant comorbidities include HTN, stroke, heart failure, and increased accident risk. Central sleep apnea has distinct etiologies requiring targeted management.

**Q:** What are the clinical features, associations, and treatment of Restless Legs Syndrome (RLS)?

**A:** RLS: urge to move legs with uncomfortable sensations, beginning/worsening with rest, partially or totally relieved with movement, worse in evening/night. Associated with iron deficiency, neuropathy, PD, alcohol, pregnancy, end-stage renal disease. 90% also have periodic limb movement disorder. Treatment: iron (target ferritin >75 μg/L), gabapentinoids, dopamine agonists (pramipexole, rotigotine), benzodiazepines (clonazepam), opioids if refractory.

Iron deficiency is a key reversible cause; ferritin should be checked and repleted to >75 μg/L. Dopamine agonists are effective but may cause augmentation (worsening of symptoms with treatment). The evening predominance reflects circadian variation in dopamine levels.

**Q:** What are the four cardinal features of narcolepsy and how is it diagnosed?

**A:** 1. Excessive daytime sleepiness (all narcolepsy). 2. Cataplexy (pathognomonic): sudden loss of muscle tone triggered by emotion. 3. Sleep paralysis: inability to move upon waking. 4. Hypnagogic (falling asleep) and hypnopompic (waking) hallucinations. Diagnosis: clinical history + multiple sleep latency test (MSLT) showing sleep latency <8 min and REM onset within 15 min of sleep on ≥2/4 naps.

Narcolepsy results from autoimmune destruction of orexin/hypocretin-producing neurons in the hypothalamus. Prevalence is 1:2000, onset in adolescence/early adulthood. Cataplexy is pathognomonic but not present in all narcolepsy cases.

**Q:** What is the treatment for narcolepsy including agents for excessive daytime sleepiness and cataplexy?

**A:** Excessive daytime sleepiness: sleep hygiene, scheduled brief naps, modafinil (non-amphetamine stimulant, first-line), methylphenidate. Cataplexy: TCAs, SSRIs, sodium oxybate. Driving restrictions apply.

Modafinil is preferred over amphetamines due to lower abuse potential. Sodium oxybate (GHB) is effective for both EDS and cataplexy. Restricted driving is important as narcolepsy is a life-long disorder with significant safety implications.

**Q:** What are the mitochondrial myopathy syndromes MERRF, MELAS, and Kearns-Sayre and their key features?

**A:** MERRF (mitochondrial encephalomyopathy with ragged red fibres): myoclonus, generalized seizures, dementia, myopathy; biopsy: ragged red fibres; elevated lactate. MELAS (lactic acidosis and stroke-like episodes): pediatric onset, stroke-like symptoms, episodic vomiting, dementia. Kearns-Sayre: progressive ophthalmoplegia, retinal pigment degeneration, cardiac conduction abnormalities.

Mitochondrial myopathies are maternally inherited. 'Ragged red fibres' on Gomori trichrome stain represent abnormal mitochondrial accumulation in subsarcolemmal regions. Elevated lactate reflects impaired oxidative phosphorylation causing lactic acidosis.

**Q:** What is McArdle's disease and what are its key clinical and laboratory features?

**A:** McArdle's disease (myophosphorylase deficiency) is a hereditary metabolic myopathy presenting with exercise-induced myalgias, cramping, and myoglobinuria. Key lab: elevated serum/urinary myoglobin; elevated lactate post-exercise.

McArdle's is caused by absence of muscle glycogen phosphorylase, preventing glycogen breakdown during exercise. A 'second wind' phenomenon occurs as fatty acids become available. It is the prototypical glycogen storage disease affecting muscle.

**Q:** What are the differences between Duchenne and Becker muscular dystrophies?

**A:** Duchenne: early onset, rapidly progressive proximal weakness, absent dystrophin, calf pseudohypertrophy, X-linked; genetic testing + absent dystrophin on analysis. Becker: later onset, slower progression, reduced (not absent) dystrophin; genetic testing + reduced dystrophin analysis.

Both result from mutations in the dystrophin gene (Xp21), but Duchenne involves frameshift mutations causing complete absence, while Becker involves in-frame mutations preserving some dystrophin function. Calf pseudohypertrophy results from fatty/fibrous replacement of muscle.

**Q:** What are the key features of temporal arteritis (giant cell arteritis) as a secondary headache cause?

**A:** Age >60 yr, temporal location, variable severity, jaw claudication (provoking), associated polymyalgia rheumatica, visual loss risk, temporal artery changes (firm, nodular, incompressible, tender). Management: urgent prednisone, CT/MRI; see Rheumatology (RH22).

Temporal arteritis (GCA) is a vasculitis primarily affecting medium/large vessels, most commonly the temporal artery. Visual loss (ischemic optic neuropathy) is a feared complication requiring immediate corticosteroid treatment. ESR and CRP are elevated; temporal artery biopsy is diagnostic.

**Q:** What are the distinguishing features of increased intracranial pressure (ICP) as a secondary headache cause?

**A:** Gradual onset, worse nocturnal and in the AM, worse with lying down/Valsalva/exertion, associated focal neurological symptoms, decreased LOC, papilledema on exam. Etiology: tumour, CSVT, idiopathic intracranial hypertension (IIH), malignant HTN. Management: CT/MRI with gadolinium and treatment to reduce pressure.

Increased ICP headache worsens with maneuvers that raise intracranial pressure (lying down, Valsalva, coughing). Papilledema is the key physical finding. This contrasts with low-pressure headache (post-LP) which worsens with standing.

**Q:** What are the stages of an uncomplicated migraine attack?

**A:** 1. Prodrome (hours to days before headache): mood changes, fatigue, food cravings. 2. Aura (in 20%): visual/sensory/motor symptoms lasting 5-60 min. 3. Headache: unilateral, pulsating, moderate-severe, with N/V, photo/phonophobia, 4-72h. 4. Postdrome: fatigue, cognitive slowing.

Recognition of these stages helps in appropriate timing of abortive therapy. Triptans are most effective when taken early in the headache phase (not during aura). The postdrome ('migraine hangover') can be as debilitating as the headache itself.

**Q:** What parasomnia is associated with Parkinson's disease in the elderly, and what sleep disorder is associated with parasomnias in children?

**A:** In the elderly, REM sleep behaviour disorder (RBD) is associated with Parkinson's disease. In children, slow wave sleep arousals (sleepwalking/night terrors) may be associated with sleep-disordered breathing (e.g., obstructive sleep apnea from tonsillar hypertrophy).

RBD involves loss of normal REM sleep muscle atonia, causing patients to act out dreams. It is a prodromal marker of synucleinopathies (PD, Lewy body dementia, MSA). Clonazepam is used for RBD. Tonsillectomy is beneficial for children with obstructive-related parasomnias.

**Q:** What is the zoster vaccine efficacy data from the landmark NEJM 2005 trial?

**A:** The live attenuated Oka/Merck VZV (zoster) vaccine in adults ≥60 years: reduced herpes zoster illness burden by 61.1% (P<0.001), incidence of herpes zoster infection by 51.3% (P<0.001), and incidence of postherpetic neuralgia (PHN) by 66.5% (P<0.001). (Oxman et al., NEJM 2005)

This landmark trial (38,546 adults ≥60 years) established the benefit of zoster vaccination in older adults. It led to widespread vaccination recommendations. A recombinant adjuvanted herpes zoster subunit vaccine (Shingrix) was subsequently shown to have higher efficacy (~97% in adults 50-69 years, Lal et al., NEJM 2015) and is now the preferred vaccine in Canada, replacing the live attenuated vaccine (Zostavax). PHN risk increases significantly with age.

**Q:** What are the two major types of stroke and their approximate proportions?

**A:** Ischemic (~80%) and hemorrhagic (~20%).

Ischemic stroke results from arterial thrombosis (large vessel or small vessel/lacunar), cardioembolism, or systemic hypoperfusion. Hemorrhagic stroke includes intracerebral hemorrhage (ICH) and subarachnoid hemorrhage (SAH). Recognizing the distinction is critical because treatment differs dramatically (thrombolytics for ischemic vs. risk of harm in hemorrhagic).

**Q:** What is a TIA and how does it differ from a stroke?

**A:** A TIA is a transient (<24 h) episode of neurological dysfunction caused by focal brain, spinal cord, or retinal ischemia WITHOUT acute infarction on CT or MRI. Stroke involves permanent tissue injury confirmed by neuroimaging.

The key distinguishing feature of TIA vs. stroke is the absence of infarction on imaging. TIA may present with amaurosis fugax (transient monocular painless vision loss). TIA is a medical emergency because it predicts high short-term stroke risk, necessitating urgent evaluation and secondary prevention.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of large vessel ischemic stroke?

**A:** Atherosclerosis.

Large vessel ischemic stroke involves stenosis or occlusion of the internal carotid artery, vertebral artery, basilar artery, or anterior/middle/posterior cerebral arteries. Atherosclerosis is the most common underlying process, causing insufficient blood flow beyond the lesion (hemodynamic stroke). Other causes include dissection and vasculitis.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of lacunar stroke and which structures are most commonly affected?

**A:** Chronic hypertension and diabetes cause vessel wall thickening and decreased luminal diameter in small penetrating arteries. Most commonly affects the basal ganglia, internal capsule, and thalamus.

Lacunar infarcts result from disease of small deep penetrating arteries. The five classic lacunar syndromes are: pure motor hemiparesis, pure sensory loss, sensorimotor stroke, ataxic hemiparesis, and dysarthria-clumsy hand syndrome. HTN and DM are the primary risk factors.

**Q:** What is the most common cardiac source of cardioembolic stroke?

**A:** Atrial fibrillation.

Cardioembolic stroke occurs when thrombus from a cardiac source blocks cerebral arterial blood flow. Atrial fibrillation is the most common cause. Other sources include rheumatic valve disease, prosthetic heart valves, recent MI, and infectious endocarditis. Cardioembolic strokes may develop hemorrhagic transformation within 1 week of initial infarction.

**Q:** What are the classic clinical features of a middle cerebral artery (MCA) stroke?

**A:** Contralateral face and arm weakness/sensory deficit, cortical sensory loss, contralateral homonymous hemianopsia or quadrantanopia, aphasia (dominant hemisphere), neglect/anosognosia (non-dominant hemisphere), and eye deviation toward the side of the lesion (away from the weak side).

MCA is the most common artery involved in ischemic stroke. It supplies the lateral cerebral cortex including motor and sensory cortex for face and arm, but not leg (which is ACA territory). Left (dominant) hemisphere MCA involvement causes aphasia; right (non-dominant) causes neglect and anosognosia.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of anterior cerebral artery (ACA) stroke?

**A:** Contralateral leg weakness and/or sensory deficit, and neurocognitive deficits (e.g., abulia, agitation, emotional lability).

ACA supplies the medial cerebral cortex including the motor and sensory areas for the leg. Therefore ACA stroke causes contralateral leg > arm weakness, which contrasts with MCA (arm > leg). Frontal lobe involvement causes behavioral changes such as abulia (lack of motivation) and emotional lability.

**Q:** What is Wallenberg (lateral medullary) syndrome and which artery is involved?

**A:** Posterior inferior cerebellar artery (PICA) occlusion. Features: ipsilateral ataxia, ipsilateral Horner's syndrome, ipsilateral facial sensory loss, contralateral limb pain and temperature loss, nystagmus, vertigo, N/V, dysphagia, dysarthria, and hiccups.

Lateral medullary syndrome affects the lateral medulla including the spinothalamic tract (contralateral pain/temp loss), trigeminal nucleus (ipsilateral facial sensory loss), vestibular nuclei (nystagmus/vertigo), nucleus ambiguus (dysphagia/dysarthria), and sympathetic fibers (ipsilateral Horner's). Crossed sensory findings (ipsilateral face, contralateral body) are pathognomonic.

**Q:** What are the neurological findings of a proximal basilar artery occlusion?

**A:** Impaired eye movements, vertical nystagmus, reactive miosis, hemi- or quadriplegia, dysarthria, ataxia, locked-in syndrome, and coma.

Proximal basilar artery occlusion (usually thrombotic) affects the pons and brainstem structures. Locked-in syndrome is a devastating complication where the patient is conscious but cannot move or speak. Distal basilar (top of the basilar syndrome, usually embolic) causes somnolence, memory/behaviour abnormalities, and oculomotor deficits.

**Q:** What are the five classic lacunar stroke syndromes?

**A:** 1. Pure motor hemiparesis (posterior limb of internal capsule or ventral pons)
2. Pure sensory loss (ventral thalamic)
3. Sensorimotor stroke (weakness and numbness without cortical signs)
4. Ataxic hemiparesis (ventral pons or internal capsule)
5. Dysarthria-clumsy hand syndrome (ventral pons or genu of internal capsule)

Lacunar syndromes result from small vessel disease in deep brain structures. Key feature is absence of cortical signs (no aphasia, apraxia, or visual field loss). The location within the internal capsule or pons determines which syndrome manifests. Distinguishing lacunar from cortical strokes affects prognosis and management.

**Q:** What is the NIHSS and what score indicates a severe stroke?

**A:** The National Institute of Health Stroke Scale (NIHSS) is a standardized 11-item clinical examination (scored 0-42) assessing LOC, visual system, facial palsy, motor, sensory, cerebellar function, and language abilities. Score >21 = severe stroke; 16-20 = moderate-to-severe; 5-15 = moderate; 1-4 = mild; 0 = no stroke.

NIHSS quantifies stroke severity and guides treatment decisions including eligibility for thrombolysis and thrombectomy. It is used acutely and to monitor response to therapy. For secondary prevention, high-risk TIA or minor stroke is defined as NIHSS <4.

**Q:** What is the ASPECTS score and what cutoff predicts high risk of bleeding with tPA?

**A:** ASPECTS (Alberta Stroke Program Early CT Score) is a 10-point quantitative score to assess early ischemic changes on CT. Score <4/10 signifies high risk of bleeding with tPA. Score 10/10 is normal.

ASPECTS subtracts 1 point for each abnormal structure within the ischemic hemisphere: caudate nucleus, lentiform, insula, internal capsule, and MCA regions 1-6. Lower scores indicate larger infarct cores and higher hemorrhagic transformation risk with thrombolysis, making it important for treatment decisions.

**Q:** What are the early CT signs of acute ischemic stroke?

**A:** Loss of cortical white-grey differentiation, sulcal effacement (mass effect decreasing sulcal visibility), hypodensity of parenchyma, insular ribbon sign, and hyperdense middle cerebral artery sign.

CT may appear normal in the first several hours after stroke onset, especially in posterior circulation strokes. The hyperdense MCA sign indicates thrombus in the MCA. CT is performed urgently to exclude hemorrhage before thrombolysis. MRI is more sensitive but less practical in the acute setting.

**Q:** What is the time window for IV tPA in acute ischemic stroke and what is the current recommended agent?

**A:** tPA (alteplase) or tenecteplase (TNK) should be given within 4.5 hours of acute ischemic stroke onset if clinical indications are met and there are no contraindications.

The NINDS trial established the 3-hour window; ECASS3 extended this to 4.5 hours. Current standard of care is IV tPA + mechanical thrombectomy for large vessel occlusions. If tPA is given, delay antiplatelet/anticoagulation by 24 hours. Blood work should only delay treatment if anticoagulant use, low platelet count, or abnormal electrolytes are suspected.

**Q:** What are absolute contraindications to tPA in acute ischemic stroke?

**A:** Hemorrhage on brain imaging, any condition that could increase the risk of hemorrhage (e.g., active bleeding, recent surgery, prior ICH), conditions increasing hemorrhagic risk, and prior stroke.

Relative contraindications include: head/spine trauma or surgery in preceding 3 months, major surgery in preceding 14 days, arterial puncture at non-compressible site, another acute neurological condition (e.g., post-ictal Todd's paralysis), blood glucose <2.7 or >22.2 mmol/L, INR >1.7, aPTT elevated, platelet count <100x10⁹/mm³, and CT signs of extensive infarction.

**Q:** What are the time windows for mechanical thrombectomy in ischemic stroke?

**A:** <6 hours: with small-to-moderate ischemic core on CT. 6-24 hours: CT perfusion used to select patients meeting DAWN trial criteria (mismatch between clinical deficit and infarct size). For basilar occlusions, weigh risks and benefits individually.

Current standard of care is IV tPA + mechanical thrombectomy for proximal anterior circulation occlusion. The DAWN trial demonstrated benefit of thrombectomy 6-24 hours after stroke in patients with clinical deficit-infarct mismatch. Multiple RCTs (MR CLEAN, ESCAPE) confirmed benefit <6 hours.

**Q:** What is the recommended acute antiplatelet therapy for TIA or minor stroke if tPA is NOT given?

**A:** Loading dose ASA 160 mg chewed at presentation. For high-risk TIA or minor stroke (NIHSS <4): dual antiplatelet therapy with ASA + clopidogrel (300 mg loading dose) for 21 days (ASA 81 mg + clopidogrel 75 mg), followed by antiplatelet monotherapy.

If patient is intolerant to ASA, use clopidogrel 300 mg. Long-term combined ASA and clopidogrel is NOT recommended for secondary prevention beyond 21 days due to increased bleeding risk and mortality, unless there is an alternate indication (e.g., coronary drug-eluting stent). The POINT trial supported dual antiplatelet therapy for 21 days in high-risk TIA/minor stroke.

**Q:** What are the blood pressure targets in acute ischemic stroke for thrombolysed vs. non-thrombolysed patients?

**A:** Non-thrombolysed: withhold antihypertensives for 48-72 hours (permissive hypertension) unless sBP >220 mmHg or dBP >120 mmHg, or acute MI/renal failure/aortic dissection (use IV labetalol if needed). Thrombolysed: target BP <180/105 mmHg.

Acutely elevated BP is necessary to maintain perfusion to the ischemic penumbra. Most patients with acute cerebral infarction are initially hypertensive and their BP improves within 1-2 days. Aggressive lowering can extend the infarct by compromising penumbral perfusion.

**Q:** What are the CHADS2 score components and what score warrants anticoagulation in atrial fibrillation?

**A:** C=CHF (1), H=HTN sBP >160 mmHg/treated HTN (1), A=Age >75 yr (1), D=DM (1), S=prior Stroke/TIA (2). Score ≥2 (high risk, 4-18.2% annual stroke risk) warrants anticoagulation. Score 1 (intermediate): anticoagulant or antiplatelet (patient-specific). Score 0 (low risk): antiplatelet. Current Canadian guidelines recommend virtually all AFib patients without contraindication be anticoagulated.

Current 2014 Stroke guidelines recommend anticoagulation for virtually all patients with AFib unless contraindicated. DOACs (dabigatran, apixaban, rivaroxaban, edoxaban) are alternatives to warfarin (INR 2-3) and generally have lower ICH risk. DOACs should NOT be used with mechanical heart valves or valvular AF.

**Q:** What are the reversal agents for dabigatran and the Factor Xa inhibitors (apixaban, rivaroxaban)?

**A:** Dabigatran: idarucizumab (Praxbind®). Apixaban and rivaroxaban: andexanet alfa (Andexanet®).

Reversal agents are essential for life-threatening bleeding or urgent surgery. Dabigatran is a direct thrombin inhibitor; idarucizumab is a monoclonal antibody fragment that binds dabigatran with high affinity. Andexanet alfa is a modified Factor Xa decoy protein that binds and sequesters Factor Xa inhibitors.

**Q:** What does the NASCET trial conclude about carotid endarterectomy?

**A:** Carotid endarterectomy clearly benefits symptomatic patients with severe (70-99%) carotid stenosis and to a lesser degree those with moderate (50-69%) stenosis. 2-year cumulative risk of ipsilateral stroke: 26% medical vs. 9% surgical (17% absolute risk reduction).

NASCET (1991) established the benefit of carotid endarterectomy in symptomatic carotid stenosis. For asymptomatic stenosis >60%, endarterectomy reduces stroke risk by 1%/year but carries 5% complication risk, making it controversial. The CREST trial showed similar composite outcomes between carotid stenting and endarterectomy, but stenting has higher periprocedural stroke risk while endarterectomy has higher periprocedural MI risk.

**Q:** What are the BP targets for primary prevention of stroke in hypertensive patients?

**A:** <140/90 mmHg in general. sBP <120 mmHg for high-risk patients without DM (SPRINT trial). <130/80 mmHg for patients with DM or renal disease.

For secondary prevention, combination ACE inhibitor + thiazide diuretic is recommended in patients with previous stroke/TIA (PROGRESS trial). Ramipril 10 mg daily is effective in high-cardiovascular-risk patients (HOPE trial), reducing stroke risk by 32% (4.9% vs. 3.4%) beyond its antihypertensive effect.

**Q:** What does the SPARCL trial conclude about statins after stroke or TIA?

**A:** High-dose atorvastatin (80 mg/day) in patients with recent stroke/TIA and LDL 2.6-4.9 mmol/L reduced fatal or nonfatal stroke by 2.2% absolute risk reduction over 5 years (11.2% vs. 13.1%), though hemorrhagic stroke rates were higher with atorvastatin.

SPARCL established statins for secondary stroke prevention. Target LDL <2 mmol/L (or 50% LDL reduction). Despite the increased hemorrhagic stroke risk, atorvastatin reduced overall stroke incidence. Lower doses are acceptable if high dose is intolerable. Statins are also recommended for primary prevention in patients with CAD or high cardiovascular risk.

**Q:** What are the most common sites for hypertensive intracerebral hemorrhage?

**A:** Putamen, caudate nucleus, thalamus, cerebellum, and pons.

Hypertensive ICH (most common type of hemorrhagic stroke) is due to chronic arteriosclerosis predisposing vessels to focal necrosis and pseudoaneurysm formation. The specific locations correspond to the deep penetrating arteries most susceptible to hypertensive damage. Early seizure activity occurs in 5-25% of patients after ICH.

**Q:** What are the BP targets for managing acute intracerebral hemorrhage (ICH) per 2020 Canadian Stroke Best Practice Guidelines?

**A:** sBP <140-160 mmHg for the first 24-48 hours post-ICH is reasonable (based on 2020 Canadian Stroke Best Practice Guidelines). No conclusive BP target ranges exist, and the INTERACT2 trial showed intensive lowering (target <140 mmHg) did not significantly reduce death or severe disability but may improve functional outcomes.

The INTERACT2 trial randomized 2839 patients with spontaneous ICH to intensive BP treatment (target <140 mmHg within 1 hour) vs. guideline-based (<180 mmHg). While death/major disability did not significantly differ, functional outcomes slightly favored intensive treatment. Current guidelines recommend sBP <140-160 mmHg as reasonable.

**Q:** What is cerebral venous sinus thrombosis (CVST) and what is its treatment?

**A:** CVST involves thrombosis of cerebral venous sinuses presenting with headache alone, seizures, focal neurologic deficits, decreased LOC, or cranial nerve palsies. Diagnosis by CT venography or MR venography. Treatment: anticoagulation with heparin initially, then warfarin.

CVST is an important differential for stroke and headache. Fever with decreased LOC, fluctuating symptoms, gradual onset, and absence of typical focal stroke signs suggest CVST. Unlike arterial stroke where anticoagulation is typically delayed, CVST is treated promptly with anticoagulation even in the presence of hemorrhagic infarction.

**Q:** What is multiple sclerosis (MS) and what are the key epidemiological features?

**A:** MS is an autoimmune chronic inflammatory disease of the CNS characterized by relapsing-remitting or progressive neurological symptoms due to inflammation, demyelination, and axonal degeneration. Onset 17-35 years; F:M = 3:1; global prevalence ~2.8 million; more common in regions with less sun exposure (Canada, Europe, US, New Zealand, SE Australia); linked to HLA-DRB1 gene and Epstein-Barr virus.

Canada has one of the highest rates of MS in the world. MS has polygenetic susceptibility (HLA-DRB1), 30% concordance in monozygotic twins, and 2-4% risk in offspring of affected parents. Vitamin D insufficiency (less sun exposure) is a key environmental risk factor.

**Q:** What are the four clinical patterns of MS?

**A:** 1. Relapsing-remitting MS (RRMS) — 85%: episodes of neurological dysfunction followed by recovery
2. Primary progressive MS (PPMS) — 10%: progressive from onset, no relapses
3. Secondary progressive MS (SPMS): initially RRMS, transitions to progressive course
4. Progressive relapsing MS (PRMS) — 5%: progressive from onset with acute relapses

Most RRMS eventually converts to SPMS. SPMS presents with progressive weakness and gait dysfunction with cerebellar findings. PPMS has poor prognosis, higher disability rates, and poor response to therapy. The Expanded Disability Status Scale (EDSS) scored 0-10 tracks disability progression.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for MS (McDonald Criteria 2017)?

**A:** Demonstration of dissemination in TIME and SPACE:
- Time: ≥2 attacks (≥24h, ≥30 days apart), OR simultaneous gadolinium-enhancing + non-enhancing MRI lesions, OR new T2/gadolinium lesion on follow-up MRI, OR CSF oligoclonal bands.
- Space: ≥1 T2 lesion in ≥2 of 4 CNS regions (periventricular, cortical/juxtacortical, infratentorial, spinal cord) OR second attack in different CNS region.

The McDonald criteria allow earlier diagnosis of MS using MRI and CSF findings. CSF oligoclonal bands are found in ~90% of MS patients. Clinically isolated syndrome (CIS) is the first episode suggestive of MS; early DMT reduces conversion to clinically definite MS.

**Q:** What are the classic MRI findings in MS?

**A:** Hyperintense lesions on T2-weighted MRI in periventricular, corpus callosum (Dawson's fingers), cerebellar peduncles, brainstem, juxtacortical, and dorsolateral spinal cord regions. Active lesions enhance with gadolinium. T1 'black holes' indicate permanent axonal loss. Cranial MRI is more sensitive than spinal MRI.

Dawson's fingers are periventricular lesions extending perpendicular into the corpus callosum — highly characteristic of MS. Gadolinium enhancement indicates blood-brain barrier disruption in active inflammation. CSF shows oligoclonal bands in 90% and increased IgG. Evoked potentials show delayed but well-preserved waveforms.

**Q:** What are Lhermitte's sign and Uhthoff's phenomenon in MS?

**A:** Lhermitte's sign: flexion of neck causes an electric shock sensation down the back into the limbs — suggestive of a dorsal cervical cord lesion. Uhthoff's phenomenon: worsening of MS symptoms in heat (fever, exercise, hot bath).

Both phenomena are highly characteristic of MS though not exclusive to it. Lhermitte's sign occurs because neck flexion stretches the demyelinated dorsal columns of the cervical spinal cord. Uhthoff's phenomenon occurs because elevated temperature impairs conduction in demyelinated axons, which have narrow safety margins for conduction.

**Q:** What is the treatment for an acute MS exacerbation?

**A:** Methylprednisolone 1000 mg IV daily x 3-7 days (no taper required) OR prednisone 1000-1250 mg PO daily x 3-7 days (no taper required). If poor response to corticosteroids, consider plasma exchange.

An MS exacerbation is defined as new neurological symptoms lasting >24 hours in the absence of fever or infection. High-dose corticosteroids accelerate recovery but do not alter long-term outcomes. Plasma exchange (plasmapheresis) is an option for severe steroid-refractory relapses. Long-term vitamin D 4000 units daily is recommended.

**Q:** What are the first-line and second-line disease modifying therapies (DMTs) for relapsing MS?

**A:** First-line: teriflunomide, interferon-β (Betaseron®, Avonex®, Rebif®), glatiramer acetate (Copaxone®), dimethyl fumarate (Tecfidera®). Second-line: natalizumab (Tysabri®), fingolimod (Gilenya®), ocrelizumab (Ocrevus®), alemtuzumab (Lemtrada®), cladribine (Mavenclad®), ofatumumab (Kesimpta®).

First-line DMTs reduce relapse rate by ~30%; second-line by 50-90%. Natalizumab carries increased risk of progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML) caused by JC virus reactivation; PML risk also described with fingolimod, dimethyl fumarate, and ocrelizumab. PPMS is treated with ocrelizumab (ORATORIO trial); SPMS with siponimod (Mayzent®).

**Q:** What is PML and which MS medication carries the highest risk?

**A:** Progressive multifocal leukoencephalopathy (PML) is a serious opportunistic infection caused by JC virus reactivation causing demyelination and often fatal or severely disabling. The highest risk is with natalizumab (Tysabri®); also occurs with fingolimod, dimethyl fumarate, and ocrelizumab to a lesser degree.

PML risk with natalizumab depends on JC virus antibody status, prior immunosuppressant use, and duration of therapy. JC antibody-positive patients on natalizumab >2 years have the highest risk. Monitoring JC antibody index helps risk stratify. PML must be distinguished from MS relapse clinically and radiologically.

**Q:** What is Devic's disease (neuromyelitis optica spectrum disorder) and how does it differ from MS?

**A:** NMO/Devic's: severe optic neuritis AND longitudinally extensive transverse myelitis extending >3 vertebral segments, aquaporin-4 (AQP4) antibody positive. Differs from MS in severity, aquaporin-4 antibody positivity, and the extent of spinal cord involvement.

NMO is an MS variant that is aquaporin-4 IgG antibody-mediated targeting the water channel on astrocytes. It causes more severe relapses with less recovery than MS. MRI shows extensive spinal cord lesions (>3 vertebral segments) unlike MS (typically <2 segments). Treatment differs — some MS DMTs can worsen NMO.

**Q:** What are good prognostic indicators in MS?

**A:** Female sex, age <40 at onset, RRMS subtype, presenting with optic neuritis, low MRI lesion burden on initial imaging, low early relapse rate, good recovery between exacerbations.

PPMS carries the worst prognosis with higher disability rates and poor response to therapy. Male sex, older age at onset, progressive subtypes, high initial MRI burden, and incomplete recovery between relapses are poor prognostic indicators. EDSS tracks disability progression (scored 0-10).

**Q:** What symptomatic treatments are used for MS-related spasticity, bladder dysfunction, and fatigue?

**A:** Spasticity: baclofen, tizanidine, dantrolene, benzodiazepines, botulinum toxin. Bladder dysfunction: oxybutynin, mirabegron. Fatigue: amantadine, modafinil, methylphenidate. Pain: TCA, carbamazepine, gabapentin. Depression: psychotherapy, antidepressants, lithium.

Symptomatic management is crucial for quality of life in MS. Baclofen (GABA-B agonist) is first-line for spasticity. Oxybutynin (anticholinergic) treats detrusor overactivity causing urgency/frequency. Fatigue, one of the most disabling MS symptoms, responds to amantadine or stimulants. Sexual dysfunction is treated with PDE5 inhibitors (sildenafil, tadalafil, vardenafil).

**Q:** What is the levodopa/carbidopa (Sinemet®) mechanism and what is the maximum recommended daily dose?

**A:** Levodopa is a dopamine precursor that crosses the blood-brain barrier. Carbidopa is a peripheral DOPA decarboxylase inhibitor that prevents peripheral conversion of levodopa to dopamine, increasing CNS bioavailability and reducing peripheral side effects. Maximum: 200 mg carbidopa and 2000 mg levodopa per day.

Levodopa remains the most effective treatment for Parkinson's disease motor symptoms. Long-term use causes motor fluctuations (wearing-off) and dyskinesias. Contraindicated with MAO inhibitors (within 14 days). Key side effects: nausea, hypotension, hallucinations, dyskinesias.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to triptans (e.g., sumatriptan) for acute migraine?

**A:** Hemiplegic/basilar migraine, ischemic heart disease, cardiovascular disease (CVD), uncontrolled hypertension, use of ergotamine or other 5-HT1 agonist within 24 hours, use of MAO inhibitor in last 14 days, severe hepatic disease.

Triptans are selective 5-HT1B/1D receptor agonists that cause vasoconstriction and inhibit neuropeptide release. They are effective for moderate-to-severe migraine but are contraindicated in vascular disease due to coronary and peripheral vasospasm risk. Serotonin syndrome risk exists with concurrent MAOIs.

**Q:** Which antiepileptic drug is most teratogenic and what are its key indications?

**A:** Valproic acid (valproate) is the most teratogenic antiseizure medication (ASM) with dose-dependent risk of neural tube defects and other congenital anomalies. It is indicated for partial and generalized seizures, and absence seizures.

Valproate is effective for multiple seizure types and was shown superior to topiramate and lamotrigine for generalized epilepsy in the SANAD trial. Despite its teratogenicity, it remains first-line for generalized epilepsy when alternatives are not suitable. Women of childbearing potential should be counselled about risks and offered folate supplementation. Key side effects: hepatic failure, alopecia, N/V, tremor, thrombocytopenia, pancreatitis, hyperammonemic encephalopathy.

**Q:** What are the characteristic side effects of phenytoin (Dilantin®)?

**A:** Nystagmus, gingival hypertrophy, peripheral neuropathy, Stevens-Johnson syndrome/toxic epidermal necrolysis, SLE-type symptoms, blood dyscrasias, hypotension (IV), constipation, sedation, and teratogenicity.

Phenytoin is a sodium channel blocker used for partial seizures, tonic-clonic seizures, and status epilepticus (IV loading dose 10-15 mg/kg). Gingival hypertrophy is a classic side effect unique to phenytoin. It has many drug interactions due to CYP-450 induction. Caution in pregnancy (teratogenic).

**Q:** What seizure type is ethosuximide specifically indicated for?

**A:** Absence seizures (childhood absence epilepsy).

Ethosuximide is a succinimide that blocks T-type calcium channels in thalamic neurons, which generate the 3Hz spike-wave discharges characteristic of absence seizures. It is NOT effective for generalized tonic-clonic or partial seizures. Valproate is an alternative for absence seizures and is preferred if generalized tonic-clonic seizures coexist.

**Q:** What is the mechanism and key side effect profile of carbamazepine (Tegretol®)?

**A:** Mechanism: sodium channel blocker. Indicated for partial seizures with or without secondary generalization and generalized tonic-clonic seizures. Key side effects: drowsiness, dizziness, nausea/vomiting, skin rash, agranulocytosis/aplastic anemia (rare), Stevens-Johnson syndrome. Contraindicated with MAO inhibitors (within 14 days) and history of bone marrow depression.

Carbamazepine is also used for trigeminal neuralgia and bipolar disorder. It is a potent CYP3A4 inducer causing many drug interactions. It can cause hyponatremia (SIADH). Asian patients with HLA-B*1502 allele have dramatically increased risk of Stevens-Johnson syndrome — genetic testing recommended prior to starting.

**Q:** What is the mechanism of pyridostigmine and what is it used for in neurology?

**A:** Pyridostigmine (Mestinon®) is an acetylcholinesterase inhibitor that increases acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction. Used for symptomatic treatment of myasthenia gravis. Dose: 60-1500 mg/day PO in 5-6 divided doses.

In myasthenia gravis, autoantibodies destroy acetylcholine receptors (AChR) at the NMJ. Pyridostigmine prevents ACh breakdown, increasing the probability of receptor activation. Side effects reflect cholinergic excess: N/V, diarrhea, abdominal cramps, increased secretions, miosis, diaphoresis, muscle cramps, fasciculations, and bradycardia. Overdose causes cholinergic crisis.

**Q:** What does the RE-LY trial conclude about dabigatran vs. warfarin for stroke prevention in AFib?

**A:** Dabigatran 110 mg BID was non-inferior to warfarin for stroke/systemic embolism with less major bleeding. Dabigatran 150 mg BID was superior to warfarin in reducing stroke/systemic embolism (1.11% vs. 1.69%/yr) with similar major bleeding rates. Both doses lower ICH rates compared to warfarin.

RE-LY (2009) enrolled 18,113 AFib patients. Dabigatran is a direct thrombin inhibitor requiring no INR monitoring. Lower dose (110 mg) prioritizes bleeding safety; higher dose (150 mg) prioritizes stroke prevention. Contraindicated in CrCl <30 mL/min and mechanical heart valves. Reversal: idarucizumab (Praxbind®).

**Q:** What does the ARISTOTLE trial establish about apixaban vs. warfarin in atrial fibrillation?

**A:** Apixaban was superior to warfarin for preventing stroke or systemic embolism (1.27% vs. 1.60%/yr; P<0.001 for non-inferiority, P=0.01 for superiority) with lower rates of major bleeding (2.13% vs. 3.09%) and death (3.52% vs. 3.94%) in AFib patients.

ARISTOTLE (2011) enrolled 18,201 AFib patients with ≥1 additional stroke risk factor. Apixaban is a Factor Xa inhibitor dosed 2.5 or 5 mg BID. It was the first DOAC to show superiority over warfarin in all three key outcomes (stroke, bleeding, mortality). This makes apixaban a preferred DOAC for many patients with AFib.

**Q:** What does the DAWN trial establish about thrombectomy timing in ischemic stroke?

**A:** Endovascular thrombectomy performed 6-24 hours after stroke onset improved disability outcomes in patients with mismatch between clinical deficit and infarct size. 90-day functional independence: 49% thrombectomy vs. 13% control.

DAWN (2018) enrolled 206 patients selected by clinical-radiological mismatch (large clinical deficit relative to small infarct core). The key criterion is that patients were last known well 6-24 hours earlier. This extended the thrombectomy window significantly. CT perfusion imaging is used to identify eligible patients (penumbra > core).

**Q:** What does the POINT trial establish about dual antiplatelet therapy in high-risk TIA or minor stroke?

**A:** Clopidogrel + ASA (dual antiplatelet) reduces major ischemic events vs. ASA alone (5% vs. 6.5%; HR 0.75) in minor ischemic stroke or high-risk TIA, but increases major hemorrhage risk (0.9% vs. 0.4%) over 3 months.

POINT (2018) supports dual antiplatelet therapy with clopidogrel + ASA for 21 days in high-risk TIA or minor stroke (NIHSS <4), followed by antiplatelet monotherapy. This is consistent with Canadian guidelines recommending 21-day dual antiplatelet therapy. Prolonged dual therapy beyond 21 days increases bleeding without additional benefit.

**Q:** What does the SAMMPRIS trial establish about intracranial stenting vs. medical management?

**A:** Aggressive medical management alone is superior to percutaneous transluminal angioplasty and stenting (PTAS) for 70-99% symptomatic intracranial arterial stenosis. 30-day stroke/death: 14.7% PTAS vs. 5.8% medical management (P=0.002). 1-year primary endpoint: 20.0% PTAS vs. 12.2% medical (P=0.009).

SAMMPRIS (2011) challenged the widespread use of intracranial stenting. The high periprocedural complication rate of PTAS (likely due to difficulty of the procedure and complexity of intracranial vasculature) made medical management the preferred strategy. Aggressive medical management included dual antiplatelet therapy, intensive risk factor control, and lifestyle modification.

**Q:** What does the HOPE trial establish about ACE inhibitors for stroke prevention?

**A:** Ramipril 10 mg/day in high-cardiovascular-risk patients (≥55 years with vascular disease or DM + one other risk factor) reduced stroke risk by 32% (3.4% vs. 4.9%; RR 0.68; P<0.001), MI/stroke/CV mortality by 26%, and all-cause mortality by 16% over 5 years.

HOPE (2000) enrolled 9297 patients and showed ramipril benefits extend beyond blood pressure lowering — the stroke risk reduction was greater than expected from BP reduction alone, suggesting direct vascular protective effects. ACE inhibitors + thiazide diuretic combination is recommended for secondary stroke prevention (PROGRESS trial).

**Q:** What is the SANAD trial conclusion regarding antiepileptic drugs for generalized epilepsy?

**A:** Valproate should remain first-line therapy for generalized and unclassified epilepsies due to superior efficacy and safety compared to topiramate and lamotrigine. Valproate was significantly better than topiramate for time to treatment failure and better than lamotrigine for time to 12-month remission.

SANAD compared valproate, lamotrigine, and topiramate. Despite valproate's superior efficacy, its teratogenicity limits use in women of childbearing potential. In such patients, lamotrigine or levetiracetam may be preferred. The MESS trial showed immediate antiepileptic treatment reduces early seizures but does not affect long-term remission.

**Q:** What are the indications for topiramate in migraine prophylaxis and what are its major side effects?

**A:** Indication: migraine prophylaxis. Contraindicated in nephrolithiasis. Major side effects: sedation, mood disturbance, cognitive dysfunction ('dopamax'), anorexia, nausea, diarrhea, paresthesias, metabolic acidosis, glaucoma, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, toxic epidermal necrolysis.

Topiramate (Topamax®) is also used as an antiseizure medication. Starting dose is 25 mg PO in the evening, titrated to 50 mg BID. Its mechanism includes sodium channel blockade, GABA-A potentiation, and glutamate AMPA/kainate receptor inhibition. The cognitive side effects ('dopamax') are particularly limiting for patients requiring mental clarity.

**Q:** What is the contraindication and key side effect profile of propranolol for migraine prophylaxis?

**A:** Contraindications: uncompensated CHF, severe bradycardia or heart block, severe COPD or asthma. Side effects: fatigue, cognitive dysfunction, disturbed sleep, rashes, dyspepsia, dry eyes, heart failure, bronchospasm, risk of acute tachycardia and HTN on abrupt withdrawal.

Propranolol (Inderal®) is a non-selective beta-blocker used for migraine prophylaxis (80-240 mg/day) and essential tremor. Its migraine mechanism is incompletely understood but may involve beta-adrenergic blockade, serotonergic effects, and inhibition of nitric oxide synthesis. Abrupt discontinuation can trigger rebound HTN and tachycardia.

**Q:** What is the correct dosing of acetylcholinesterase inhibitor donepezil for Alzheimer's disease and what are its contraindications?

**A:** Donepezil (Aricept®): 5 mg PO once daily, may increase to 10 mg PO once daily after 4-6 weeks. Contraindicated in hypersensitivity to donepezil or piperidine derivatives. Side effects: diarrhea, N/V, insomnia, muscle cramps, fatigue, anorexia, HTN, syncope, AV block.

Donepezil is indicated for mild to moderate Alzheimer's disease and dementia with Lewy bodies. It inhibits acetylcholinesterase, increasing acetylcholine at cholinergic synapses in the basal forebrain. CATIE-AD trial showed atypical antipsychotics (olanzapine, quetiapine, risperidone) used for behavioral symptoms in Alzheimer's cause faster cognitive decline vs. placebo.

**Q:** What does the CATIE-AD trial conclude about atypical antipsychotics in Alzheimer's disease?

**A:** Long-term use of atypical antipsychotics (olanzapine, quetiapine, risperidone) for behavioral symptoms and psychosis in Alzheimer's dementia is associated with greater rates of cognitive decline compared to placebo (MMSE decline -0.067/week vs. -0.007/week).

CATIE-AD (2011) enrolled 421 outpatients with Alzheimer's and psychosis/agitation randomized to atypical antipsychotics or placebo. Despite being used commonly for behavioral disturbances, the cognitive harm suggests these agents should be reserved for situations where non-pharmacological approaches fail and risk-benefit is carefully considered. Atypical antipsychotics also carry a black box warning for increased mortality in elderly dementia patients.

**Q:** What are the DOAC options for stroke prevention in atrial fibrillation and which patients should NOT receive DOACs?

**A:** DOACs: dabigatran (110 or 150 mg PO BID), apixaban (2.5 or 5 mg PO BID), rivaroxaban (15 or 20 mg PO once daily), edoxaban (30 or 60 mg once daily). DOACs should NOT be used in: patients with mechanical heart valves OR AF with valvular heart disease (mitral stenosis).

DOACs offer predictable pharmacokinetics, no INR monitoring, fewer drug/food interactions, and generally lower ICH risk compared to warfarin. The RE-LY (dabigatran), ARISTOTLE (apixaban), ROCKET AF (rivaroxaban) and ENGAGE AF (edoxaban) trials all established DOAC non-inferiority or superiority to warfarin. Warfarin (INR 2-3) remains standard for mechanical heart valves.

**Q:** What does the ROCKET AF trial establish about rivaroxaban vs. warfarin in AFib?

**A:** Rivaroxaban (20 mg once daily) was non-inferior to warfarin for prevention of stroke or systemic embolism (1.7% vs. 2.2%/yr), with significantly lower rates of intracranial hemorrhage (0.5% vs. 0.7%) and fatal bleeding (0.2% vs. 0.5%) in non-valvular AFib.

ROCKET AF (2011) enrolled 14,264 patients with non-valvular AFib at increased stroke risk. Rivaroxaban demonstrated non-inferiority (not superiority) to warfarin for stroke prevention. Its major advantage is the once-daily dosing improving compliance. Contraindicated with concomitant anticoagulants, severe hepatic disease, pregnancy, and strong CYP3A4/P-glycoprotein inhibitors.

**Q:** What is the PreCISe trial conclusion about glatiramer acetate in clinically isolated syndrome (CIS)?

**A:** Early treatment with glatiramer acetate (20 mg SC/day) in CIS patients with ≥2 T2 brain lesions reduced the risk of converting to clinically definite MS by 45% (HR 0.55; P=0.0005) and prolonged time to conversion by 115% compared to placebo.

PreCISe (2009) enrolled 481 CIS patients and established early DMT benefit. In CIS, first-line DMTs including glatiramer acetate, interferons, and teriflunomide all have RCT data supporting early treatment. The most common adverse events with glatiramer acetate are injection-site reactions and immediate post-injection reactions (transient chest pain, flushing).

**Q:** What are the systemic hypoperfusion (global cerebral ischemia) watershed areas susceptible to stroke?

**A:** Watershed (border zone) areas between the major cerebral arterial territories — typically between the ACA-MCA territory and MCA-PCA territory on the cortical surface, and between the deep penetrating arteries and cortical branches (internal watershed).

Systemic hypoperfusion occurs when cardiac output is insufficient (cardiac arrest, arrhythmia, MI) causing ischemia in the most distal territory from the major arteries — the watershed zones. Classic presentation: bilateral proximal arm weakness (the 'man-in-a-barrel' syndrome from anterior and posterior watershed infarcts), cortical blindness, and cognitive deficits.

**Q:** A 68-year-old with known atrial fibrillation presents with sudden left-sided weakness, left facial droop, and gaze deviation to the right. NIHSS is 16. NCHCT shows no hemorrhage. What is the most likely diagnosis and next step?

**A:** Right MCA ischemic stroke (cardioembolic from AFib). NIHSS 16 = moderate-to-severe stroke. Urgent CT angiography to evaluate for large vessel occlusion; administer IV tPA within 4.5 h of symptom onset if no contraindications; proceed to mechanical thrombectomy if proximal large vessel occlusion confirmed.

Gaze deviation is TOWARD the side of the lesion (away from weakness) in supratentorial stroke. Left-sided weakness + left facial droop + rightward gaze = right hemisphere involvement = right MCA territory. NIHSS 16 = moderate-to-severe. The clinical scenario calls for CODE STROKE activation: CT head (no hemorrhage), CT angiography, then tPA + thrombectomy as per current standard of care.

**Q:** What is the ECASS3 trial and what does it establish about tPA timing?

**A:** ECASS3 (2008) demonstrated that IV alteplase (0.9 mg/kg) given 3-4.5 hours after acute ischemic stroke onset improved clinical outcomes (favorable outcome 52.4% vs. 45.2%; OR 1.34) compared to placebo, despite higher intracranial hemorrhage rates (27.0% vs. 17.6%). No significant mortality difference.

ECASS3 extended the tPA window from 3 hours (NINDS trial) to 4.5 hours. Key exclusions in the 3-4.5 hour window include: age >80, NIHSS >25, prior stroke + DM, and anticoagulant use. The modest but significant benefit must be weighed against the increased ICH risk. Current Canadian guidelines support tPA within 4.5 hours.

**Q:** What is the target smoking cessation timeline after which stroke risk returns to baseline?

**A:** After smoking cessation, the risk of stroke decreases to baseline within 2-5 years.

Smoking increases stroke risk in a dose-dependent manner (primary prevention). For secondary prevention, a combination of pharmacological therapy (e.g., varenicline, bupropion, NRT) and behavioural therapy should be offered. The 2-5 year timeline to return to baseline stroke risk underscores the importance of counselling stroke patients about smoking cessation.

**Q:** What are the secondary prevention HbA1c and glucose targets in diabetic stroke patients?

**A:** HbA1c <7%, fasting blood glucose 4-7 mmol/L, 2-hour postprandial plasma glucose target 5-10 mmol/L.

Both Type 1 and Type 2 diabetes are independent risk factors for ischemic stroke, particularly lacunar stroke. Glycemic control reduces microvascular complications and contributes to stroke risk reduction. During acute stroke, hyperglycemia should be avoided as it increases infarct size.

## Neurology Movement Disorders

**Q:** What are the four cardinal features of Parkinson's Disease (TRAP)?

**A:** T - Tremor (resting, 4-6 Hz pill-rolling)
R - Rigidity (cogwheel)
A - Akinesia/Bradykinesia (slow, small amplitude decrementing movements)
P - Postural instability (late finding)

These four features define the clinical diagnosis of PD. Bradykinesia is the most essential feature for diagnosis per MDS criteria. Postural instability is a late finding; its early presence should raise suspicion for atypical parkinsonism.

**Q:** What is the pathological hallmark of Parkinson's disease at the cellular and regional level?

**A:** Alpha-synuclein accumulates intracellularly as Lewy bodies and Lewy neurites, causing neuronal loss primarily in the substantia nigra pars compacta. Motor symptoms emerge when ~70% of dopaminergic neurons are lost.

Alpha-synucleinopathy progresses in a stereotyped manner (Braak staging): dorsal motor nucleus of vagus and olfactory bulbs first (constipation, anosmia), then pons (REM sleep behaviour disorder), then midbrain/substantia nigra (motor symptoms), then cortex (cognitive impairment, hallucinations).

**Q:** What is the mechanism by which levodopa/carbidopa (Sinemet) works in Parkinson's disease, and what is its major adverse effect?

**A:** Levodopa is a dopamine precursor that crosses the blood-brain barrier and is converted to dopamine. Carbidopa is a peripheral dopa decarboxylase inhibitor that prevents peripheral metabolism of levodopa, increasing its half-life and CNS bioavailability while reducing side effects. Major adverse effect: dyskinesia.

Carbidopa/benserazide (Prolopa in Canada) prevents levodopa conversion outside the CNS, reducing nausea, vomiting, and cardiovascular side effects. Dyskinesia (involuntary movements) is the most significant long-term complication, along with motor fluctuations ('wearing-off', 'on-off' phenomena).

**Q:** A patient with Parkinson's disease develops random oscillations between normal movement and akinesia throughout the day. What is this called, and how does it differ from 'wearing-off'?

**A:** 'On-off' phenomenon: unpredictable random oscillations between mobile ('on') and akinetic ('off') states, not related to dosing timing. 'Wearing-off' is predictable end-of-dose deterioration where symptoms return before the next levodopa dose is due.

Both are levodopa-related motor fluctuations. 'Wearing-off' is managed by increasing dose frequency or adding adjuncts (dopamine agonists, MAOIs, COMT inhibitors). 'On-off' phenomenon is more difficult to manage and may benefit from continuous dopaminergic stimulation via apomorphine infusion or deep brain stimulation.

**Q:** What are the red flags (atypical features) that suggest a diagnosis other than idiopathic Parkinson's disease?

**A:** - Abrupt onset
- Rapid progression
- Early falls
- Early autonomic dysfunction
- Symmetric symptoms at onset
- Early age of onset (<50 yr)
- Early cognitive impairment
- FMHx of psychiatric disorders and/or dementia
- Recent diagnosis of psychiatric disease
- History of encephalitis
- Unusual toxin exposure
- Extensive travel history

These atypical features suggest diagnoses such as progressive supranuclear palsy (PSP), corticobasal syndrome, multiple system atrophy (MSA), vascular parkinsonism, or drug-induced parkinsonism. Poor or absent response to levodopa also favours atypical parkinsonism.

**Q:** What distinguishes Progressive Supranuclear Palsy (PSP) from Parkinson's disease?

**A:** PSP features: limited vertical gaze (downgaze palsy more specific), early postural instability with frequent falls, wide-based unsteady gait, axial rigidity, akinesia, dysarthria, dysphagia, and little or no response to levodopa. It is a tauopathy.

The downgaze palsy in PSP can be overcome by the oculocephalic reflex (doll's eye maneuver), indicating the palsy is supranuclear. Early falls and the characteristic 'surprised' facial expression (from frontalis overactivity compensating for lid retraction) are clinical pearls. PSP does not respond well to levodopa.

**Q:** What are the distinguishing features of Multiple System Atrophy (MSA)?

**A:** MSA is a synucleinopathy with two subtypes:
- MSA-C: cerebellar predominant
- MSA-P: parkinsonism predominant
Key feature: early autonomic dysfunction (urinary incontinence or orthostatic hypotension) distinguishes it from idiopathic PD.

MSA has poor response to levodopa. Early and prominent autonomic failure (orthostatic hypotension, urinary incontinence, erectile dysfunction) is the hallmark. The combination of cerebellar, parkinsonian, and autonomic features in a patient should prompt consideration of MSA.

**Q:** What genetic mutation causes Huntington's disease and what is the inheritance pattern?

**A:** Autosomal dominant CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the HTT gene on chromosome 4p16.3.
- 27-35 repeats: meiotic instability (premutation)
- 36-39 repeats: reduced penetrance
- ≥40 repeats: full disease penetrance
Genetic anticipation causes repeat expansion with each generation.

The expanded CAG repeats encode a polyglutamine tract in the huntingtin protein, leading to toxic protein accumulation in neurons. Genetic anticipation means offspring may have more repeats (especially with paternal transmission) and earlier onset.

**Q:** Describe the clinical progression of Huntington's disease.

**A:** Insidious onset (mean age 35-44 yr) with clumsiness, fidgetiness, irritability → progresses over 15 years to:
- Major NCD (progressive memory impairment)
- Chorea (begins with eyebrow/forehead movement, shoulder shrugging, parakinesia; progresses to dance-like or ballistic movements; late stage replaced by dystonia and rigidity)
- Mood changes (irritability, depression, anhedonia, impulsivity, violence)
Juvenile-onset (Westphal variant): parkinsonism, dystonia, rigidity, seizures.

Pathology shows global cerebral atrophy particularly affecting the striatum. Loss of striatal neurons increases activity of the direct pathway and decreases indirect pathway activity, causing the characteristic chorea. MRI shows enlarged ventricles and caudate atrophy.

**Q:** What is the treatment for chorea in Huntington's disease?

**A:** Tetrabenazine (vesicular monoamine transporter inhibitor), amantadine, and neuroleptics (antipsychotics). Botulinum toxin for focal dystonia. There is no disease-modifying treatment for HD.

Tetrabenazine depletes presynaptic monoamines (including dopamine) and blocks postsynaptic dopamine receptors, reducing chorea. Psychiatric symptoms are managed with antidepressants and antipsychotics. Genetic counselling is essential given the autosomal dominant inheritance.

**Q:** Define dystonia and list its treatment options.

**A:** Dystonia: co-contraction of agonist and antagonist muscles causing sustained twisting movements that can be tonic (dystonic postures) or phasic (dystonic movements). It is the 3rd most common movement disorder after PD and essential tremor.

Treatment:
- Local: botulinum toxin (first-line for focal dystonia)
- Systemic: anticholinergics (trihexyphenidyl, benztropine), baclofen, benzodiazepines, tetrabenazine, levodopa (for dopa-responsive dystonia)
- Surgical: DBS or denervation

Dystonia is exacerbated by fatigue, stress, and emotion, but relieved by sleep or sensory tricks (e.g., touching the chin for cervical dystonia - 'geste antagoniste'). Younger onset or leg dystonia is more likely to be progressive and generalized.

**Q:** What is the difference between resting tremor, action-postural tremor, and action-intention tremor in terms of characteristics and common causes?

**A:** Resting tremor:
- 3-6 Hz pill-rolling; worse at rest
- DDx: PD, parkinsonism, Wilson's disease, severe essential tremor
- Tx: levodopa/carbidopa, DBS

Action-postural tremor:
- 6-12 Hz fine tremor; worse with sustained posture (outstretched arms)
- DDx: physiologic, essential tremor, hyperthyroidism, drug toxicity, EtOH/sedative withdrawal
- Tx: propranolol, primidone, topiramate

Action-intention tremor:
- <5 Hz coarse; worse during goal-directed movement (finger-to-nose)
- DDx: cerebellar disorders, Wilson's disease, MS, EtOH
- Tx: treat underlying cause

Essential tremor usually does not require treatment. Alcohol dampens essential tremor but potentiates intention tremor during abstinence (delirium tremens). In young patients (<45 yr) with tremor, always check TSH, ceruloplasmin (Wilson's), and CT/MRI.

**Q:** What is asterixis and what conditions cause it?

**A:** Asterixis is a transient loss of muscle tone (negative myoclonus), causing a flapping tremor when the arms are outstretched. It is caused by metabolic encephalopathies including hepatic encephalopathy, uremia, hypercapnia, and drug toxicity.

Asterixis is assessed by asking the patient to hold arms outstretched with wrists extended - brief lapses in posture cause a 'flapping' movement. It reflects metabolic dysfunction rather than a primary movement disorder and should prompt workup for an underlying systemic cause.

**Q:** What is hemiballism and what is its most common cause?

**A:** Hemiballism is large-amplitude, involuntary, flinging movements that are most commonly unilateral. Most commonly caused by a vascular lesion (stroke/infarction) to the subthalamic nucleus contralateral to the affected limb.
