The subthalamic nucleus normally provides excitatory (glutamate) drive to the globus pallidus interna via the indirect pathway. Loss of this drive disinhibits the thalamus and motor cortex, causing ballistic movements. Management includes tetrabenazine or antipsychotics.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for Tourette's Syndrome?

**A:** 1. Presence of both multiple motor AND one or more vocal tics at some point during illness (not necessarily concurrently)
2. Tics have persisted >1 year since first onset (with no tic-free period >3 months)
3. Onset before 18 years of age
4. Not due to substance or another medical condition

Epidemiology: prevalence 3-8 in 1000 school-age children, M:F = 2-4:1. Peak severity ages 10-12, with 50% tic-free by age 18. Commonly associated with OCD and ADHD. Tics can be voluntarily suppressed, worsen with anxiety/excitement, and may be preceded by premonitory urges.

**Q:** Differentiate between simple and complex motor and vocal tics, giving examples of each.

**A:** Motor tics:
- Simple (short, msec): eye blinking, head jerking, shoulder shrugging
- Complex (longer, more purposeful): copropraxia (obscene gestures), echopraxia (imitating gestures), throwing, touching

Vocal tics:
- Simple: blowing, coughing, grunting, throat clearing
- Complex: coprolalia (shouting obscenities), echolalia (repeating others' phrases), palilalia (repeating own phrases)

Complex tics appear more purposeful. Coprolalia (uttering obscenities) is the most recognized but actually occurs in only ~10-15% of Tourette's patients. Tics wax and wane in type and severity.

**Q:** What are the treatment options for debilitating tics in Tourette's syndrome?

**A:** - Alpha-2 adrenergic agonists: guanfacine, clonidine (first-line for mild-moderate)
- Antipsychotics: haloperidol, pimozide (effective but significant side effects)
- Botulinum toxin (for focal tics)
- Topiramate
- Deep Brain Stimulation (DBS) for refractory cases
- Comprehensive Behavioural Intervention for Tics (CBIT) for mild tics
- OCD component: exposure-response prevention CBT

Treatment is indicated only when tics affect quality of life. Mild tics are managed with education, counselling, and CBIT. Antipsychotics are effective but carry risks of tardive dyskinesia with long-term use. Guanfacine and clonidine also help with associated ADHD.

**Q:** What is myoclonus and what are the major categories of symptomatic myoclonus?

**A:** Myoclonus: brief muscle group contraction that is focal, segmental, or generalized. Can be stimulus-sensitive (induced by sudden noise, movement, light, visual threat, or pinprick).

Categories:
1. Physiologic: hiccups, nocturnal myoclonus (sleep starts)
2. Essential: myoclonus-dystonia
3. Epileptic: part of seizure disorder
4. Symptomatic:
   - Degenerative: Wilson's disease, HD, corticobasal degeneration
   - Infectious: CJD, viral encephalitis, AIDS-dementia
   - Metabolic: drug intoxication/withdrawal, hypoglycemia, hyponatremia, hyperglycemic hyperosmolar syndrome, hepatic encephalopathy, uremia, hypoxia
   - Focal brain damage: stroke, head injury, mass

Symptomatic myoclonus suggests an underlying cause requiring workup. Metabolic causes are common and reversible. CJD (prion disease) characteristically presents with rapidly progressive dementia, ataxia, and myoclonus. Palatal tremor can result from lesions to the dentato-rubro-olivary tract.

**Q:** What is the direct vs. indirect pathway of the basal ganglia, and how do they relate to movement disorders?

**A:** Direct pathway (facilitates movement): Cortex → Striatum (inhibitory GABA to GPi) → GPi inhibited → thalamus disinhibited → cortex activated

Indirect pathway (suppresses movement): Cortex → Striatum (GABA to GPe) → GPe (GABA to STN) → STN (glutamate to GPi) → GPi activated → thalamus inhibited → cortex suppressed

In PD: Dopamine (from SNpc) normally facilitates direct and inhibits indirect pathway. Dopamine loss → decreased direct + increased indirect → net cortical inhibition → bradykinesia/rigidity

In HD: Striatal loss disproportionately affects indirect pathway (early) → decreased GPi output → thalamic disinhibition → chorea

Understanding these pathways explains the rationale for PD treatment (dopaminergic agents restore balance) and the pathophysiology of different movement disorders. DBS of the subthalamic nucleus in PD reduces the hyperactive indirect pathway, restoring normal cortical excitation.

**Q:** What protective and risk factors are associated with developing Parkinson's disease?

**A:** Risk factors: family history, male sex, head injury, rural living, exposure to certain neurotoxins (pesticides, MPTP)

Protective factors: coffee drinking, cigarette smoking, estrogen replacement in postmenopausal women

Genetics: 10% familial (autosomal dominant: alpha-synuclein, LRRK2 mutations; autosomal recessive: parkin, PINK1, DJ-1 - juvenile onset)

PD is the second most common neurodegenerative disorder after AD, with prevalence 0.3% in industrialized countries rising with age (mean onset 60 yr). MPTP is a neurotoxin that selectively destroys dopaminergic neurons in the substantia nigra and has been used to create animal models of PD.

## Neurology Cerebellar

**Q:** What are the clinical features of cerebellar disorders, organized by region of involvement?

**A:** Lateral zone (cerebellar hemisphere) → ipsilateral limb ataxia:
- Dysmetria (finger-to-nose, heel-to-shin)
- Intention tremor
- Dysdiadochokinesia
- Hypotonia/pendular reflexes
- Holmes rebound phenomenon

Cerebellar vermis → truncal ataxia:
- Wide-based gait
- Inability to tandem walk
- Truncal titubation

Other: gaze-evoked nystagmus, scanning speech (explosive with pauses)

Cerebellar signs are IPSILATERAL to the lesion (unlike UMN lesions). Scanning speech (also called dysarthria) has characteristic explosive quality with accentuated syllables. Dysdiadochokinesia is tested with rapid alternating movements (pronation/supination).

**Q:** What is Friedreich's ataxia? Describe its key clinical features and prognosis.

**A:** Friedreich's ataxia is the most common hereditary ataxia (prevalence 2/100,000), autosomal recessive, typically onset 8-15 years.

Features:
- Gait and limb ataxia
- Weakness
- Areflexia
- Extensor plantar response (Babinski sign)
- Impaired proprioception and vibration
- Dysarthria, nystagmus
- Cardiomyopathy (cause of death)
- Kyphoscoliosis

Prognosis: Death in 10-20 years from cardiomyopathy or kyphoscoliotic pulmonary restriction.

Friedreich's ataxia is caused by GAA trinucleotide repeat expansion in the frataxin gene. The combination of cerebellar signs with absent deep tendon reflexes and extensor plantars (mixed UMN and LMN) is characteristic. Cardiomyopathy management is critical for survival.

**Q:** What causes Wernicke's encephalopathy and what are the classic clinical triad features?

**A:** Caused by thiamine (vitamin B1) deficiency.

Classic triad:
1. Ophthalmoplegia (often horizontal nystagmus, lateral rectus palsy)
2. Ataxia (cerebellar-type gait ataxia)
3. Confusion/encephalopathy

Etiologies: alcohol use disorder (most common), GI malabsorption, gastric bypass surgery, malnutrition, AIDS, hemodialysis, malignancy.

Korsakoff's psychosis is the chronic sequela with amnesia (anterograde > retrograde), confabulation, and relatively preserved other cognitive functions. Note: alcohol can also cause cerebellar ataxia through direct cerebellar atrophy or alcohol polyneuropathy, separate from thiamine deficiency.

## Neurology Motor Neuron

**Q:** What is ALS (Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis)? Describe its UMN and LMN clinical features.

**A:** ALS (Lou Gehrig's disease): progressive neurodegenerative disease affecting both UMN and LMN, ultimately fatal.

UMN symptoms:
- Poor dexterity, spasticity (spastic gait)
- Hyperreflexia
- Bulbar: dysarthria (spastic, strained slow speech), dysphagia, pseudobulbar affect, laryngospasm

LMN symptoms:
- Weakness (hands, arms, tongue, pharynx, diaphragm)
- Muscle atrophy
- Fasciculations
- Muscle cramps
- Bulbar: dysarthria (flaccid, slurred, nasal quality), dysphagia

Cognitive/behavioural dysfunction and FTD in ~15%

The combination of UMN and LMN signs in the same patient is the diagnostic hallmark of ALS. Sensory symptoms are uncommon (20-30%). Most common genetic cause is C9orf72 mutation (familial ALS accounts for 5-10%). EMG shows denervation (fibrillations, positive sharp waves) and reinnervation (increased amplitude/duration motor units).

**Q:** What are the two disease-modifying treatments for ALS and their mechanisms?

**A:** 1. Riluzole: slows disease progression and prolongs survival; mechanism: reduces glutamate-mediated excitotoxicity
2. Edaravone: shown to slow functional decline by 33% in early-stage ALS; mechanism: free radical scavenger

Non-pharmacologic: BiPAP (ventilatory support improves survival), PEG tube for nutritional support.

Riluzole is the original disease-modifying therapy shown in trials to extend survival. Edaravone benefit was shown in a 2017 RCT (Lancet Neurology) in carefully selected early-stage patients meeting stringent inclusion criteria. BiPAP is also shown to improve survival. Median survival is 3 years, with death typically from respiratory failure.

**Q:** Differentiate Progressive Muscular Atrophy, Primary Lateral Sclerosis, and Progressive Bulbar Palsy.

**A:** Progressive Muscular Atrophy (progressive bulbar palsy):
- Only LMN symptoms, asymmetric weakness
- Isolated LMN version of ALS; 5-10% of ALS centre patients

Primary Lateral Sclerosis:
- Only UMN symptoms
- Later onset, not fatal, variable disability
- Isolated UMN version of ALS; 5-10% of ALS centre patients

Progressive Bulbar Palsy:
- Both UMN and LMN disorder of cranial muscles
- Prominent bulbar symptoms (dysarthria, dysphagia)

These are considered variants on the ALS spectrum. Prognosis is better for PMA and PLS than classic ALS. Progressive bulbar palsy has prominent cranial nerve involvement and significant impact on communication and swallowing.

## Neurology Peripheral Neuropathy

**Q:** What is carpal tunnel syndrome? List its symptoms, signs, risk factors, and diagnostic tests.

**A:** Compression of the median nerve in the carpal tunnel at the wrist.

Symptoms: paresthesias in thumb, index, long finger, and lateral half of ring finger; decreased grip strength; nocturnal worsening.

Signs:
- Tinel's sign (tap over carpal tunnel → reproduces symptoms)
- Phalen's test (wrist flexion for 30-60s → reproduces symptoms)
- Thenar atrophy (late)

Risk factors: pregnancy, DM, rheumatoid arthritis, acromegaly, hypothyroidism

Diagnosis: clinical, confirmed by EMG/NCS

Carpal tunnel syndrome is the most common mononeuropathy. NCS shows decreased amplitude (axonal) or decreased conduction velocity (demyelinating). The median nerve supplies the lateral 3.5 fingers (LOAF muscles: lumbricales 1&2, opponens pollicis, abductor pollicis brevis, flexor pollicis brevis). Treatment ranges from splinting and corticosteroids to surgical decompression.

**Q:** What is the approach to classifying peripheral neuropathies?

**A:** 1. Differentiate: motor vs. sensory vs. autonomic vs. mixed
2. Pattern of deficit: symmetry; focal vs. diffuse; upper vs. lower limb; CN involvement
3. Temporal pattern: acute vs. chronic; relapsing/remitting vs. progressive
4. History: PMHx, FMHx, exposures (toxins, insects, travel), systemic symptoms
5. Detailed peripheral neuro exam: LMN findings, differentiate root vs. peripheral nerve

Axonal neuropathies: decreased amplitude on NCS
Demyelinating neuropathies: decreased conduction velocity on NCS

Common patterns: radiculopathy (single dermatomal/myotomal deficit), plexopathy (multiple root territories), mononeuropathy (single nerve), mononeuropathy multiplex (multiple individual nerves, asymmetric, painful - think vasculitis), polyneuropathy (symmetric distal, stocking-glove).

**Q:** What is Erb's palsy and Klumpke's palsy? Which trunk of the brachial plexus is involved in each?

**A:** Erb's palsy: Upper trunk injury (C5, C6)
- Arm hangs at side, internally rotated, extended at elbow (waiter's tip position)
- Loss of shoulder abduction, external rotation, elbow flexion, supination

Klumpke's palsy: Lower trunk injury (C8, T1)
- Loss of intrinsic hand muscles, finger flexors
- Claw hand deformity
- May have ipsilateral Horner syndrome (T1)

Causes: birth trauma (obstetric), idiopathic neuritis, tumour infiltration, thoracic outlet syndrome

Erb's palsy from C5/C6 affects deltoid, biceps, brachioradialis. Klumpke's from C8/T1 affects intrinsic hand muscles. Obstetric brachial plexus injuries occur from excessive lateral head traction during delivery. Horner syndrome in Klumpke's indicates involvement of T1 sympathetic fibres.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of symmetrical polyneuropathy and what are the initial screening investigations?

**A:** Most common cause: Diabetes mellitus (ischemic/axonal mechanism, chronic course, sensory-autonomic pattern).

Screening lab tests:
- Blood glucose / HbA1c / 2-hour OGTT
- Serum vitamin B12
- Serum protein electrophoresis (SPEP)
- TSH
- CBC, renal function (Cr, BUN, electrolytes)
- Genetic testing for cryptogenic cases with hereditary phenotype

Diabetic polyneuropathy is length-dependent (stocking-glove distribution), primarily sensory then autonomic, then motor. The pattern is symmetric, distal-predominant. Vitamin B12 deficiency and hypothyroidism are important treatable causes. CIDP is important immune-mediated cause (demyelinating, increased CSF protein).

**Q:** What is CIDP (Chronic Inflammatory Demyelinating Polyneuropathy)? How does it differ from GBS?

**A:** CIDP: Chronic relapsing sensorimotor polyneuropathy or polyradiculopathy with:
- Increased protein in CSF (without pleocytosis)
- Demyelination on EMG/NCS
- Fluctuating/relapsing course

Differences from GBS:
- CIDP: chronic/relapsing course; GBS: acute onset (evolves over <4 weeks)
- CIDP requires treatment to prevent relapse; GBS is monophasic

Treatment of CIDP: First-line prednisone; alternatives: plasmapheresis, IVIG, azathioprine

Both are immune-mediated demyelinating neuropathies with albuminocytologic dissociation on CSF. GBS is acute and monophasic; CIDP is chronic and relapsing. In CIDP, unlike GBS, long-term immunosuppression is usually needed.

**Q:** What is Guillain-Barré Syndrome (GBS)? Describe its presentation, investigations, and treatment.

**A:** Acute (<4 weeks) demyelinating inflammatory polyradiculoneuropathy, often post-infectious.

Presentation:
- Sensory: distal symmetric paresthesias, loss of proprioception/vibration, neuropathic pain
- Motor: weakness starting distally in legs, ascending proximally, areflexia
- Autonomic: BP dysregulation, arrhythmias, bladder dysfunction
- Respiratory failure (neurological emergency)

Investigations:
- CSF: albuminocytologic dissociation (high protein, normal WBC)
- EMG/NCS: conduction block, motor>sensory slowing, decreased F-wave, sural sparing

Treatment: IVIG or plasmapheresis (equal efficacy), pain management, monitor vital capacity

Prognosis: Peak symptoms 2-3 weeks; 5% mortality; 15% permanent deficits

GBS is a neurological emergency due to risk of respiratory failure (monitor FVC). IVIG and plasmapheresis lead to faster recovery but do not change mortality or relapse rate. Preceding infections include Campylobacter jejuni (associated with axonal variant), CMV, EBV, and others.

**Q:** What is the Miller-Fisher variant of GBS and what is its classic triad?

**A:** Miller-Fisher variant of GBS:
Classic triad: Ophthalmoplegia + Ataxia + Areflexia

Associated with anti-GQ1b antibodies. Unlike classic GBS, weakness is predominantly cranial/ocular rather than ascending limb weakness.

Anti-GQ1b antibodies are present in >90% of Miller-Fisher cases and can help confirm diagnosis. The condition is generally self-limiting with good prognosis. The triad is important to recognize as it can be confused with posterior fossa lesions or botulism.

**Q:** What is mononeuropathy multiplex and what is the most important diagnostic consideration?

**A:** Mononeuropathy multiplex: subacute involvement of multiple individual peripheral nerves in an asymmetric, non-length-dependent manner; often painful.

Most important diagnostic consideration: vasculitis or collagen vascular disease (SLE, RA, PAN) causing nerve infarction.

Also consider: MMN (multifocal motor neuropathy), MADSAM, multiple compressive neuropathies, DM, sarcoidosis

The asymmetric, non-length-dependent, painful involvement of multiple individual nerves (as opposed to the symmetric distal pattern of polyneuropathy) is the hallmark. Vasculitis should always be ruled out as it is treatable. Nerve biopsy showing necrotizing arteritis confirms vasculitic neuropathy.

**Q:** What common peripheral nerve entrapment neuropathies should be known, and where does each nerve get compressed?

**A:** - Median nerve: carpal tunnel (at wrist) - most common; also at pronator teres (between heads)
- Ulnar nerve: cubital tunnel syndrome (at elbow)
- Radial nerve: spiral groove of humerus (Saturday night palsy)
- Common peroneal nerve: neck of fibula (from crossing legs, surgical positioning)
- Posterior tibial nerve: tarsal tunnel
- Obturator nerve: childbirth

Radial nerve palsy at the spiral groove causes wrist drop (loss of wrist/finger extension). Ulnar nerve palsy causes weakness of intrinsic hand muscles and clawing of 4th/5th fingers. Common peroneal palsy causes foot drop (loss of dorsiflexion and eversion). Site of compression determines which muscles are affected.

## Neurology Neuromuscular

**Q:** Compare Myasthenia Gravis (MG), Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome (LEMS), and Botulism at the neuromuscular junction.

**A:**                     MG          LEMS        Botulism
Ocular/Bulbar:      +++         +           ++ (early)
Limb Weakness:      +           +++         +++
Fatigability:       +++         ++          ++
Post-exercise:      worse       better      better
Reflexes:           normal      decreased   decreased
Anticholinergic Sx: absent      present     present
EMG:                Decremental Incremental Incremental (rapid)
Associated:         Thymoma     Small cell  GI symptoms
                                carcinoma

Key distinguishing features: MG worsens with exercise (decremental EMG response); LEMS and botulism improve briefly with exercise (incremental EMG response). LEMS is paraneoplastic (small cell lung cancer). Botulism presents with GI symptoms first. MG is associated with thymoma (15%) and thymic hyperplasia (85%).

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of Myasthenia Gravis and what are the key investigations?

**A:** Pathophysiology: Autoimmune; antibodies against acetylcholine receptors (AChR) at the NMJ (most common) or muscle-specific kinase (MuSK) antibodies → reduced functional AChRs → fatigable weakness.

Investigations:
1. AChR antibody assay (50-90% sensitivity)
2. MuSK antibody (if seronegative for AChR)
3. Repetitive nerve stimulation: >10% decremental response
4. Single fibre EMG: increased jitter (80-100% sensitivity, most sensitive)
5. Spirometry/FVC: monitor respiratory adequacy
6. CT/MRI chest: screen for thymoma/thymic hyperplasia
7. Edrophonium (Tensilon) test: historical, no longer performed

MG has bimodal age of onset: women in 20s and men in 60s. 15% have thymoma, 85% thymic hyperplasia. Symptoms are worsened by infection, pregnancy, menses, and various drugs. Respiratory failure (myasthenic crisis) is a medical emergency requiring ICU management.
